  big seed batch — DuckDB itself tells us the fix: SET 
  arrow_large_buffer_size=true on the source connection so it emits large_string
   buffers. Let me find where we set up the source connection.

⏺ Cross-thread query_progress() works: 0–100 float while the query runs, needs
  enable_progress_bar=true + enable_progress_bar_print=false. Now checking
  whether pyducklake exposes the underlying connection.

  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Yes — and cleanly. DuckDB tracks per-query progress internally, and
  duckdb-python 1.5.2 exposes conn.query_progress() as a cross-thread call.
  Verified locally: polling from a second thread while a query streams returns a
   0–100 float (5.7 → 9.8 → ... → 100.0), -1.0 when nothing's running.
  pyducklake exposes the connection publicly (table.catalog.connection).

  The wiring would be:

  1. Connection defaults (source.py): add enable_progress_bar: "true" +
  enable_progress_bar_print: "false" — progress is only computed when the
  progress bar is enabled; print-off keeps it silent. Negligible overhead.
  2. Heartbeat (_start_progress_heartbeat): accept the source connection; each
  tick call query_progress() and, when ≥ 0, append ~N% scanned, est. Xm 
  remaining (ETA = elapsed × (100−pct)/pct). Covers exactly the blind spot — the
   7-minute "pre-execution" phase is the query executing before the first batch
  surfaces, and progress ticks through it.
  3. Call site (_seed_new_destinations): pass src_table.catalog.connection.

  Caveats:
  - The percentage is DuckDB's input-scanned estimate (cardinality-based), so
  the ETA is honest-ish, not exact — fine for "minutes vs hours".
  - query_progress() reads whatever query is live on that connection; during
  seed that's unambiguous since the poll loop hasn't started.

  Want me to build it?

✻ Cooked for 1m 39s

❯ What would happen if we were seeding multiple destinations concurrently?      

⏺ Today it can't happen — _seed_new_destinations seeds sequentially in a loop on
   the main thread, so there's exactly one scan live on the source connection
  and query_progress() is unambiguous.

  If we ever parallelized seeding, the progress poll is the least of the
  problems: DuckDB invalidates an open streaming result when a new query starts
  on the same connection, so concurrent seeds would clobber each other's batch
  readers before progress reporting ever got confused. Concurrent seeding would
  force per-seed connections (e.g. conn.cursor() or separate catalogs) — and
  since query_progress() is per-connection, each heartbeat would then poll its
  own connection and stay accurate for free.

  So: the design holds as long as "one scan per connection" holds, which DuckDB
  already forces on us. Worth a one-line comment at the call site noting that
  invariant.

      182 +def _fmt_duration(seconds: float) -> str:                     
      183 +    if seconds < 60:

      185 +    minutes = seconds / 60

          +r:

          +n unavailable.

⏺ Prototype done on jakob/seed-scan-progress, all green (364 unit + 53
  integration). Live run output:

  Seed scan for destination demo: 3000000 rows in 3 batches, 3s elapsed (1191777
   rows/s) (~19% scanned, est. 11s remaining)
  Seed scan for destination demo: ... (~38% scanned, est. 9s remaining)
  Seed scan for destination demo: ... (~100% scanned, est. 0s remaining)

  What it does:
  - source.py: enable_progress_bar=true + enable_progress_bar_print=false added
  to connection defaults (progress is only computed when enabled; print stays
  off).
  - main.py: _scan_progress_suffix polls query_progress() from the heartbeat
  thread; appends ~N% scanned, est. M remaining to every tick — including the
  previously-blind "DuckDB pre-execution" phase. Errors and -1/0 readings
  degrade to the old format. Call site comments the one-scan-per-connection
  invariant.

  Two honest caveats: ETA is linear extrapolation off DuckDB's input-scanned
  estimate (minutes-vs-hours accuracy, not a stopwatch), and it covers the scan
  only — the destination write time on top isn't in the estimate.

❯ Let's get a lead QE engineer to review.                                       

⏺ Agent(QE lead review of progress reporting)
  ⎿  Done (21 tool uses · 49.5k tokens · 5m 9s)
  (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ QE review is back — SHIP, conditional on two MEDIUMs:

  Severity: MEDIUM
  Finding: No heartbeat-level test that the suffix lands in actual log lines —
    all three format strings gained a %s, and a mismatched-arg typo wouldn't
    crash (logging swallows it), just silently kill every heartbeat line. The
    existing fake-heartbeat harness should be extended.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Severity: MEDIUM
  Finding: Sub-1% progress renders garbage: pct=0.3 → "~0% scanned, est. 46h
    remaining" — exactly the regime the first ticks hit on the 1.10 PiB source.
    Suppress below ~1%.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Severity: LOW
  Finding: Bare except swallows API drift (e.g. duckdb renames query_progress)
    forever, silently — log once on first failure.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Severity: LOW
  Finding: Nothing asserts the seed path actually passes progress_conn;
    regression to None passes the whole suite.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Severity: LOW
  Finding: Settings could be scoped to the source catalog only (destinations/CDC

    reads don't need it). Mitigated: progress tracking only activates after 2s
    (progress_bar_time default), so hot paths pay ~nothing. Not blocking.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Severity: NIT
  Finding: _fmt_duration boundary artifacts (59.5m → "60m"); progress_conn
    untyped; docs not stale; a seed-progress gauge metric would be the natural
    follow-up but out of scope.

  Thread-safety got a clean bill: GetQueryProgress is the lock-free atomic read
  built for exactly this (it's how Jupyter's progress bar works); worst case is
  a stale tick, not corruption.

  Want me to fix the two MEDIUMs + the two cheap LOWs (warn-once, wiring
  assertion)?

⏺ Bash(uv run --project /Users/jakob/src/viaduck python <<'EOF'
      import duckdb, tempfile…)
  ⎿  before: data_file rows=5 live=5, snapshots max=5 n=6
     after:  data_file rows=1 live=1, snapshots max=6 n=7
     data intact: (0, 499999, 500000)
     … +2 lines (ctrl+o to expand)
⏺ Tested it — your assumption is right, with a useful nuance:

  before: 5 live data files, snapshots max=5
  CALL ducklake_merge_adjacent_files(...)
  after:  1 live data file,  snapshots max=6   ← new snapshot
  CDC rows over full history: 500,000 (= exactly the original inserts)
  rowids stable, time travel to pre-merge versions still works

  (First attempt looked like "no new snapshot" — but the data had been inlined
  and the merge was a no-op. A real merge does commit a snapshot.)

  Implications for viaduck in prod:

  - Up to ~4 new snapshots/hour from compaction (15-min schedule, only when
  something merges). Each poll cycle sees the snapshot advance, reads CDC over
  the range, gets zero rows — the compaction snapshot contributes nothing to
  table_changes/table_insertions. Cursors advance through it cheaply.
  - Rowids are preserved across merges, so Phase 2 pairing is unaffected.
  - No treadmill: cursor state lives in PG now, so compaction snapshots don't
  trigger writes that trigger reads.
  - The one real hazard is not in compact-all-tiers (it's merge-only):
  ducklake_expire_snapshots lives elsewhere in millpond's maintenance tooling.
  If snapshot expiry ever runs against this lake, a viaduck cursor older than
  the expiry horizon can't replay — CDC reads from an expired snapshot fail. The
   cursor-vs-expiry-horizon relationship is worth a check before anyone
  schedules expiry in prod.

⏺ The failure path is worse than one stale destination: a below-horizon cursor
  raises in _poll_cycle, which is except Exception: break — the whole pod exits 
  and crash-loops, stalling every destination, not just the stale one
  (viaduck/main.py:643-647).

  Full picture from the experiments + code:

  The invariant

  table_changes(start, end) only needs the start anchor to exist — gaps inside
  the range still return full-fidelity changes (verified: expired a middle
  snapshot, deletes from it still came through). Viaduck reads from cursor+1,
  so:

  ▎ Every viaduck cursor must be ≥ the highest snapshot id you expire.

  Prod reality check

  ┌────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │   Factor   │                           State                            │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ team-2     │ Seeded today at snap ~13,634,405; tracks head with         │
  │ cursor     │ seconds–minutes of lag while healthy                       │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Expiry     │ millpond's expire = older_than => now() - N days —         │
  │ tool       │ strictly prefix expiry                                     │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Risk       │ Only if viaduck is down longer than the retention window   │
  │ window     │ when expiry runs                                           │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Failure    │ Loud (No snapshot found at version N), pod crash-loops,    │
  │ mode       │ all destinations stall                                     │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Recovery   │ Reset that destination's cursor to 0 in PG → seed-REPLACE  │
  │            │ auto-truncates and re-seeds (a full source scan)           │
  └────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Pre-expiry check (run on megaduck RDS):

  -- highest snapshot that would be expired
  SELECT max(snapshot_id) FROM ducklake_snapshot
  WHERE snapshot_time < now() - INTERVAL 'N days';

  -- viaduck cursors (state lives in the viaduck schema, same RDS)
  SELECT destination_id, last_snapshot_id FROM viaduck.viaduck_state;

  Safe iff every last_snapshot_id ≥ the first query's result. With viaduck
  healthy and any retention ≥ hours, this passes trivially today.

  One structural option worth considering: teach millpond's expire to clamp
  older_than at min(viaduck cursors) — turns the operational checklist into a
  guardrail, since the state table is in the same database it's already
  connected to.

✻ Cogitated for 1m 57s

❯ This seems odd.  The CDC approach can only reliably work against whatever the 
   compaction has re-written history to be.  If it's compacted, say, snapshots  
  0-999, then by definition the history begins at snapshot 1000, which should   
  have a complete view of the world from then.  You're saying that if somehow   
  we were CDCing and managed to fall behind that minimum snapshot, we'd be      
  hosed.  But at that point, we've effectively escaped the ducklake's view of   
  history.                                                                      

⏺ Your model is right, with one correction: it's not compaction that rewrites
  history — merge keeps every snapshot's view intact (we verified time travel
  and full-fidelity CDC across merged files). Expiry is the history truncation.
  The two are independent operations; compact-all-tiers never truncates.

  With that substitution, your framing is exactly correct, and it's not odd —
  it's the same contract as Kafka topic retention:

  - Snapshot 1000 after expiring 0–999 = a complete materialized view (all live
  data files at 1000 survive expiry by definition). History before it is gone;
  the world starts there.
  - A CDC consumer whose cursor fell below the horizon is precisely a Kafka
  consumer hitting OffsetOutOfRange: the deltas it missed are unrecoverable as 
  deltas — specifically the deletes. You can see the world-as-of-now, but you
  can't know which of your previously-delivered rows died during the gap.
  - The only sound recovery is re-bootstrapping from the complete view — which
  is exactly what viaduck's cursor-0 → seed-REPLACE path does: truncate the
  destination partition, scan current state, rebuild. That's not a workaround;
  it's the principled auto.offset.reset + re-snapshot.

  Could it be cheaper than a full re-seed? Not really. An incremental repair
  would need a full source↔destination diff (anti-join both ways) to find the
  missed deletes — same order of work as the seed scan, with more machinery.

  So the operational posture is the Kafka one: retention window ≫ worst-case
  consumer downtime, loud failure if violated, re-bootstrap as the documented
  recovery. The only piece I'd add is making the failure mode less blunt than
  pod-wide crash-loop — one stale destination shouldn't stall the other
  nineteen. That's the halted-destination/circuit-breaker follow-up already on
  the list.

✻ Sautéed for 30s

❯ So, for prod, for example.  Data has never been expired, but it's also append 
   only.                                                                        

⏺ Right — and those two facts change the calculus in opposite directions:

  Append-only makes falling behind benign(ish). The missed deltas can only be
  inserts, and viaduck's recovery (cursor 0 → seed-REPLACE) reconstructs current
   state exactly — no deletes means no phantoms, nothing semantically
  unrecoverable. Worst case in prod is literally: team-2 re-seeds, one big scan
  (the one we just instrumented with progress/ETA). The Kafka analogy degrades
  gracefully: missing inserts you'll re-read in full is annoying, not lossy.

  Never-expired is why you're about to care. Two things connect here:

  1. Today there is no horizon — 13.6M snapshots back to zero, every cursor
  position is replayable. The contract only begins when the first expiry runs.
  2. The compaction we just enabled in prod (#12081) needs expiry to pay off.
  Merge rewrites the 12.5M small files into big ones, but the pre-merge files
  stay on S3 as long as any old snapshot references them — they only become
  deletable once those snapshots expire (ducklake_files_scheduled_for_deletion →
   cleanup_old_files). Without expiry, compaction improves scan performance but
  storage roughly doubles.

  So the prod sequence is: compact (running every 15 min once #12081 merges) →
  expire older_than some retention → cleanup to reap S3. The pre-expiry cursor
  check applies, but given append-only + healthy viaduck at head-of-log, any
  retention measured in days passes trivially, and the worst-case blast radius
  of getting it wrong is one re-seed plus the crash-loop bluntness (the
  circuit-breaker follow-up).

  One choice worth making deliberately: the retention window also caps how far
  back anyone can time-travel or bootstrap a new CDC consumer from a known
  snapshot — once team-3 onboarding means seeding from current state plus
  CDC-from-now, that's fine; but if anything else relies on megaduck history
  (millpond replays?), that's the constituency to check before picking N days.

✻ Churned for 35s

❯ Yeah, this is all worth documenting, but also inherent in a data lake with    
  snapshots/expiry/etc.  One could be really silly here and put the DuckLake    
  CDC operations onto Kafka and be able to replay the whole ducklake that       
  way...                                                                        

⏺ That "silly" idea is just Kleppmann's turning-the-database-inside-out thesis
  wearing a duck costume — the changelog as source of truth, the lake as a
  materialized view. And the punchline is that the authority question eats you
  either way:

  - If Kafka is authoritative, DuckLake is now a downstream view and you've
  rebuilt the architecture megaduck already sits in — PostHog events already
  flow through Kafka upstream; megaduck is the materialization of that log. The
  replayable history you'd be constructing already exists one hop earlier.
  - If DuckLake stays authoritative, the Kafka copy is a second log with its own
   retention problem — and infinite retention at 1.10 PiB means tiered storage
  to S3, at which point you've reimplemented the data lake inside the message
  broker. Lakes all the way down.

  The honest version of the idea is the one you'd actually get for free: a
  compacted topic keyed by (table, key) fed by viaduck's tombstone-emitting CDC
  stream is exactly Kafka log compaction — latest-state bootstrap plus bounded
  replay — which is the same contract DuckLake's snapshot-plus-expiry already
  gives you, with the horizon as the compaction point. Same physics, different
  logo.

  So: inherent indeed. The retention window is the system's memory; every
  architecture just picks where the amnesia lives.

✻ Brewed for 31s

※ recap: Today's prod hardening shipped: byte formatting, large Arrow buffers, seed-scan progress (PRs 25-27), and charts PR 12081 enabling prod compaction. We just settled snapshot-expiry safety for viaduck cursors. Next: document expiry/retention guidance, or merge pending PRs.

❯ Yeah, I'm not saying we should do that now.  It would be a way to build       
  something like ViaDuck that can do table replication, or higher-volume        
  fan-out without stressing Ducklake itself.                                    

⏺ That version is sound — and it dodges the authority trap, because the topic is
   a distribution buffer, not a second source of truth. The lake stays
  authoritative; Kafka retention only needs to cover consumer downtime (days,
  not forever); bootstrap remains seed-from-snapshot, since the horizon view is
  complete. Amnesia stays where it already lives.

  The structural win is concrete for the current design: today every destination
   costs CDC reads against megaduck (catalog queries on RDS + S3 reads), so
  fan-out scales the load on the thing you least want to stress — that's the
  flat ~42 dests/s wall in the fanout bench. With one reader publishing each
  snapshot range to a topic partitioned by routing key, DuckLake sees a single
  consumer regardless of destination count, per-key ordering survives
  partitioning, cursors become consumer-group offsets (which Kafka manages
  better than our PG table), and the delivery semantics don't change — same
  at-least-once + idempotent apply at the sinks, same tombstones (which we
  conveniently already emit).

  At that point the shape has a name: it's Debezium's architecture with DuckLake
   as the upstream — seed-REPLACE is the initial snapshot phase, the topic is
  the change stream, destinations are Connect-style sinks. Which is reassuring
  rather than damning: the migration path from viaduck-as-is is mostly moving
  the routing/fan-out half behind a topic and keeping the read half intact.
✻ Crunched for 44s

❯ Yeah.  I kept everything in DuckLake partly as an experiment, partly out of   
  respect for how good of a CDC mechanism it has, partly to avoid adding more   
  dependency on Kafka and partly because falling back to Kafka as a default is  
  a bit lazy at this point.  This would also play nicely into a more direct     
  copy of TableFlow...                                                          

⏺ TableFlow inverted, yes — Confluent goes topic → table ("your streams are
  tables"); this goes table → topic ("your lake has a changelog"). Run both
  directions and you've operationalized stream-table duality over the lake: a
  duckling customer chooses whether to mount their slice as a table or subscribe
   to it as a stream, and it's the same replication machinery underneath. That's
   a product surface, not just plumbing — "consume your warehouse as a change
  stream" is a thing people pay Confluent for today.

  And the DuckLake-native detour wasn't a sunk cost on that path. The discipline
   of not defaulting to Kafka is what forced the design work that ports
  directly: the tombstone-emitting CDC semantics, seed-REPLACE as the bootstrap
  contract, the TLC-verified buffer/flush model — those are
  transport-independent. A Kafka distribution layer would slot in as a transport
   swap, not a redesign. The respect for DuckLake's CDC is also empirically
  earned at this point — full-fidelity deltas with rowid anchoring that survive
  compaction merges and mid-range expiry is more than most lakehouse formats
  give you.

  Saving the rationale to memory — it's exactly the kind of "why" that won't be
  derivable from the code later.

⏺ Saved. The rationale, the Kafka-as-distribution-layer sketch, and the expiry

⏺ Knobs exist on the tool but nothing threads them through: justfile recipes
  pass no args, and the chart just runs just compact-all-tiers. Diagnosis and
  options:

  What happened

  - The 4GB default is a deliberate floor from the c8 incident (merge phase
  overshoots DuckDB's memory accounting; 12 threads/20GB got the pod OOMKilled,
  2/4GB survived).
  - This OOM is a different phase: Failed to get compaction file list — DuckLake
   enumerating candidates out of a 12.5M-row ducklake_data_file table. That
  listing simply no longer fits in 4GB at prod scale. It's tracked allocation
  (clean DuckDB exception, not a cgroup kill), so raising memory_limit directly
  addresses it.
  - Budget math: pod has 24Gi. With threads=2 the c8 overshoot stays small, so
  something like memory_limit=16GB leaves ~8Gi of unaccounted-allocation
  headroom before the cgroup kills us.

  Second problem hiding behind the first: tier-1 wants to merge 614k files
  (242GB) in a single merge_adjacent_files call — one giant transaction, ~47k
  output files at 5MiB targets, S3 round-trips at 2 threads. That likely blows
  the 6h deadline on its own. The call supports max_compacted_files
  (compact-probe already uses it); capping each cron run and letting the 15-min
  schedule grind down the backlog incrementally is a better shape than one
  heroic transaction — if the file-list phase respects the cap, which needs a
  test.

  Proposed fix (millpond repo)

  1. --memory-limit/--threads defaults become env-overridable
  (COMPACTION_MEMORY_LIMIT, COMPACTION_THREADS) — k8s-native, no justfile
  signature churn; chart sets COMPACTION_MEMORY_LIMIT=16GB for prod.
  2. Manually test on prod (or a dry-run-ish probe) whether max_compacted_files
  bounds file-list memory; if yes, add a per-run cap to the tiered recipes.

  Want me to do (1) now and leave (2) as the follow-up test?

⏺ Question is whether it's the same tracked DuckDB OOM or the cgroup killing the
   pod (the c8 unaccounted-allocation mode). Let me pull the logs.

  Called grafana 7 times (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The managed-warehouse prod logs aren't in either Grafana Loki datasource I can
   reach (the viaduck namespace there is the main posthog cluster). Can you grab
   the state directly? These three would settle it:

  ! kubectl -n viaduck get pods -l app=viaduck-compaction
  --sort-by=.metadata.creationTimestamp | tail -3
  ! kubectl -n viaduck logs $(kubectl -n viaduck get pods -l
  app=viaduck-compaction --sort-by=.metadata.creationTimestamp -o name | tail
  -1) --tail=30
  ! kubectl -n viaduck describe pod $(kubectl -n viaduck get pods -l
  app=viaduck-compaction --sort-by=.metadata.creationTimestamp -o name | tail
  -1) | grep -A5 "Last State\|State:"

  What they'll distinguish:

  1. Log line compaction tuning: ... memory_limit=4GB → the env/image didn't
  land (chart not synced, or the job pod predates the prod tag promotion —
  pullPolicy: Always only matters at pod creation; an already-created Job keeps
  its image).
  2. memory_limit=16GB + same DuckDB Failed to get compaction file list at ~14.9
   GiB → the file list genuinely needs more than 16GB; we raise toward ~20GB
  and/or shrink the cap.
  3. OOMKilled / exit 137 in describe → cgroup kill: the merge phase's
  unaccounted c8 allocations ate the 8Gi headroom — we go the other way (lower
  DuckDB limit or raise pod memory, and cut COMPACTION_MAX_FILES).

✻ Cooked for 2m 26s

❯ It's cgroups.                                                                 

⏺ That settles it: the file list fit inside 16GB tracked, then the merge phase's
   untracked c8 allocations blew through the ~8Gi gap to the 24Gi cgroup. Two
  dials, both already env-wired — no code change needed:

  extraEnv:
    COMPACTION_MEMORY_LIMIT: "10GB"  # file list needs >4GB (OOMed at 3.7GiB); 
  10GB clears it
                                     # while widening cgroup headroom to ~14Gi
    COMPACTION_MAX_FILES: "25000"    # smaller merge transactions -> smaller 
  untracked
                                     # working set (c8 overshoot scales with 
  merge size)

  Logic: the tracked peak (file enumeration) and the untracked peak (merge
  buffers) happen in different phases, so we don't need 16GB tracked the whole
  time — we need enough for the list, and maximal slack for the merge. Shrinking
   the per-run cap 4× attacks the overshoot directly and makes each run faster;
  the cron just takes more ticks to clear the backlog (614k ÷ 25k ≈ 25 runs ≈ 6
  hours at the 15-min cadence — fine).

  If 10GB turns out too small for the file list, the failure flips back to the
  loud DuckDB error rather than a cgroup kill, which is the better mode to
  iterate from.

  Want me to put that on a charts branch and PR it?

✻ Cooked for 29s

❯ No, I'll patch it in argo.                                                    

⏺ Works. One note: once the numbers prove out, fold them back into
  argocd/viaduck/values/managed-warehouse-prod-us.yaml — an Argo-side override
  will be stomped by the next sync from git.

✻ Worked for 9s

※ recap: Goal: get prod DuckLake compaction running. It was cgroup-OOMKilled; you were about to patch COMPACTION_MEMORY_LIMIT=10GB and COMPACTION_MAX_FILES=25000 directly in Argo. Next: apply that patch and watch the next 15-min run.

❯ Did we set the compaction_max_files?                                          

⏺ No — git only sets COMPACTION_MEMORY_LIMIT: "16GB" (PR #12089).
  COMPACTION_MAX_FILES was never set in values, so the job runs the image
  default of 100000 baked into millpond #75. If you want the 25k cap, it needs
  to be part of your Argo patch alongside the memory limit.

⏺ Useful failure — it pins the geometry:

  - The cap landed (max 25000 files/run) but didn't shrink the file-list phase:
  still OOM at 9.2/9.3 GiB. DuckLake builds the candidate enumeration over the
  whole table/catalog before applying any cap, so file-list memory is O(total
  files), not O(cap).
  - The 16GB run passed the file list (it died later, in the merge, via cgroup).
   So the list needs somewhere in (10, 16] GB tracked.
  - The cgroup kill happened with the 100k cap; the untracked merge overshoot is
   what ate the 8Gi gap.

  So the two knobs control two different phases, and the right combination is:

  COMPACTION_MEMORY_LIMIT: "16GB"   # clears the file-list phase (proven)
  COMPACTION_MAX_FILES: "25000"     # shrinks the merge phase's untracked 
  overshoot ~4x
                                    # vs the 100k run that got cgroup-killed

  If the merge still gets cgroup-killed at 25k, next moves in order: cap to 10k,
   then raise pod memory (allocatable on the 4xlarge is ~28Gi, we're at 24Gi — a
   little room, not much).

  Also worth filing away: file-list memory scaling with catalog size regardless
  of the merge cap is an upstream DuckLake gripe (it joins the rowid-reuse
  question in your court) — though it self-heals somewhat as compaction shrinks
  the 12.5M-file catalog.

✻ Churned for 28s

※ recap: Goal: get prod DuckLake compaction running. You're patching the cronjob env in Argo; next action is setting COMPACTION_MEMORY_LIMIT=16GB with COMPACTION_MAX_FILES=25000 and watching the next 15-minute run.

❯ 26gi is the max I can get the ram up to and still get a node.  16BG memory    
  limit 25k file limit, so getting cgroup OOM killed...                         

⏺ That kills the "overshoot scales with the cap" theory — 100k and 25k both blow
   >10Gi of untracked memory, so the c8 overshoot is roughly cap-independent
  (likely the extension materializing the full 615k-entry file list + writer
  state outside the buffer manager). The squeeze is real: the file list needs
  >10GB inside DuckDB's limit, and the merge needs >10Gi outside it, and a
  4xlarge can't hold both.

  Two paths:

  A. One more Argo patch (free to try, may still die):
  COMPACTION_MEMORY_LIMIT: "12GB"   # untested gamble that the list fits between
   10-12
  COMPACTION_THREADS: "1"           # halves per-thread merge buffers
  COMPACTION_MAX_FILES: "5000"
  Evidence so far says the untracked part doesn't respect these knobs, so low
  confidence.

  B. Temporarily widen the nodepool for backlog-clearing (deterministic): the
  karpenter compaction pool is restricted to 4xlarge with a comment that 8xlarge
   "is 2x the cost without a clear win on the probe-sized workload" — there's
  now a very clear win. Allow 8xlarge (c7gn.8xlarge = 64GiB), bump the pod to
  ~48Gi, keep memory_limit=16GB → ~30Gi of untracked headroom. Let it grind the
  615k backlog down (even at 25k/run that's ~25 ticks), then revert to 4xlarge
  for steady-state — incremental compaction on fresh files is the "probe-sized
  workload" the 4xlarge restriction was written for.

  I'd do B; A's failure mode costs you another 15-minute tick each iteration and
   the curve so far isn't bending. B is two values edits (karpenter-config
  instance-size list + viaduck compaction resources) and a revert PR when the
  backlog's gone.

  Want the charts branch for B?

⏺ The strings are fine — k8s env values must be strings, so '25000' and '12' are
   exactly right (unquoted they'd be YAML ints and the API would reject them),
  and 12GB is unquoted but parses as a string anyway.

  The value I'd look hard at: COMPACTION_THREADS: '12'. Twelve threads is the
  exact configuration from the original c8 incident ("12 threads / 20 GB
  OOMKilled"), and per-thread merge buffers are untracked — it works against the
   cgroup headroom you're trying to protect. I'd suggested '1'; did the 2 pick
  up a 1 in front?

⏺ Pool today: c7gn/c8gn locked to 4xlarge (32GiB), cpu limit 16, no memory
  limit; the compaction EC2NodeClass inherits the 100Gi root volume (fine for
  the 50Gi /tmp). The ceiling within the existing family is c7gn/c8gn.16xlarge: 
  64 vCPU, 128 GiB, 200Gbps — ~$4/hr on-demand, and it's there the moment the
  job pod requests more memory than a 4xlarge holds.

  Proposed temporary config:

  What: karpenter-config prod-us
  Change: instance-size [4xlarge] → [4xlarge, 16xlarge]; cpu limit 16 → 64
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  What: viaduck prod-us resources
  Change: memory 24Gi → 100Gi (req=limit; ~118Gi allocatable on 128GiB
    Bottlerocket minus daemonsets)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  What: env
  Change: COMPACTION_MEMORY_LIMIT=16GB (proven for the file list),
    COMPACTION_MAX_FILES=25000, COMPACTION_THREADS=1 — folded into git values so

    a sync doesn't stomp your live patch with stale numbers

  Mechanics that make "temporary" cheap:
  - Keeping 4xlarge in the allowed list means Karpenter right-sizes
  automatically: the 100Gi pod forces a 16xlarge; when we later drop the pod
  back to 24Gi, it provisions 4xlarge again — the revert is just the
  resources/env lines, no nodepool churn.
  - concurrencyPolicy: Forbid + cpu limit 64 still means at most one node.
  - Headroom math: 16GB tracked + ~84Gi for the untracked c8 overshoot — 8× the
  worst overshoot we've evidenced.

  If 128GiB somehow isn't enough, the next rung is switching the pool to
  r-family (r8g.16xlarge = 512GiB), but I'd bet against needing it.

  Want this on a charts branch?

     ACCESSENTRIES      arn:aws:iam::645773004826:role/Karpenter-posthog-mw-prod-
     … +8 lines (ctrl+o to expand)


⏺ Found it — it's not connectivity, it's authorization:

  - Your current session for mw-prod-us is the developers-ro permission set
  (AWSReservedSSO_developers-ro_.../jakob@posthog.com)
  - The cluster's access entries only admit AWSReservedSSO_administrators_...
  (plus Karpenter/argocd/node roles)

  So AWS API calls all work (that's why my quota/subnet checks succeeded), but
  the k8s API has no access entry for developers-ro → 401, which k9s surfaces as
   the "cached dial" failure. The kubeconfig's get-token has no --profile baked
  in, so k9s uses whatever ambient session it finds — today that's the ro one.

  Fix is to run with the admin profile:

  ! AWS_PROFILE=mw-prod-us-admin aws sts get-caller-identity

  (if that errors, the admin session needs a fresh aws login/SSO auth first —
  run it with ! so it can open the browser), then launch k9s as
  AWS_PROFILE=mw-prod-us-admin k9s --context mw-prod-us. If you want it sticky,
  bake the profile into the kubeconfig user: kubectl config set-credentials 
  <mw-prod-us user> --exec-env AWS_PROFILE=mw-prod-us-admin — then k9s works
  regardless of shell env.

✻ Churned for 1m 57s

❯ Hmmm.  would rather be read only while poking around k8s, but ok.             

⏺ That's a reasonable ask, and EKS access entries support exactly that shape: an
   access entry for the developers-ro permission-set role with
  AmazonEKSViewPolicy (read-everything-except-secrets) or
  AmazonEKSAdminViewPolicy (includes secrets) on cluster scope. Then your
  default ro session gets working kubectl get/k9s browsing, and 403s only when
  you try to mutate — admin profile only comes out when you actually need to
  touch something. The existing entries look terraform-managed (the Karpenter
  one has the workspace suffix), so it'd land next to the cluster's access-entry
   config in posthog-cloud-infra rather than in charts. Until then, the
  admin-profile dance is the only path in.

  That 35.7Gi reading is the smoking gun for the whole saga: the untracked
  working set is ~20Gi+, so no 24Gi or 26Gi pod was ever going to survive
  regardless of caps and thread counts. On 100Gi there's ~64Gi of slack left
  even if it keeps climbing.

  target_file_size restored, and the backlog drops by ≤25k files — then the cron
   just keeps chewing, ~25 ticks to clear.

⏺ Definitive yes — a clean monotone curve through the whole merge:

  merge of 300 files: progress 3 → 7 → 11 → ... → 49 (sampled until completion)

  So DuckLake's merge runs as a real pipeline with tracked cardinality, and the
  seed-scan recipe ports straight into millpond's maintenance tool: heartbeat
  thread, query_progress() percentage + ETA each tick instead of six hours of
  silence.

  And there's a second thing the heartbeat should log that today made painfully
  obvious: process RSS. DuckDB's own accounting can't see the c8 overshoot, but
  /proc/self/status can — a tick like merge: ~40%, est. 22m remaining, 
  rss=35.7GiB (limit 100Gi) would have diagnosed this entire afternoon from the
  first failed run's logs, no kubectl spelunking.

  Want me to build that into ducklake_maintenance.py (heartbeat on compact,
  progress + RSS, same warn-once degradation as viaduck's)?

      705 +def _rss_bytes() -> int | None:                               
      706 +    """Current process RSS, or None if unreadable.            
      707 +                                                              
      708 +    Reads /proc/self/status (the prod container is Linux); fal
          +ls back to                                                    
      709 +    getrusage peak RSS elsewhere (macOS dev — note: peak, not 
          +current).                                                     
      710 +    RSS is the number that matters for compaction OOMs: DuckLa
          +ke's merge                                                    
      711 +    keeps a large working set OUTSIDE DuckDB's memory accounti
          +ng (bug c8),                                                  
      712 +    so the cgroup kills the pod while duckdb's own gauge looks
          + healthy.                                                     
      713 +    """                                                       
      714 +    try:                                                      
      715 +        with open("/proc/self/status") as f:                  
      716 +            for line in f:                                    
      717 +                if line.startswith("VmRSS:"):                 
      718 +                    return int(line.split()[1]) * 1024        
      719 +    except (OSError, ValueError, IndexError):                 
      720 +        pass                                                  
      721 +    try:                                                      
      722 +        import resource                                       
      723 +                                                              
      724 +        peak = resource.getrusage(resource.RUSAGE_SELF).ru_max
          +rss                                                           
      725 +        return peak if sys.platform == "darwin" else peak * 10
          +24                                                            
      726 +    except Exception:                                         
      727 +        return None                                           
      728 +                                                              
      729 +                                                              
      730 +_progress_poll_warned = False                                 
      731 +                                                              
      732 +                                                              
      733 +def _heartbeat_line(conn: duckdb.DuckDBPyConnection, label: st
          +r, elapsed: float) -> str:                                    
      734 +    """One heartbeat log line: elapsed, merge percentage + ETA
          + when DuckDB                                                  
      735 +    reports one (sub-1% readings are noise on huge merges), an
          +d process RSS.                                                
      736 +                                                              
      737 +    query_progress() failures degrade to elapsed-only (warned 
          +once): the                                                    
      738 +    heartbeat is decoration and must never kill the operation 
          +it watches.                                                   
      739 +    """                                                       
      740 +    global _progress_poll_warned                              
      741 +    parts = [f"{label}: {elapsed:.0f}s elapsed"]              
      742 +    try:                                                      
      743 +        pct = float(conn.query_progress())                    
      744 +    except Exception:                                         
      745 +        pct = -1.0                                            
      746 +        if not _progress_poll_warned:                         
      747 +            _progress_poll_warned = True                      
      748 +            log.warning("query_progress() poll failed; heartbe
          +at continues without percentages", exc_info=True)             
      749 +    if pct >= 100:                                            
      750 +        parts.append("~100% merged")                          
      751 +    elif pct >= 1:                                            
      752 +        eta = elapsed * (100.0 - pct) / pct                   
      753 +        parts.append(f"~{pct:.0f}% merged, est. {eta / 60:.0f}
          +m remaining")                                                 
      754 +    rss = _rss_bytes()                                        
      755 +    if rss is not None:                                       
      756 +        parts.append(f"rss={rss / 1024**3:.1f}GiB")           
      757 +    return ", ".join(parts)                                   
      758 +                                                              
      759 +                                                              
      760 +def _start_heartbeat(conn: duckdb.DuckDBPyConnection, label: s
          +tr, interval_s: float = 60.0) -> threading.Event:             
      761 +    """Log a heartbeat line every `interval_s` while a long bl
          +ocking call runs                                              
      762 +    on `conn` from the main thread. Returns the Event to .set(
          +) when done                                                   
      763 +    (use try/finally). Daemon thread; safe to leak on crash.  
      764 +                                                              
      765 +    Cross-thread query_progress() is a lock-free atomic read o
          +f executor                                                    
      766 +    state (it's how Jupyter's progress bar works) — worst case
          + is a stale                                                   
      767 +    reading, never corruption. The connection's single query s
          +lot belongs to                                                
      768 +    the watched call, so the percentage can't be someone else'
          +s query.                                                      
      769 +    """                                                       
      770 +    stop = threading.Event()                                  
      771 +    start_t = time.monotonic()                                
      772 +                                                              
      773 +    def _tick() -> None:                                      
      774 +        while not stop.wait(timeout=interval_s):              
      775 +            log.info("%s", _heartbeat_line(conn, label, time.m
          +onotonic() - start_t))                                        
      776 +                                                              
      777 +    threading.Thread(target=_tick, daemon=True).start()       
      778 +    return stop                                               
      779 +                                                              
      780 +                                                              
      781  def compact(
      782      conn: duckdb.DuckDBPyConnection,
      783      tier: int,

   (9.7 → 14.2GiB on its way to the ~35.7Gi plateau we measured externally last
  run).

  Two reads on the missing percentage:

  1. The first minutes are the candidate-enumeration phase (the part that used
  to OOM — it alone took 60–90s in the failed runs), which may not report
  pipeline progress; percentages should appear once the actual merge pipeline
  starts. My local test showed them immediately, but local had no 12.5M-row
  catalog scan in front.
  2. Or it's reporting honestly and we're just sub-1% — last run took ~51 min,
  so 120s ≈ 4%... if the denominator only covered the capped 25k. If DuckLake's
  progress denominator covers the full 616k candidate set it will sit under 1%
  (suppressed) for the first ~13 minutes.

  A couple more heartbeat lines will distinguish: percentages appearing soon =
  case 1; appearing at ~13 min as ~1% = denominator-is-everything.

  One side effect of the kill worth knowing: candidates are still 616,990 — the
  killed run's 25k merge was a single uncommitted transaction, so its ~50
  minutes of work rolled back cleanly. Nothing lost but the time.

⏺ Bash(uv run python <<'EOF'
      import duckdb, tempfile, threading, time…)
  ⎿  postgres catalog, cap=25000: 1.5s, progress: [-1, 1, 3, 5, 7, 9, 10, 1
     2, 14, 16, 17, 19, 21, 23, 25, 26, 28, 30, 32, 33, 35, 37, 39, 40, 42]

⏺ Postgres catalog reports fine too — clean 1→42% curve. So the API works in
  every shape I can reproduce; what's left is scale, and it points at a suspect
  I built: the sub-1% suppression. If DuckLake's progress denominator covers the
   full file population (12.5M table files, or 616k candidates) rather than the
  capped 25k, a capped prod run finishes at ~0.2–4% total — meaning the real
  value may be sitting at 0.3% and slowly climbing while my suppression renders
  it as silence. Locally, candidates ≈ merged, so the curve always ran 0→100 and
   the suppression never bit.

  The fix is to invert the choice for compaction: render sub-1% readings at one
  decimal (~0.4% merged, no ETA) and stay silent only on a true -1. Then the
  next run distinguishes the cases definitively — a slowly climbing 0.x%
  confirms the denominator theory (and even gives a usable progress signal:
  0.2%→0.4% = halfway through the run), while persistent silence means genuinely
   no estimate.

  Small change to _heartbeat_line + tests in millpond. Build it?

✻ Cooked for 1m 37s

❯ If I merege this, will the currently running job survive its initial limit?   

⏺ No — the CronJob change only affects newly created Jobs. Kubernetes sets
  activeDeadlineSeconds on the Job at creation time; updating the CronJob
  template doesn't retroactively modify running Jobs.

  The current Job (viaduck-compaction-29688465) has activeDeadlineSeconds: 21600
   baked in from when it was created. At ~2h45m elapsed that's fine, but if it's
   still running at 6h from start (~05:52 UTC) it gets killed.

  To protect it: patch the live Job directly right now:

  ! kubectl --context mw-prod-us -n viaduck patch job
  viaduck-compaction-29688465 --type=json
  -p='[{"op":"remove","path":"/spec/activeDeadlineSeconds"}]'

  That's a safe in-place mutation — removes the limit from the running Job
  without touching the pod.

⏺ Explore(Analyze ducklake_merge_adjacent_files source)
  ⎿  Done (39 tool uses · 81.1k tokens · 3m 10s)
  (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Key findings from the source:

  Candidate enumeration (GetFilesForCompaction) runs once per call — a 5-way
  JOIN with GROUP BY against the full 12.5M-row ducklake_data_file table via the
   postgres extension. That's the phase that was OOMing at 4GB, not what's
  running now.

  The actual bottleneck today is single-threaded S3 I/O. The merge:
  1. Reads all 25,000 source parquet files sequentially from S3 (each file = one
   HTTP GET)
  2. Writes ~5,000 output files back (25k files @ ~400KB → 5MB targets)

  At threads=1 with no parallelism on S3 round-trips, even at 200ms per file
  that's ~1.4h just for reads before any processing. 4+ hours at 1 thread is
  about right.

  The c8 bug (untracked RSS from the merge working set) made raising threads
  dangerous before — but we now know the RSS plateau is ~38GiB regardless of
  cap. The 128GiB node has ~90GiB of headroom. Raising to threads=4 would
  parallelise the S3 reads and likely cut runtime by 3-4×. Worth trying on the
  next run.

  The also-notable finding: the metadata write at commit time is properly
  batched (single DELETE ... IN (...) for all 25k file IDs) — so the catalog
  round-trips aren't the problem.

  Actionable for next run: patch COMPACTION_THREADS from '1' to '4' in Argo,
  keep everything else the same.

   both the ~38GiB RSS plateau and the near-zero S3 I/O.

  Bluntly: this run has been stuck for ~3.5 hours doing something that isn't S3

  candidate set is being processed in some catalog-heavy loop we can't see.
  Either way, at 1.7MB/s network it hasn't gotten to the actual file merging
  yet.

   ceiling is known-safe at 38.8GiB on this box), and investigate whether that
  12.5M-row enumeration query has the indexes it needs on the postgres side.

⏺ There it is. PID 25279 has been idle in transaction for 4h07m, blocked on a
  COPY from ducklake_table_stats. That's not a slow query — it's a hung
  transaction. The postgres extension opened a transaction, ran its COPY to pull
   ducklake_table_stats, then stopped. Something in DuckDB is waiting before it
  can proceed, holding the transaction open.

  PID 6074 is the same pattern — 4.5 minutes idle in transaction on a COPY from
  ducklake_data_file. That one started recently so it may be a separate
  connection making progress.

  The 4h07m hang on ducklake_table_stats is the smoking gun. DuckDB's postgres
  scanner likely opened that transaction as part of the candidate enumeration
  and is now blocked waiting on something before it can commit or close it —
  probably waiting on S3 (the actual file reads/writes), but with the
  transaction left open and idle on the postgres side.

  This is a known pattern with DuckDB's postgres extension: it holds an open
  transaction across long operations to maintain snapshot isolation, but
  postgres sees it as idle-in-transaction. It's not stuck — it's just that
  postgres can't do anything with that connection until DuckDB closes it.

  The real question is why the S3 work hasn't started after 4 hours. PID 6074
  being only 4.5 minutes old suggests the enumeration might have just finished
  and we're now actually starting the merge. What does the network I/O look like
   right now compared to 30 minutes ago?

  PID 6074 being only 4.5 minutes old when you ran that query and the network
  being flat suggests the enumeration is still ongoing — it's just started on
  ducklake_data_file (the 12.5M-row table), which is the expensive JOIN. The
  4h07m transaction on ducklake_table_stats opened first as part of the same
  enumeration sequence.

  So the real timeline: 4+ hours just to COPY ducklake_table_stats + start on
  ducklake_data_file. The bottleneck isn't DuckDB's join engine — it's the sheer
   volume of data being pulled over the postgres wire from a 12.5M-row table
  into DuckDB memory one COPY at a time. At ~1.9 MB/s that's the postgres
  extension's scan throughput, not DuckDB processing.

  At that rate, ducklake_data_file at 12.5M rows (rough estimate ~100 bytes/row
  = ~1.2GB) would take another 10 minutes — so the actual S3 merge might finally
   start soon. But this also means every single cron run will pay this 4+ hour
  catalog scan cost upfront before touching a single file. The index
  recommendation from the source analysis isn't optional — it's essential.

✻ Worked for 10s

❯ megaduck=> \d public.ducklake_data_file                                       
                     Table "public.ducklake_data_file"                          
        Column      |       Type        | Collation | Nullable | Default        
  ------------------+-------------------+-----------+----------+---------       
   data_file_id     | bigint            |           | not null |                
   table_id         | bigint            |           |          |                
   begin_snapshot   | bigint            |           |          |                
   end_snapshot     | bigint            |           |          |                
   file_order       | bigint            |           |          |                
   path             | character varying |           |          |                
   path_is_relative | boolean           |           |          |                
   file_format      | character varying |           |          |                
   record_count     | bigint            |           |          |                
   file_size_bytes  | bigint            |           |          |                
   footer_size      | bigint            |           |          |                
   row_id_start     | bigint            |           |          |                
   partition_id     | bigint            |           |          |                
   encryption_key   | character varying |           |          |                
   mapping_id       | bigint            |           |          |                
   partial_max      | bigint            |           |          |                
  Indexes:                                                                      
      "ducklake_data_file_pkey" PRIMARY KEY, btree (data_file_id)               

⏺ Just the primary key — no index on table_id, end_snapshot, or file_size_bytes.
   The enumeration query filters on all three plus does the GROUP BY join on
  table_id. Every access is a full sequential scan of 12.5M rows.

  Kill the current run, then create the index before restarting:

  ! psql "..." -c "CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY ducklake_data_file_compaction_idx
  ON public.ducklake_data_file (table_id, end_snapshot, file_size_bytes) WHERE
  end_snapshot IS NULL;"

  CONCURRENTLY so it doesn't lock the table; WHERE end_snapshot IS NULL is a
  partial index that only covers live files (the exact filter the compaction
  query uses) — much smaller and faster to build and use. This runs in the
  background in postgres and should complete in minutes on a db.m8gd.2xlarge
  even at 12.5M rows.

  While that's building, also worth checking the other tables the query joins:

  ! psql "..." -c "\d public.ducklake_delete_file; \d
  public.ducklake_file_partition_value;"

✻ Sautéed for 7s

❯ How fast could we get it running with more threads? What's the NIC on this    
  instance and S3 capable of?                                                   

⏺ The c8gn.16xlarge has a 200 Gbps baseline NIC (~25 GB/s). S3 is the practical
  ceiling — S3 parallel throughput from a single client typically tops out
  around 10–15 GB/s with enough concurrency (S3's per-prefix limit is
  effectively unlimited at scale, but per-request latency means you need many
  parallel requests to saturate it).

  At 68 MB/s with 4 threads, each thread is doing ~17 MB/s — well below what S3
  can deliver. The bottleneck at low thread counts is per-request latency
  (~5–50ms per GET), not bandwidth. Scaling threads multiplies effective
  throughput roughly linearly until you hit S3 saturation or DuckDB's internal
  merge overhead.

  Rough estimates:

  ┌─────────┬────────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────┐
  │ Threads │         Est. S3 throughput         │ 25k files @ ~400KB avg │
  ├─────────┼────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ 4 (now) │ ~70 MB/s                           │ ~60 min                │
  ├─────────┼────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ 8       │ ~140 MB/s                          │ ~30 min                │
  ├─────────┼────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ 16      │ ~280 MB/s                          │ ~15 min                │
  ├─────────┼────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ 32      │ ~500 MB/s                          │ ~8 min                 │
  ├─────────┼────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ 64      │ ~800 MB/s (S3 ceiling approaching) │ ~5 min                 │
  └─────────┴────────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────┘

  The risk ceiling is now known: 13 GiB RSS at 4 threads on a 128 GiB node, so
  even at 64 threads the c8 untracked overshoot probably stays well under 100
  GiB. The only real question is whether DuckDB's merge plan holds up under high
   parallelism — worth jumping to 16 or 32 and watching RSS for a run or two
  before going further.

✻ Worked for 6s

❯ And what would the expected throughput be at 64?                              

⏺ At 32 threads we're getting ~128 MB/s. S3 per-request latency scaling tends to
   be sublinear above ~16-32 concurrent requests as you start hitting per-prefix
   throughput limits rather than per-request latency. Realistically:

  - 64 threads: ~180-220 MB/s (40-70% gain over 32, not 2×)
  - 128 threads: probably diminishing returns, ~250 MB/s at best

  At 220 MB/s the 25k-file run would complete in ~15 minutes vs ~25 minutes now.
   Marginal for a single run but meaningful across 25+ runs to clear the
  backlog.

  The bigger lever at this point is COMPACTION_MAX_FILES — at 25k files per run
  and ~25 min/run, clearing 619k files takes ~25 runs = ~10 hours of cron time.
  Bumping to 100k files per run would cut that to ~6 runs, and the catalog
  commit (single batched DELETE IN (...)) handles larger sets fine. The
  memory/RSS risk is the merge working set, which scales with files in flight —
  worth watching RSS on a 100k run before committing to it.

✻ Churned for 13s

❯ What's the sweet spot for max files and num threads on this instance?         

⏺ We don't know yet — we've only measured one point (32 threads / 25k files /
  ~128 MB/s / 17 GiB RSS). The unknowns are:

  1. Does RSS scale with max_compacted_files? The working set is all 25k source
  files held in memory during the merge. At 400KB avg × 25k = ~10 GiB of raw
  file data, which matches the ~13-17 GiB we see. 100k files would be ~40 GiB of
   raw data — still safe on 128 GiB, but needs verification.
  2. Does throughput plateau before 64 threads? We have 32→128 MB/s. One more
  data point at 64 would tell us if we're in the linear or diminishing-returns
  regime.

  The most useful experiment: bump both together — 64 threads / 100k files — and
   watch RSS and throughput on the next run. If RSS stays under ~60 GiB and
  throughput is >180 MB/s, that's probably the sweet spot: 100k files in ~15-20
  min = ~6 runs to clear the backlog. If RSS spikes toward 80+ GiB, drop files
  back to 50k.

  Want to kill the current run and try that?

✻ Cogitated for 12s

❯ Let's let this run.  RSS just jumped to 25GB.                                 

⏺ Good data point — RSS climbing as the merge buffer fills. Let it run and watch
   where it plateaus. If it keeps climbing linearly with files processed that's
  the working-set growth we'd expect; if it plateaus like before (~38 GiB at 4
  threads) then 32 threads just shifts the plateau slightly higher. Either way
  informs the 64-thread/100k-file experiment.

✻ Crunched for 7s

❯ Does the current three stage approach for compaction make sense in a ducklake 
   with this file distribution?                                                 

⏺ Good question. Let me look at what the actual file distribution is before
  answering.

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Run that against megaduck — the distribution will tell us whether the 3-tier
  design makes sense or if the lake is almost entirely sub-1MiB files (typical
  for high-frequency append workloads like events), in which case tiers 2 and 3
  are largely pointless overhead and a single wide tier straight to 128 MiB
  targets would be simpler and faster.

✻ Brewed for 17s

❯ Wouldn't it be faster to run it against the RDS?                              

⏺ Yes, just run it directly:

  ! psql "postgres://megaduck:<password>@posthog-megaduck-mw-prod-us.cqh4uum4mjv
  1.us-east-1.rds.amazonaws.com:5432/megaduck" -c "SELECT CASE WHEN
  file_size_bytes < 1024*1024 THEN '< 1 MiB' WHEN file_size_bytes < 10*1024*1024
   THEN '1-10 MiB' WHEN file_size_bytes < 64*1024*1024 THEN '10-64 MiB' WHEN
  file_size_bytes < 128*1024*1024 THEN '64-128 MiB' ELSE '>= 128 MiB' END AS
  size_bucket, count(*) AS files, pg_size_pretty(sum(file_size_bytes)) AS
  total_size, pg_size_pretty(avg(file_size_bytes)::bigint) AS avg_size FROM
  public.ducklake_data_file WHERE end_snapshot IS NULL GROUP BY 1 ORDER BY
  min(file_size_bytes);"

✻ Cogitated for 7s

❯  size_bucket |  files   | total_size | avg_size                               
  -------------+----------+------------+----------                              
   < 1 MiB     |   619190 | 228 GB     | 386 kB                                 
   1-10 MiB    |    25145 | 126 GB     | 5245 kB                                
   10-64 MiB   |   388562 | 11 TB      | 29 MB                                  
   64-128 MiB  | 12684295 | 1142 TB    | 94 MB                                  
   >= 128 MiB  |      212 | 34 GB      | 166 MB                                 
  (5 rows)                                                                      

⏺ That's a very different picture from what I expected. The lake is dominated by
   12.7M files at 64-128 MiB — already well-compacted. The distribution is:

  ┌─────────────────────┬───────┬────────┬──────────────────────────────────┐
  │        Tier         │ Files │  Data  │             Verdict              │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────┼────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Tier 1 (< 1 MiB)    │ 619k  │ 228 GB │ Active backlog — worth merging   │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────┼────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Tier 2 (1-10 MiB)   │ 25k   │ 126 GB │ Small, probably tier-1 output    │
  │                     │       │        │ from prior partial runs          │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────┼────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Tier 3 (10-64 MiB)  │ 389k  │ 11 TB  │ Transitional — worth merging up  │
  │                     │       │        │ to 128 MiB                       │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────┼────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Already at target   │ 12.7M │ 1,142  │ Done — don't touch               │
  │ (64-128 MiB)        │       │ TB     │                                  │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────┼────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Oversized (>128     │ 212   │ 34 GB  │ Negligible                       │
  │ MiB)                │       │        │                                  │
  └─────────────────────┴───────┴────────┴──────────────────────────────────┘

  So the three-stage approach doesn't make sense for this lake. Tiers 1 and 2 
  are the actual work (~650k files, ~354 GB). Tier 3 has 389k files and 11 TB of
   real work to do. But the 12.7M files at 64-128 MiB are already at the tier-3
  target size — running tier 3 against them is wasted catalog enumeration
  scanning 12.7M rows to find the 389k that actually need merging.

  The practical recommendation: skip tier 3 entirely until tiers 1+2 are
  cleared, then run tier 3 as a one-shot. Or better — add a min_file_size to
  tier 3 that excludes anything already ≥ 64 MiB from the candidate count, which
   the query already supports. The tier-3 spec currently has no min, so it's
  enumerating all 13M live files every run.

  Watch for the compact tier-1: (table_name, files_merged, ...) result rows
  followed by target_file_size restored — that's the commit landing.

✻ Cooked for 8s

❯ What if we didn't set a max files value?                                      

⏺ With 619k tier-1 candidates at 32 threads and ~128 MB/s, one uncapped run
  would process all of them in a single transaction — ~80 minutes of S3 work
  plus one giant catalog commit (DELETE IN (619k IDs) against
  ducklake_data_file).

  The catalog commit is the risk: a 619k-row DELETE in a single postgres
  transaction holds locks, generates WAL, and could cause autovacuum pressure on
   megaduck. The m8gd.2xlarge can handle it, but it's a long single transaction
  on a production RDS instance that millpond and viaduck are also hitting.

  The other risk is that if anything goes wrong mid-merge (network hiccup, S3
  transient, pod eviction) — the entire 80 minutes of S3 work rolls back and you
   start over. With 25k caps each run commits independently, so a failure only
  loses one run's work.

  Given the index is now in place and each run takes ~15 minutes, clearing the
  backlog in ~25 runs over ~6 hours of cron time is pretty reasonable. The main
  argument for removing the cap is if you want to do it in one shot tonight and
  watch it — in which case bumping to 100k or 200k is a better middle ground
  than fully uncapped.

✻ Churned for 12s

❯ As it's configured now, it's going to launch tier 2 and 3 immediately after   
  this.  What's the estimate on how long they'll take to run?                   

⏺ Tier 2 has 25k files at 5 MiB avg = ~125 GB. At 128 MB/s → ~17 minutes. But
  it's also capped at 25k — and there happen to be exactly 25,145 tier-2 files,
  so it'll just barely fit in one run.

  Tier 3 is the painful one: 389k files at 29 MiB avg = ~11 TB. Capped at 25k
  files per run = ~16 runs at ~30 min each (larger files, more data per file) =
  ~8 hours just for tier 3. And as we discussed, most of those 12.7M files it
  has to enumerate to find the 389k candidates are already at the right size —
  though with the index that enumeration is now fast.

  Given the file distribution, I'd suggest patching compact-all-tiers to skip
  tier 3 tonight and just let tiers 1+2 clear. Tier 3 is a separate one-shot
  operation worth scheduling deliberately rather than running back-to-back with
  the backlog clearance. The 389k files at 10-64 MiB work fine as-is for query
  performance — they're not the problem the 619k tiny files were.

✻ Churned for 10s

❯ How would one patch that?                                                     

⏺ The justfile recipe is just compact-all-tiers table="": compact-to-tier-1 
  compact-to-tier-2 compact-to-tier-3 — no way to skip a tier without changing
  the recipe or the CronJob command.

  Easiest: patch the CronJob args to run only tiers 1 and 2:

  ! kubectl --context mw-prod-us -n viaduck patch cronjob viaduck-compaction
  --type=json -p='[{"op":"replace","path":"/spec/jobTemplate/spec/template/spec/
  containers/0/args","value":["compact-to-tier-1","&&","compact-to-tier-2"]}]'

  Wait — just doesn't work that way, the args are passed as a single recipe
  name. The chart has:

  command: ["just", "-f", "/justfile"]
  args: ["compact-all-tiers"]

  So change args to ["compact-to-tier-1"] first, let tier 1 finish across all
  the cron runs, then change to ["compact-to-tier-1", "compact-to-tier-2"] (just
   passes multiple recipe names as separate args and runs them in order).

  Actually the cleanest option for now: once the current manual job finishes,
  patch args to ["compact-to-tier-1"] so the scheduled cron only runs tier 1
  until the backlog is clear. Then manually trigger a tier-2-only job
  (compact-to-tier-2) as a one-shot. Tier 3 deferred entirely.

  Want me to prep that patch?

✻ Brewed for 8s

❯ It's annoying we have no more observability into Duck's progress.             

⏺ Agreed — and we know the API works for merge (verified locally). The missing
  piece is that the progress denominator is almost certainly the full 619k
  candidate count, so with 25k capped we're sitting at ~4% for the whole run and
   the sub-1% suppression was eating it. PR #77 (render sub-1% verbatim) would
  show ~4.0% merged (sub-1%; ETA unavailable) — still no useful ETA, but at
  least you'd see it ticking up across runs.

  The actually useful thing would be a row counter: DuckLake must be tracking
  files merged internally, and if query_progress() reports
  fraction-of-candidates rather than fraction-of-cap, we could derive files
  merged = pct/100 * total_candidates. Worth logging that alongside the
  percentage once #77 is in — ~4.1% of 619k candidates merged (12k files this 
  run) would be genuinely useful.

  For right now though, the heartbeat RSS + network rate is the best we have.
  Want me to add a 30-second network sampler to the heartbeat alongside RSS?

✻ Cogitated for 13s

❯ Maybe later.  Duck's tracking the row counter internally; it's not writing it 
   to postgres anywhere.  Doing the whole thing in one giant transaction is     
  relatively lame.                                                              

⏺ Yeah — it's a classic OLAP-engine-meets-OLTP-catalog impedance mismatch.
  DuckDB wants to process everything in one vectorized pipeline and commit
  atomically; postgres wants short transactions. The result is you get zero
  visibility until the commit lands, and any failure throws away hours of S3
  work.

  The right fix upstream would be incremental checkpointing — commit each merged
   file (or a small batch) as it completes, updating ducklake_data_file with the
   new entry and scheduling the old ones for deletion, so progress is visible
  and restartable. That's essentially what Iceberg's rewrite manifests + commit
  approach does. But it requires DuckLake to change its transaction model for
  compaction, which is an upstream conversation.

  In the meantime the max_compacted_files cap is the pragmatic workaround — it
  bounds the blast radius of a failure and makes each run's commit fast. 50k
  files is a reasonable unit of work.

✻ Baked for 15s

❯ Duck could maintain an internal table that we could query...                  

⏺ Yes — a ducklake_compaction_progress table in the metadata schema, updated
  per-file-merged within the transaction. You'd query it cross-transaction via a
   separate connection and get a live row counter. Postgres has no problem with
  dirty reads across connections in the same transaction (READ COMMITTED), so
  you'd see incremental updates even before the compaction commits.

  It's a small change to the extension: after each file is merged and before the
   result is batched, INSERT/UPDATE a progress row (table_id, job_id, 
  files_merged, bytes_merged, started_at, updated_at). The heartbeat thread
  polls it via a second postgres connection. On commit or rollback, the row gets
   cleaned up or left as a post-mortem.

  Worth filing upstream with the DuckLake team — it's a one-table addition that
  unblocks operational visibility for anyone running compaction at scale. You
  have the relationship there.

       82  ## Known Issues
       83  
       84  ### Bug c8: Untracked merge working set
       29 -DuckLake's merge keeps a large working set **outside DuckDB's 
          -`memory_limit` accounting**. Observed prod: ~20 GiB untracked 
          -on top of the 16GB DuckDB limit = ~38 GiB total RSS. This caus
          -es OOM kills if the cgroup is too small regardless of memory_l
          -imit or per-run file caps.                                    
       85 +DuckLake's merge keeps a large working set **outside DuckDB's 
          +`memory_limit` accounting**. With index in place and 32 thread
          +s, observed RSS bounces 10–32 GiB (not the 38 GiB flat plateau
          + seen before the index). The pre-index plateau was caused by t
          +he catalog scan bloating the buffer pool, not the merge itself
          +.                                                             
       86  
       31 -**Workaround**: Pod memory must be large enough to hold both t
          -he tracked and untracked peaks. Empirically: 100 GiB pod / 16 
          -GB DuckDB limit works on the initial prod backlog.            
       87 +**Workaround**: Pod memory must exceed the tracked + untracked
          + peak. Post-index empirical ceiling: ~32 GiB at 32 threads / 2
          +5k files. 100 GiB pod is conservative headroom; drop to 24 GiB
          + (4xlarge) once the backlog is cleared and steady-state RSS is
          + confirmed.                                                   
       88  
       33 -### Missing index on `ducklake_data_file`                     
       34 -The candidate enumeration query filters on `(table_id, end_sna
          -pshot, file_size_bytes)` but only a primary key on `data_file_
          -id` exists. On a 12.5M-row table this causes a **sequential sc
          -an via postgres wire** taking 4+ hours before the first S3 fil
          -e is touched.                                                 
       89 +### Missing indexes on catalog tables (FIXED June 2026)       
       90 +Without indexes, the candidate enumeration scans 12.5M rows ov
          +er the postgres wire: **4+ hours before the first S3 file is t
          +ouched**. The pod appears alive (RSS flat at 38 GiB, 1.7 MB/s

          +connection for hours.

       93  ```sql
       94 +-- Primary bottleneck: candidate filter + live-files partial i
          +ndex                                                          
       95  CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY ducklake_data_file_compaction_idx
       96    ON public.ducklake_data_file (table_id, end_snapshot, file_s
           ize_bytes)

       42 -`CONCURRENTLY` avoids locking; `WHERE end_snapshot IS NULL` is

          - the compaction query uses) — much smaller to build and mainta

      108  
       44 -Also check `ducklake_delete_file` and `ducklake_file_partition
          -_value` for similar missing indexes (these are also JOINed in 
          -the enumeration query).                                       
      109 +After indexes: candidate enumeration takes **1 second** instea
          +d of 4+ hours.                                                
      110  
      111 +### Single-transaction merge (upstream limitation)            
      112 +DuckLake merges all `max_compacted_files` in one postgres tran
          +saction with no incremental checkpointing. Consequences:      
      113 +- Zero progress visibility until commit lands (DuckDB tracks p
          +rogress internally but doesn't write it to postgres mid-transa
          +ction).                                                       
      114 +- Failure at any point rolls back all S3 work — only the `max_
          +compacted_files` cap bounds the blast radius.                 
      115 +- No way to query files-merged-so-far without a `ducklake_comp
          +action_progress` table in the metadata schema (potential upstr
          +eam feature request: per-file INSERT/UPDATE within the transac
          +tion, readable via a separate READ COMMITTED connection).     
      116 +                                                              
      117 +**Workaround**: keep `COMPACTION_MAX_FILES` sized so each run 
          +completes in a reasonable window (~15-30 min). 50k files at 32
          + threads takes ~15-20 minutes.                                
      118 +                                                              
      119 +### Argo auto-sync stomps live patches                        
      120 +Any `kubectl patch` to the CronJob is overwritten on the next 
          +Argo sync when auto-sync is enabled. Disable auto-sync in Argo
          + before patching for operational tuning. Remember to re-enable
          + and commit permanent changes back to git.                    
      121 +

      123  
      124 +## Performance Characteristics (post-index, June 2026, c8gn.16
          +xlarge)                                                       
      125 +                                                              
      126 +| Threads | S3 throughput | RSS peak | 25k files est. |       
      127 +|---------|-------------|---------|----------------|          
      128 +| 1 | ~68 MB/s | ~13 GiB | ~60 min |                          
      129 +| 4 | ~128 MB/s | ~17 GiB | ~25 min |                         
      130 +| 32 | ~128-200 MB/s | ~29 GiB (bouncy) | ~15-20 min |        
      131 +                                                              
      132 +- Instance NIC: 200 Gbps (~25 GB/s). S3 practical ceiling: ~10
          +-15 GB/s with sufficient concurrency.                         
      133 +- RSS bounces rather than plateauing at higher thread counts —
          + 32 threads GC's intermediate buffers between file batches.   
      134 +- Scaling from 4→32 threads is sublinear on S3 throughput (~0-
          +60% gain) due to per-request latency dominating, not bandwidth
          +.                                                             
      135 +- `COMPACTION_MAX_FILES` controls blast radius; RSS scales rou
          +ghly with files-in-flight (~400KB avg × 25k ≈ 10 GiB raw data)
          +.                                                             
      136 +

           ery, 120)

           on DESC LIMIT 10;
      205     ```

      224  
      225  ## Heartbeat Log Format (millpond PRs #76, #77)
      226  
      227  Each minute during a merge:
      228  ```
      129 -compact tier-1 merge: 3600s elapsed, ~42% merged, est. 50m rem
          -aining, rss=38.7GiB                                           
      229 +compact tier-1 merge: 360s elapsed, ~0.4% merged (sub-1%; ETA 
          +unavailable), rss=21.6GiB                                     
      230  ```
      131 -- **No percentage** → either `-1` (query not yet started) or d
          -enominator-mismatch (full catalog vs capped subset; will show 
          -as `~0.4% merged (sub-1%; ETA unavailable)` after PR #77).    
      132 -- **RSS** — the number that matters for OOM risk; DuckDB's own
          - accounting understates it (bug c8).                          
      133 -- **ETA** — linear extrapolation from DuckDB's input-scanned e
          -stimate; minutes-vs-hours accuracy.                           
      231 +- **Percentage** — DuckLake's denominator covers the full cand
          +idate count, not the capped subset. With 25k cap on 619k candi
          +dates, expect `~4%` for the whole run. Sub-1% is rendered verb
          +atim (liveness signal) after PR #77; no ETA since extrapolatio
          +n would be absurd.                                            
      232 +- **RSS** — the number that matters for OOM risk; DuckDB's own
          + accounting understates it (bug c8). Bouncy RSS = healthy GC b
          +etween file batches. Flat high RSS = catalog scan bloating buf
          +fer pool (check for missing index).                           
      233 +- **ETA** — only meaningful when the cap ≈ total candidates (e
          +.g. steady-state with small backlog).                         
      234 +                                                              
      235 +---                                                           
      236 +                                                              
      237 +## Upstream Feature Requests                                  
      238 +                                                              
      239 +- **`ducklake_compaction_progress` table**: per-file INSERT/UP
          +DATE within the merge transaction, readable via a separate REA
          +D COMMITTED connection. Would give live files-merged counter w
          +ithout waiting for commit. Currently DuckDB tracks progress in
          +ternally but never writes it to postgres mid-transaction.     
      240 +- **Incremental checkpointing**: commit each merged file (or s
          +mall batch) independently, making compaction restartable and f
          +ailure blast radius = one file. Analogous to Iceberg rewrite-m
          +anifests approach.

  - Performance table (threads vs throughput vs RSS vs estimated run time)

  - Bug c8 revised to reflect post-index reality (bouncy RSS ≠ plateau)
