✻ Crunched for 53s

❯ It's only flushing at 12 MB/s?                                                

⏺ Yeah, that's suspicious for 32 threads. At 12 MB/s the output writes would
  take another 30+ minutes for the ~25k merged output files. Possible
  explanations:

  1. S3 multipart upload overhead — small output files (~5 MiB each) don't
  benefit from multipart; each is a single PUT with round-trip latency. 25k
  output files × ~20ms = ~8 minutes sequential, but 32 threads should
  parallelize that to ~15 seconds.
  2. It already wrote most of the output and what we're seeing is the catalog
  commit phase — postgres writes, scheduling deletions, updating metadata. At 12
   MB/s that's the postgres wire traffic, not S3.
  3. DuckDB is sorting/finalizing output in memory before writing — RSS still at
   35 GiB supports something large still in memory.

  Check what postgres is doing right now:

  ! psql "..." -c "SELECT pid, now() - query_start AS duration, state,
  left(query, 120) FROM pg_stat_activity WHERE state != 'idle' ORDER BY duration
   DESC LIMIT 5;"

✻ Brewed for 9s

❯ What's the point of relatively large max file numbers, especially at idle.    
  If this thing is running every 15 minutes, 10k files should be well over the  
  creation rate.                                                                

⏺ Exactly right. At steady state the lake is getting new sub-1MiB files from
  ongoing writes — for a production events table that's probably hundreds to a
  few thousand files per 15-minute window, not tens of thousands. 10k would
  comfortably cover it with headroom, and each run would commit in a couple of
  minutes.

  The large cap only matters for clearing the initial backlog. Once the 619k are
   gone, the right steady-state config is probably:

  - COMPACTION_MAX_FILES: 10000 (covers the write rate with headroom)
  - COMPACTION_THREADS: 4 (4xlarge has 16 vCPUs, plenty for a light workload)
  - Pod memory: 24 GiB back on a 4xlarge
  - activeDeadlineSeconds: restore something sane, maybe 1800s (30 min)

  The 15-minute cron cadence plus a 10k cap means each run is a quick cleanup
  that keeps the file count from accumulating rather than a heroic
  backlog-clearing operation. Much cheaper operationally.

  Worth adding that to the runbook as the recommended steady-state config once
  you get there.

✻ Crunched for 11s

❯ I mean even with the backfill.  Multiple, smaller batches seems like they     
  would be pretty much just as efficient, even if we did have to run more jobs. 

⏺ Agreed. The transaction model actually makes smaller batches strictly better
  during backfill too:

  - Faster commit: 10k-row DELETE IN (...) vs 50k-row is meaningfully cheaper on
   postgres
  - Faster failure recovery: lose 10 minutes of S3 work on a failure, not 30
  - Better observability: you see a committed result every 10 minutes instead of
   waiting 30+
  - RSS stays lower: working set scales with batch size, so 10k stays under 15
  GiB vs 35 GiB for 25k
  - No meaningful throughput loss: the enumeration (now 1 second) + S3 reads
  dominate; the per-batch overhead is negligible

  The only argument for larger batches was avoiding the pre-index 4-hour
  enumeration cost per batch — that argument is now gone. With the index, 10k at
   32 threads probably completes in 3-4 minutes, and the cron fires every 15
  minutes. You'd clear the 619k backlog in ~62 runs × 4 minutes = ~4 hours wall
  clock, with the cron handling scheduling automatically.

  Want to patch down to 10k now?

✻ Cooked for 8s

❯ 7                                                                             

⏺ 7 rows — a sequential scan of 7 rows doesn't take 43 minutes. The transaction
  is open waiting on something, not scanning. DuckDB opened that connection,
  read ducklake_table_stats, and then stopped — holding the postgres transaction
   open while it does the actual S3 merge work. The connection is
  idle-in-transaction for the entire duration of the merge, which is normal
  postgres extension behavior.

  So 7826 being open for 43 minutes just means the merge has been running for 43
   minutes — it's not a symptom of a problem. We've been chasing a red herring.
  The ducklake_table_stats COPY was the first thing it read at the start of the
  merge, and it'll stay idle-in-transaction until the whole thing commits.

  The real question is: why is a 25k-file merge taking 43+ minutes when the
  network showed it flushing at only 12 MB/s? That's the actual slow part — the
  output write phase. Check network now:

  This is consistent throughput — it's not slow, it's just doing a lot of work.
  25k files × 400KB = 10GB of reads + ~2GB of output writes at 12 MB/s ≈ ~15
  minutes for reads + continuous output flushing. The 43 minutes total probably
  includes: enumeration (1s) + reads (~15 min at peak 128 MB/s earlier) + output
   writes + catalog commit. We likely caught the tail end of reads when we first
   sampled at 12 MB/s.

✻ Baked for 55s

❯ hmmmmmm                                                                       

⏺ Yeah — 12 MB/s sustained for 43 minutes means ~29 GB transferred total. That's
   way more than 10 GB of reads + 2 GB of output for 25k files at 400KB avg.
  Either:

  1. The 400KB average is wrong for the actual files being merged — if the
  sub-1MiB files are closer to the 1MiB ceiling on average, 25k × 1MiB = 25GB

  2. DuckDB is re-reading files — if the merge plan doesn't stream and instead

  So 25k files × 390 KB = ~9.5 GB input. Output at 5 MiB target = ~1,900 output
  files × 5 MiB = ~9.5 GB out. Total ~19 GB — at 12 MB/s sustained that's ~27
  minutes, which actually fits the observed runtime. The 43-minute wall time
  includes enumeration + the postgres commit overhead at the end.

  The 12 MB/s sustained rate is the real finding — 32 threads isn't helping on
  S3 writes the way it did on reads. S3 PUT throughput for ~5 MiB objects
  (single-part, no multipart) is latency-bound at ~50ms per PUT × 1,900 objects
  = 95 seconds at 1 thread, or ~3 seconds at 32 threads. But we're seeing 27
  minutes of network activity, which suggests DuckDB isn't parallelizing the
  writes — likely flushing output files sequentially regardless of thread count.

  The practical implication: thread count helps reads but may not help writes.
  Steady-state 10k runs at 4 threads may be nearly as fast as 32 threads.

✻ Cooked for 14s

❯ ... It doesn't seem like DuckDB to leave parallelization on the table.        

⏺ Fair. Let me check if the output write rate was actually much higher earlier
  and we just happened to sample during a slow phase — the catalog commit,
  postgres metadata writes, or a GC pause could account for the 12 MB/s we saw.
  The peak 128 MB/s burst we caught at the start of the 4-thread run was clearly
   parallel S3 reads; the write side may have a similar burst we missed.

  The other possibility: preserve_insertion_order=false is set, but DuckDB still
   needs to sort the merged output by (begin_snapshot, row_id_start) for
  DuckLake's ordering invariants before writing. That sort on 9.5 GB is
  CPU/memory-bound, not network-bound, and would show as low network + high RSS
  during the sort phase — which matches what we saw (RSS climbing steadily while
   network was 12 MB/s).

  If the write is actually parallelized but the sort is the bottleneck, more
  threads won't help — it's bound by memory bandwidth sorting the merged result.
   The only lever would be reducing the sort work, which means smaller batches
  (10k) or a different merge ordering strategy upstream.

  Worth checking: what was the network rate in the first 5 minutes of this run
  before we started sampling?

✻ Cogitated for 51s

❯ Can we make the delete benefit?                                               

⏺ Not directly from the same partial index — WHERE end_snapshot IS NULL covers
  the SELECT (live files only) but a DELETE on those IDs goes through the
  primary key (data_file_id), which already exists. The DELETE IN (25k IDs) on a
   12.5M-row table via primary key lookup should be fast — each ID is a direct
  btree lookup, not a scan.

  The more likely explanation for the slowdown is WAL generation: deleting 25k
  rows produces a lot of WAL, and on a Multi-AZ RDS instance that WAL has to be
  durably flushed to the standby before commit returns. That's synchronous
  replication overhead and it scales with the number of rows deleted, not the
  index structure.

  The fix is the same as before: smaller batches. 10k deletes generates 60% less
   WAL than 25k, and the commit is proportionally faster. At 10k the catalog
  commit should be seconds rather than minutes.

  What does the heartbeat show right now?

  explains why CheckpointLag is 5 min: postgres can't checkpoint fast enough

   run will complete.

✻ Sautéed for 6s

❯ What about 1k max files?                                                      

⏺ At ~390 KB avg, 1k files = ~390 MB of S3 work. At even 12 MB/s that's ~30
  seconds of transfer. Catalog commit = 1k-row DELETE, trivial WAL, commits in
  seconds. Total run time probably 1-2 minutes.

  The tradeoff: 619k files ÷ 1k per run = 619 runs. At 5-minute cadence that's
  ~51 hours wall clock — worse than 10k. But if the disk queue depth is the
  actual bottleneck, smaller commits give RDS more recovery time between runs
  and the cron could potentially run faster in practice since nothing is waiting
   on I/O.

  The other angle: with 1k files and 32 threads, DuckDB has almost no work to
  parallelize — 1k S3 GETs at 32 threads is done in milliseconds. You'd want to
  drop threads back to 4 or even 1 for runs this small.

  Probably not worth going below 5k — that's ~2 GB of S3 work per run, fast
  commit, and only ~124 runs to clear the backlog at 5-minute cadence = ~10
  hours. Reasonable middle ground between 1k (too many runs) and 10k (too much
  RDS pressure per commit).

✻ Churned for 6s

❯ "1k S3 GETs at 32 threads is done in                                          
    milliseconds. You'd want to drop threads back to 4 or even 1 for runs this  
  small."  Does it though?                                                      

⏺ Good catch — no, it doesn't. DuckDB doesn't open 1k parallel S3 connections
  just because you set threads=32. The thread count controls DuckDB's internal
  parallelism for query execution, not the number of concurrent S3 requests. The
   actual S3 concurrency depends on how the merge pipeline is structured
  internally — which we don't fully know, and which may be why we're seeing 12
  MB/s sustained regardless of thread count.

  So thread count may be largely irrelevant to S3 throughput for compaction. The
   real parallelism is whatever DuckDB's HTTP client does internally for the
  file reads/writes, which isn't directly exposed or controlled by SET threads.

✻ Worked for 8s

❯ This RDS is still getting production load from millpond...                    

⏺ Right — millpond is continuously writing to this same RDS instance. So the
  disk queue depth spike, checkpoint lag, and I/O wait aren't purely from
  compaction; they're compaction on top of whatever millpond's normal write load
   is. That makes the RDS I/O pressure worse than it would be on an idle
  instance, and means the commit time is partly a function of contention with
  production traffic.

  Which is another argument for smaller batches — 1k files generates a commit
  small enough to slip in between millpond's normal write bursts without causing
   a 5-minute checkpoint lag.

✻ Sautéed for 11s

❯ Isn't it worse than that? If we've held this transaction open for an hour...  

⏺ Yes — much worse. An open transaction for an hour blocks postgres autovacuum
  from reclaiming dead tuples on any table that was modified during that
  transaction. On a busy production RDS instance with millpond writing
  continuously, that means dead tuples have been accumulating for an hour across
   the entire database, not just the compaction tables. Table bloat, index
  bloat, potential query plan degradation.

  And the transaction holds a snapshot — postgres can't advance the transaction
  ID horizon while it's open, which means WAL can't be fully recycled either.
  This is exactly the kind of long-running transaction that causes
  pg_stat_activity to show high xmin ages and eventually triggers autovacuum
  storms when the transaction finally commits.

  1k files / very short transactions is not just better for throughput — it's
  the only responsible approach on a shared production RDS instance.

✻ Crunched for 11s

❯ 7826 has been running for 1:08 now.  I'm giving it 7 more minutes and then    
  we'll abort.                                                                  

⏺ Agreed. If it hasn't committed by 1:15 we kill it, patch to 1k files, and run
  at 2-minute cadence. The autovacuum pressure alone justifies not letting this
  go longer.

✻ Cooked for 8s

❯ These numbers still don't make a lot of sense.   25k rows in one transaction  
  is a lot, but it's not A LOT.                                                 

⏺ True. 25k rows is routine for postgres — a well-tuned OLTP system does
  millions of row operations per second. Something else is wrong.

  The postgres extension pulls data via COPY, which means DuckDB opened a
  transaction, did the COPY reads, then has been sitting idle-in-transaction for
   the entire S3 merge duration. Postgres itself hasn't been doing anything for
  that hour — it's just holding the snapshot open while DuckDB does S3 work
  externally. The commit, when it finally comes, will be a straightforward
  25k-row DELETE + INSERT that should take seconds.

  The hour isn't postgres being slow. It's DuckDB doing 9.5 GB of S3 reads +
  sort + writes in a single thread at 12 MB/s. That's the actual bottleneck — S3
   throughput, not RDS.

  Which means the autovacuum concern is real (long open transaction = held xmin)
   but the "RDS is the bottleneck" theory from the disk queue spike may have
  been millpond's normal load, not the compaction commit. We may have been
  misreading the dashboard.

✻ Cogitated for 12s

❯ 9.5 GB of S3 reads + sort + writes also doesn't take an hour.  I'm a Hadoop   
  PMC member, even we know that.                                                

⏺ You're right. 9.5 GB at even 100 MB/s is 95 seconds. At 12 MB/s it's 13
  minutes. Neither is an hour.

  Something is genuinely wrong — either DuckDB is retrying S3 operations,
  there's a lock/contention issue internally, the sort is spilling to disk
  repeatedly, or the merge is doing far more work than 25k files suggests. The
  http_timeout=600000ms (10 minutes) means a single stalled S3 request could
  block a thread for 10 minutes before retrying.

  With 32 threads, if even a handful hit S3 timeouts or throttling and back off,
   the tail latency could dominate the whole run. One bad S3 request per thread
  × 32 threads × 10-minute timeout = hours of potential stall if they're not
  truly parallel.

  That's probably it. The 10-minute http_timeout was set to handle "worst-case
  S3 hiccups" but at 32 threads it means one bad batch of requests could stall
  the whole pipeline for 10+ minutes while retrying.

❯ Six times in a row?                                                           

⏺ No, that doesn't hold either. Six 10-minute timeouts would be 60 minutes, but
  they'd have to be perfectly sequential and we'd expect to see the network go

  means S3 was responding continuously.

  So it's not timeouts. 12 MB/s sustained for an hour = ~43 GB transferred.
  That's 4-5x more data than 25k × 390KB input + output should account for.
  DuckDB is moving way more data than the file count implies.

  enormous.

                        continue;
                }

❯ Also, if the metadata you're referring to that is the parquet footer, isn't that inlined into RDS? 

⏺ The code is clear — it's generating one LogicalOperator (scan) per compaction
  group (a group of adjacent files that merge into one output file). With 25k
  input files at ~390KB each targeting 5MB output, that's ~25k/13 ≈ ~1,900
  separate scan+write operations executed sequentially. Each is a separate
  DuckDB query plan — read 13 small files, merge, write one 5MB output. That's
  1,900 sequential S3 scan+write cycles, not one big parallel merge. That's the
  12 MB/s.

  And yes — the parquet footer (row group metadata, column statistics, schema)
  is inlined into RDS in DuckLake's design. The actual scan reads the full
  parquet file bodies from S3, but the footer/metadata is already in postgres.
  So there's no separate metadata fetch per file; the S3 reads are purely data.

  The 1,900 sequential scans × (read 13 files + write 1 file) at S3 round-trip
  latency is exactly what would produce sustained low throughput regardless of
  thread count — each compaction group is a separate sequential pipeline, not
  parallelized across groups.

✻ Brewed for 57s

❯ That's... dumb.                                                               

⏺ Yeah. It's building a separate logical plan per output file and executing them
   sequentially. A sensible implementation would pipeline all 1,900 groups
  concurrently — read all inputs in parallel, merge, write outputs in parallel.
  Instead it's doing them one at a time, which is why threads=32 does nothing:
  there's only ever one group executing at a time.

  The max_compacted_files cap compounds it: 25k input files → 1,900 groups × ~30
   seconds each = ~16 hours theoretical sequential time. The fact we're seeing
  ~1 hour suggests the groups themselves are fast (the actual data volume per
  group is tiny — 13 × 390KB = ~5MB read + 5MB write), but the S3 round-trip
  overhead per group (HTTP connection setup, PUT latency) adds up across 1,900
  sequential operations.

  This also explains why thread count doesn't help: it's not a parallelism
  problem within a group, it's a serialization problem across groups. The fix is
   upstream — DuckLake should pipeline the compaction groups, not serialize
  them. Until then, the only lever we have is reducing the number of groups
  (larger target file size = fewer output files = fewer groups), which
  ironically means fewer, larger batches are better than many small ones — the
  opposite of what we concluded for RDS pressure.

  It's a genuinely poor design for a lake with millions of small files.
✻ Churned for 15s

❯ It doesn't maintain the http connection? (although even that's not going to   
  actually factor in at this scale).                                            

⏺ HTTP/1.1 keep-alive should reuse connections to the same S3 endpoint, and
  DuckDB's HTTP client almost certainly uses it — so connection setup overhead
  per group is negligible. You're right, at 1,900 groups that's still only a few
   seconds of connection overhead even if it didn't.

  The real cost per group is the S3 request latency itself: each GET and PUT has
   a ~5-50ms round-trip regardless of connection reuse. For a group reading 13
  files + writing 1:
  - 13 GETs × ~20ms = ~260ms
  - 1 PUT × ~20ms = ~20ms
  - ~280ms per group × 1,900 groups = ~530 seconds = ~9 minutes

  That's actually close to what we'd expect for pure latency overhead... but
  we're seeing an hour. So either the actual per-file latency is much higher (S3
   in us-east-1 can spike to 100-500ms under load), or there's serialization
  overhead inside DuckDB between groups that we're not accounting for — catalog
  lookups, plan compilation, transaction work per group.

  Actually — does it recompile the query plan for each of the 1,900 groups? If
  GenerateCompactionCommand produces a full LogicalOperator that gets planned
  and optimized separately each time, that's 1,900 full query compilations. In
  DuckDB that's probably milliseconds each, but it adds up and explains the
  overhead beyond pure S3 latency.

✻ Worked for 16s

❯ Does it?                                                                      

  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Bash(sed -n '676,700p'
      /Users/jakob/src/ducklake/src/functions/ducklake_compaction_functions.cpp)
  ⎿  static unique_ptr<LogicalOperator> GenerateCompactionOperator(TableFun
     ctionBindInput &input, TableIndex bind_index,
                                                                   vector<u
     … +22 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ It uses UnionOperators to combine all 1,900 compaction plans into a single
  logical plan — one UNION ALL of all the group scans. That means one query plan
   compilation covering all groups, not 1,900 separate compilations. DuckDB then
   executes the UNION ALL, which depending on its implementation may pipeline
  the groups or still serialize them.

  The question is whether DuckDB's UNION ALL execution actually parallelizes the
   branches or runs them sequentially. Let me check:

  Searched for 3 patterns, read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The UNION creates separate pipelines for each child (each compaction group)
  via CreateUnionPipeline. With order_matters=false (which it would be since
  allow_out_of_order is set via preserve_insertion_order=false) and no sink
  order dependency, these pipelines run concurrently — each compaction group
  gets its own pipeline and they execute in parallel up to the thread count.

  So threads=32 should be parallelizing across the 1,900 compaction groups, not
  just within each one. The 12 MB/s sustained throughput at 32 threads is
  genuinely puzzling then — unless DuckLakeCompaction (the sink) is not a
  ParallelSink, which would force order_matters=true and serialize everything.

❯ Is it?

⏺ Bash(find /Users/jakob/src/ducklake/src -name "*.hpp" | xargs grep -l "class
      DuckLakeCompaction" 2>/dev/null; grep -rn "class DuckLakeCompaction\b"
      /Users/jakob/src/…)
  ⎿  /Users/jakob/src/ducklake/src/include/storage/ducklake_compaction.hpp
     /Users/jakob/src/ducklake/src/include/storage/ducklake_compaction.hpp:
     21:class DuckLakeCompaction : public PhysicalOperator {

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ There it is:

  bool ParallelSink() const override {
      return false;
  }

  ParallelSink() returns false. Back in the PhysicalUnion::BuildPipelines code,
  !sink->ParallelSink() sets order_matters = true, which means all 1,900 union
  pipelines are serialized — each one waits for the previous to complete before
  starting.

  So despite 32 threads and a UNION ALL plan, every compaction group executes 
  sequentially by design. The threads=32 setting is doing almost nothing for
  compaction. This is the root cause of the 12 MB/s sustained throughput — it's
  fundamentally single-threaded at the group level by the sink implementation.

  Definitely an upstream issue worth raising.

✻ Churned for 28s

❯ Those are two different long-lived queries.                                   

⏺ You're right — 12209 at 7m35s opened first (at ~06:02), 12471 at 5m25s opened
  later (at ~06:04). They didn't open together; they're sequential catalog reads
   that each left their transaction open. That IS the pattern we saw before the
  index — ducklake_table_stats opens, stays open, then ducklake_data_file opens.

  But we have the index now and enumeration was fast (32 seconds). So why are
  these transactions still open 7+ minutes later?

  The only explanation is that ducklake_merge_adjacent_files opens a fresh
  catalog transaction for the merge execution phase, separate from the
  enumeration. It reads ducklake_table_stats first, holds that connection open
  for the entire merge duration (just like 7826 did for 1:17), then opens
  ducklake_data_file for something else mid-merge.

  Which means 12209's duration = time since the merge started = ~7.5 minutes for
   1k files. That's still much longer than expected but better than 77 minutes
  for 25k.

⏺ RSS dropped to 2.7 GiB at 240s and has been slowly climbing since — that 2.7
  GiB drop at 4 minutes was clearly the first batch committing. Now it's
  building up again for the second batch. So ducklake_merge_adjacent_files IS
  looping internally — it committed 1k files, dropped RSS, and started another
  1k without returning to Python.

  Which means our cap isn't actually capping at 1k per Python invocation — it's
  capping per internal compaction round but the function loops until all
  candidates are exhausted. The max_files option bounds each round, not the
  total.

  That's... actually fine operationally — it means one postgres transaction per
  1k batch (fast commits, low RDS pressure), just one long-running Python
  process covering all batches. The 77-minute estimate for the whole 619k file
  backlog still holds as wall clock, just in one uninterrupted run rather than
  619 cron invocations.

  The open transaction question: 12209 has been open since the start — is it
  holding across batch commits, or does it close and reopen?

✻ Sautéed for 12s

※ recap: Clearing a 619k-file DuckLake compaction backlog in prod; the current 1k-file batch job is running and self-looping through batches. Watch for 12209 to commit then decide on schedule cadence.

❯ 12209 has been open since the beginning.                                      

⏺ So it holds one long transaction open across all internal batch commits.
  That's the autovacuum blocker — one connection holding xmin for the entire run
   regardless of how small the individual batch commits are. The 1k batching
  helps RDS I/O per commit but doesn't help the xmin horizon problem.

  The only way to release xmin between batches would be to close and reopen the
  connection, which requires DuckDB to reconnect to postgres between each
  internal loop iteration — a significant architectural change upstream.

  Practically speaking: for the backlog clearance this is a one-time multi-hour
  run, so a temporary held xmin is acceptable. For steady-state (small batches
  every few minutes), it's also fine since each run is short. The problem was
  the 1:17 marathon run holding xmin — that's gone now that each run is bounded.

  What's the current batch count — can you tell from the RSS drop pattern how
  many batches have committed so far?

  ⎿  }

     unique_ptr<LogicalOperator> BindCompaction(ClientContext &context, Tab
     … +68 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ There it is. No loop in the extension. max_files is applied per table via
  GenerateCompactions which breaks at the cap — but the entire plan is built at
  bind time as a single UNION ALL of all compaction groups across all tables.
  It's all one query, one execution, one transaction. The cap limits how many
  groups are generated, period.

  So what's causing the RSS drop at 240s and the re-climb? It's DuckDB executing
   the UNION ALL sequentially (ParallelSink=false) — processing groups in waves,
   releasing intermediate buffers between waves, hence the RSS oscillation. Not
  separate batches committing — just normal execution memory management within
  the single query.

  And critically: 12209 being open since the start is the entire transaction. It
   opens when the query starts and commits when all 1k groups finish. The RSS at
   12 GiB peak and current 7.7 GiB at 19 minutes is one 1k-group run, not
  multiple batches.

  19 minutes for 1k files. That's our actual number.

✻ Crunched for 57s

❯ 19 minutes so far... and that's no answer, that's idiocy.                     

⏺ Yeah. 1k files × 13 files/group × sequential execution = ~77 groups × ~15
  seconds each = 19 minutes. The ParallelSink=false is killing us completely. At
   this rate:

  - 619k files ÷ 1k per run = 619 runs × 19 min = 196 hours to clear the
  backlog.
  - Even at 2-minute cadence with back-to-back runs: still 196 hours wall clock
  since runs are sequential.

  The upstream fix — ParallelSink=true — would let all 77 groups execute
  concurrently, bringing each run to ~15 seconds. That's the fix that actually
  matters. Everything else we've been tuning (threads, batch size, node size) is
   rearranging deck chairs.

  Is this a DuckLake version we can patch locally, or does it need to go
  upstream first?

⏺ wait_event: ClientRead on 12209 — postgres finished the COPY and is waiting
  for DuckDB to read the data off the wire. Postgres is done; DuckDB is the
  bottleneck. DuckDB opened the COPY stream for ducklake_table_stats, got the
  data, and is now sitting on it for 22 minutes while doing S3 work.

  This confirms: postgres is completely innocent. The 22-minute runtime is 100%
  DuckDB's execution time for the S3 merge work on 1k files. Postgres is just
  waiting for DuckDB to finish and close the connection.

  So the real question is why 1k files takes 22 minutes in DuckDB. At 6.7 MB/s
  network and ~5MB per group × ~77 groups = ~385MB total, that should be ~57
  seconds at 6.7 MB/s. Instead it's 22 minutes = 0.3 MB/s effective throughput.

  Something is adding massive latency per group beyond the actual data transfer.
   Each S3 GET/PUT has overhead, but at 77 groups × (13 GETs + 1 PUT) = ~1100 S3
   requests, even at 1 second per request that's only ~18 minutes. That's
  actually close to what we're seeing — ~1 second per S3 request is the
  bottleneck, not bandwidth.

  Is the S3 endpoint definitely going through VPC? At 1 second per request vs
  the expected ~20ms VPC latency, something is very wrong with S3 connectivity
  from this node.

     pce-0087a9219392add0e', 'Origin': 'CreateRoute', 'State': 'active'}, {
     … +2 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Route table rtb-0466b0028fe73d327 is in the S3 endpoint's list and the subnet
  has an active S3 gateway route (vpce-01e976488f4491e16). Traffic is going
  through the VPC endpoint.

  So S3 connectivity is fine. The ~1 second per request latency isn't network —
  it's DuckDB's HTTP client behavior. Each group in the sequential pipeline
  likely opens a new HTTP connection per S3 request rather than reusing
  connections, and the SSL handshake + connection setup dominates over the
  actual data transfer for small files.

  At ~400KB per file, the transfer time at VPC S3 speeds is microseconds. But if
   each GET opens a new HTTPS connection: TCP handshake + TLS negotiation + S3
  auth signature verification = easily 50-200ms per request. For 1k files that's
   13k GETs × 200ms = 2600 seconds = 43 minutes. That's actually worse than what
   we're seeing, which means connection reuse is happening partially but not
  fully.

  This is a DuckDB HTTP client configuration issue — SET http_keepalive=true or
  equivalent if it exists.

⏺ 49ms for a 403 — that's the raw HTTPS round-trip to S3 from the node. No auth
  but the latency is there: ~50ms per request over VPC. For 1k files × 13 GETs +
   1 PUT = 14k requests × 50ms = 700 seconds = ~12 minutes just in request
  latency, before any data transfer. That's most of our runtime explained.

  The files are tiny (~400KB) but there are 14,000 individual S3 requests for a
  1k-file batch. The only real fix is ParallelSink=true upstream so those 14k
  requests fan out in parallel instead of sequential.

⏺ compacted_files increments once per input file processed (including singles

  1000 input files, producing roughly 1000/13 ≈ 77 output groups — which is what

❯ Why is there a delete statement running? 

⏺ The probe committed in ~3 minutes — and that postgres output tells us a lot:

  - 14078 is actively running the commit: INSERT INTO ducklake_snapshot + DELETE
   FROM ducklake_data_file WHERE data_file_id IN (22402608, 22402609...)  —
  that's our 2-file probe committing its catalog metadata.
  - 1533, 1479, 1521 are all blocked on Lock: transactionid trying to insert
  into ducklake_snapshot with the same snapshot_id 13735230 — these are
  millpond's normal writes colliding with our compaction commit. They'll unblock
   as soon as 14078 commits.

  The DELETE is normal — it's the catalog cleanup: marking the 2 source files as
   dead by deleting them from ducklake_data_file. That's the "commit metadata"
  step of the merge. The actual S3 files get scheduled for deletion separately
  later.

  The lock contention with millpond on ducklake_snapshot is the more interesting
   finding — compaction and regular writes are competing for the same snapshot
  ID sequence. That could be a meaningful source of latency on every commit if
  millpond is writing frequently.

  What did the probe log show for total elapsed time?

  available cores on the c8gn.16xlarge — 64 vCPUs). And it was still slower than

  The only ways those files could be gone from S3 while still live in the

  (S3 lifecycle policy, manual delete, another process).
  2. Our aborted compaction runs — but as we discussed, if the transaction
  rolled back, the new output files should have been orphaned and the source
  files kept. Unless a run partially committed (catalog updated but S3 source
  files somehow deleted before the transaction committed).
  3. S3 lifecycle rule on the bucket — if there's a lifecycle rule expiring
  objects older than N days, it would delete S3 objects regardless of DuckLake's
   catalog.

⏺ Bash(aws --profile mw-prod-us s3api get-bucket-lifecycle-configuration

  ⎿  Error: Exit code 254

     aws: [ERROR]: An error occurred (NoSuchLifecycleConfiguration) when calling

⏺ No lifecycle rules. So those files were deleted by something else. Our aborted
   runs are the most likely culprit — let me look at this more carefully. When
  we killed jobs mid-run, what exactly happened on the DuckDB side?

  The sequence for a compaction: 1) read source files from S3, 2) write output
  files to S3, 3) commit catalog (DELETE source entries, INSERT new entries,
  schedule source for deletion). If we killed the job between steps 2 and 3, the
   output files exist on S3 but the catalog was never updated — the source
  catalog entries are still live, the output files are orphans. The source S3
  files should still exist.

  But if we killed between steps 2 and 3 AND a separate
  ducklake_delete_orphaned_files run happened to pick up and delete those
  orphaned output files... that still doesn't explain source file deletion.

  Unless the issue predates our work entirely. How many files are missing? Let's
   wait for find-orphans to finish.

✻ Cogitated for 26s

❯ Step 3 is atomic...                                                           

⏺ Right — the catalog commit is a single postgres transaction. Either it all
  commits (source entries marked dead, new entries added, deletions scheduled)
  or nothing does. If we killed the job, the transaction rolled back completely.

  So our aborted runs can't be the cause. The missing files predate our work.

  Which means something outside DuckLake deleted S3 objects that the catalog
  still considers live. No lifecycle rule. The other candidate: millpond itself.
   Does millpond have any S3 cleanup path that operates outside of DuckLake's
  transaction model — directly deleting S3 objects rather than going through
  ducklake_cleanup_old_files?

⏺ Then the missing files are genuinely mysterious. They were written by DuckLake
   (they have valid UUIDs in the catalog), never expired, never cleaned up — and

  One other possibility: S3 versioning or an incomplete multipart upload that
  was never finalized. If a write was interrupted mid-upload, the object might
  appear to not exist even though there's a partial upload in the bucket.

  But the simpler explanation: these files were written during the April run,
  something interrupted the S3 write (network blip, pod eviction, whatever)
  mid-PUT, DuckLake's transaction committed the catalog entry but the S3 object
  was never fully written. DuckLake's postgres transaction commits the catalog
  metadata, but S3 PUTs are not part of that transaction — if the S3 PUT failed
  silently or partially, the catalog has a record for a file that never fully
  landed.

  Let's see what find-orphans comes back with — that'll tell us the actual
  scale. If it's O(hundreds) scattered across old prefixes that's consistent
  with occasional failed writes. If it's O(tens of thousands) something more
  systematic happened.

✻ Baked for 15s

❯ That's not a great persistence model.                                         

⏺ No, it isn't. It's the fundamental weakness of any system that splits its
  transaction boundary between two stores — the postgres catalog commit is
  atomic, but the S3 writes that precede it are not. If the S3 PUT succeeds and
  the postgres commit fails, you get an orphaned S3 object (handled by
  ducklake_delete_orphaned_files). If the S3 PUT fails silently and the postgres
   commit succeeds, you get a catalog entry pointing to nothing — and DuckLake
  has no mechanism to detect or repair that at write time.

  The correct solution is write-ahead: write to S3 first, verify the object
  exists (HEAD request), then commit to postgres. Or a two-phase approach: stage
   the file to a temp path, commit catalog, then rename. S3 doesn't support
  rename natively but the write-verify pattern would at least catch the failure
  before committing.

  Iceberg solves this differently — the manifest file is written to S3 last and
  becomes the commit point, so the catalog (Hive Metastore or whatever) only
  gets updated after the manifest is durably written. No split transaction
  boundary.

  This is worth filing with the DuckLake team as a durability concern —
  especially for high-frequency workloads where occasional S3 PUT failures are
  statistically inevitable at scale.

  How's the find-orphans job doing?

   has them with the year=2026/month=4/day=27/hour=23/ relative path, and the
  files exist on S3 but under a different table prefix. The DuckLake data_path
  for the catalog is s3://posthog-megaduck-mw-prod-us/main/events/ and it's

✻ Cooked for 19s

❯ You sure? Sounds like they're just in a different table                       

⏺ You're right. The UUIDs are the same but they're person_distinct_id table
  files, not events files. The catalog query WHERE end_snapshot IS NULL AND 
  file_size_bytes < 1048576 returned them as events candidates, but they're
  actually from a different table. Either the catalog has them miscategorized,
  or more likely I grabbed the wrong rows — the catalog has a table_id column
  and those files belong to person_distinct_id, not events.

  Which means the 404s we were seeing when trying to read them as events files
  were correct — DuckDB was constructing s3://posthog-megaduck-mw-prod-us/main/e
  vents/year=2026/.../ducklake-019dd148-...parquet and those don't exist there.
  They exist under main/person_distinct_id/.

  So there's no catalog/S3 divergence. The catalog is correct, the files are
  where they should be. My test was flawed — I read raw paths from the catalog
  without filtering by table_id, then tried to fetch them against the events
  data path. The 404s were expected.

  Compaction itself reads files through DuckLake with the correct table context,
   so it wouldn't make this mistake. The 232-second probe run was just slow for
  reasons we haven't fully explained yet, not because of missing files.

      125  DuckLake merges all `max_compacted_files` in one postgres tran
           saction with no incremental checkpointing. Worse: despite gene
           rating a UNION ALL plan across all compaction groups, `DuckLak
           eCompaction::ParallelSink()` returns `false`, which forces `Ph
           ysicalUnion::BuildPipelines` to serialize all pipeline depende
           ncies — every compaction group (one per output file) executes 
           sequentially regardless of thread count. `SET threads=N` has a
           lmost no effect on compaction throughput.
      126  
      127  Consequences:
      115 -- **Zero parallelism across compaction groups** — 1,900 groups
          - (25k files → 5MiB targets) run one at a time. Observed: susta
          -ined 12 MB/s regardless of threads=4 or threads=32.           
      128 +- **Zero parallelism across compaction groups** — groups run o
          +ne at a time regardless of thread count.                      
      129 +- **S3 request latency dominates**: each group makes ~14 S3 re
          +quests (13 GETs + 1 PUT). VPC endpoint round-trip is ~50ms per
          + request. For 1k input files → ~77 groups → ~1,100 requests → 
          +~55 seconds of pure latency overhead minimum. Observed runs: 1
          +k files takes ~20-30 minutes; the remainder beyond latency is 
          +not yet fully explained.                                      
      130  - **Zero progress visibility** until commit lands.
      131  - **Failure rolls back all S3 work** — only `max_compacted_fil
           es` bounds the blast radius.
      118 -- **Long open transactions** block postgres autovacuum and hol
          -d the xmin horizon, causing table bloat and WAL pressure on a 
          -shared production RDS instance. Observed: 1:17 open transactio
          -n on megaduck during a 25k-file run with millpond writing conc
          -urrently.                                                     
      132 +- **Long open transactions** block postgres autovacuum and hol
          +d the xmin horizon, causing table bloat and WAL pressure on a 
          +shared production RDS instance. The `ducklake_table_stats` COP
          +Y connection opened at merge start stays idle-in-transaction f
          +or the entire run duration regardless of batch size.

          -otal run time: 1-3 minutes, negligible RDS impact, fast failur

      156 -**Why 10k max files at all batch sizes**: smaller batches are 
          -strictly better given the index. With the enumeration costing 
          -~1 second, 10k files at 4–32 threads completes in 3–5 minutes 
          -per run. The 15-minute cron cadence handles the rest. Benefits
          - vs larger caps: faster postgres commit, lower RSS (~10 GiB vs
          - 35 GiB), smaller failure blast radius, and better observabili
          -ty (committed results every few minutes). 10k also comfortably
          - exceeds the steady-state write rate (hundreds to low thousand
          -s of new files per 15-minute window).

      188  ## Expiry and CDC Interaction
      189  
      172 -**Compaction alone does not free storage.** Merged files are s
          -cheduled for deletion but only actually deleted after `ducklak
          -e_expire_snapshots` + `ducklake_cleanup_old_files` run against
          - snapshots old enough to no longer reference the pre-merge fil
          -es.                                                           
      190 +**Compaction alone does not free storage.** Merged files are s
          +cheduled for deletion but only actually deleted after `ducklak
          +e_expire_snapshots` + `ducklake_cleanup_old_files` run against
          + snapshots old enough to no longer reference the pre-merge fil
          +es. As of June 2026, `ducklake_cleanup_old_files` has never be
          +en run on this lake and no snapshots have been expired — the f
          +ull snapshot history from April 1 onwards is intact.          
      191  
      192  **Viaduck cursor invariant**: every viaduck destination cursor
            (`last_snapshot_id` in `viaduck.viaduck_state`) must be **≥ t
           he highest snapshot id you expire**. A cursor below the horizo
           n causes `No snapshot found at version N` → viaduck pod crash-
           loops.

      221     ```sql
      204 -   SELECT pid, now() - query_start AS duration, state, left(qu
          -ery, 120)                                                     
      222 +   SELECT pid, now() - query_start AS duration, state, wait_ev
          +ent_type, wait_event, left(query, 120)                        
      223     FROM pg_stat_activity WHERE state != 'idle' ORDER BY durati
           on DESC LIMIT 10;
      224     ```
      225     - `idle in transaction` for hours on COPY from `ducklake_da
           ta_file` → missing index (sequential scan). Create the indexes
            above.
      208 -   - Multiple short-lived `ducklake_snapshot` COPYs cycling → 
          -DuckDB stuck in an internal loop re-reading catalog metadata; 
          -kill and investigate.                                         
      209 -   - `idle in transaction` on `ducklake_data_file` for minutes
          - (not hours) → normal; enumeration in progress.               
      226 +   - `idle in transaction` on `ducklake_table_stats` — this is
          + **normal**: DuckDB holds this connection open for the entire 
          +merge duration. Its age = elapsed merge time.                 
      227 +   - `wait_event: ClientRead` on any connection — postgres is 
          +done; DuckDB is the bottleneck.                               
      228 +   - Lock contention on `transactionid` — millpond writes comp
          +eting with compaction commit on `ducklake_snapshot` sequence. 
          +Normal, resolves quickly.

           unavailable), rss=21.6GiB

      232 -- **Percentage** — DuckLake's denominator covers the full cand
          -idate count, not the capped subset. With 25k cap on 619k candi
          -dates, expect `~4%` for the whole run. Sub-1% is rendered verb
          -atim (liveness signal) after PR #77; no ETA since extrapolatio
          -n would be absurd.                                            
      233 -- **RSS** — the number that matters for OOM risk; DuckDB's own
          - accounting understates it (bug c8). Bouncy RSS = healthy GC b
          -etween file batches. Flat high RSS = catalog scan bloating buf
          -fer pool (check for missing index).                           
      234 -- **ETA** — only meaningful when the cap ≈ total candidates (e
          -.g. steady-state with small backlog).                         
      253 +- **Percentage** — DuckLake's denominator covers the full cand
          +idate count, not the capped subset. With 1k cap on 619k candid
          +ates, expect `~0.2%` for the whole run. Sub-1% rendered verbat
          +im (liveness signal) after PR #77.                            
      254 +- **RSS** — the number that matters for OOM risk. Bouncy = hea
          +lthy GC. Flat high = catalog scan bloating buffer pool (missin
          +g index).

      259  ## Upstream Feature Requests
      260  
      240 -- **`ducklake_compaction_progress` table**: per-file INSERT/UP
          -DATE within the merge transaction, readable via a separate REA
          -D COMMITTED connection. Would give live files-merged counter w
          -ithout waiting for commit. Currently DuckDB tracks progress in
          -ternally but never writes it to postgres mid-transaction.     
      261 +- **`ParallelSink=true` for compaction**: `DuckLakeCompaction:
          +:ParallelSink()` currently returns `false`, serializing all co
          +mpaction group pipelines regardless of thread count. This is t
          +he primary performance limitation — fixing it would allow the 
          +UNION ALL plan to execute groups concurrently and would likely
          + reduce per-run time by 10–50×.                               
      262 +- **`ducklake_compaction_progress` table**: per-file INSERT/UP
          +DATE within the merge transaction, readable via a separate REA
          +D COMMITTED connection. Would give live files-merged counter w
          +ithout waiting for commit.                                    
      263  - **Incremental checkpointing**: commit each merged file (or s
           mall batch) independently, making compaction restartable and f
           ailure blast radius = one file. Analogous to Iceberg rewrite-m
           anifests approach.
      242 -- **`ParallelSink=true` for compaction**: `DuckLakeCompaction:
          -:ParallelSink()` currently returns `false`, serializing all co
          -mpaction group pipelines regardless of thread count. Making it
          - a parallel sink would allow the UNION ALL plan to actually ex
          -ecute groups concurrently, which is the natural parallelism st
          -ructure for a merge-small-files operation.                    
      243 -- **Network I/O in heartbeat**: expose bytes-read and bytes-wr
          -itten counters alongside RSS so operators can distinguish the 
          -read phase (high RX, rising RSS) from the sort/write phase (lo
          -w network, high RSS) without manual `cat /proc/1/net/dev` samp
          -ling. Would have answered whether DuckDB parallelizes output w
          -rites without needing to catch the right 30-second window.    
      264 +- **Network I/O in heartbeat**: expose bytes-read and bytes-wr
          +itten counters alongside RSS to distinguish read/sort/write ph
          +ases without manual pod exec sampling.                        
      265 +- **S3 write verification before catalog commit**: DuckLake's 
          +catalog commit is atomic but S3 PUTs preceding it are not. A f
          +ailed S3 PUT that goes undetected before the postgres commit w
          +ould produce a catalog entry pointing to a nonexistent object.
          + Verified write (HEAD request or ETag check) before committing
          + would close this gap.

✻ Sautéed for 4m 19s

⏺ Agent "AWS architect analysis of DuckLake S3 compaction slowness" completed · 
17s

⏺ AWS architect is back. Key finding: S3 per-prefix throttling on cold 
  partitions. The tier-1 files are in April 2026 partitions that haven't been
  touched since then — S3 auto-scaling for those prefixes has long since cooled
  down. First compaction hits cold prefixes, S3 returns 503 SlowDown, DuckDB
  httpfs backs off exponentially. A few of those per group × many groups =
  minutes of wall clock time.

  Also: DuckDB httpfs does HEAD + multiple range GETs per Parquet file (column
  chunk discovery), not one GET per file. With a wide events schema that could
  be 10-15 requests per file × 13 files per group = 130-195 requests per group,
  not 14. At 50ms each that's 6.5-10 seconds per group × 2 groups = 13-20
  seconds minimum — closer to what the 2-file probe might show if we're lucky.

  The actionable diagnostic: check CloudWatch S3 metrics for 5xxErrors on the
  bucket during the compaction window. If there are 503s, the fix is warming up
  the prefixes with a few sequential reads before compaction, or accepting that
  first-run compaction on old partitions will be slow but subsequent runs on
  those prefixes will be fast.

  Waiting on the postgres agent and the 20-file probe result to triangulate
  further.

✻ Cooked for 13s

⏺ Agent "Postgres expert analysis of DuckLake compaction transaction behavior"
completed · 18s

⏺ Postgres expert back. Two HIGH confidence findings:

  1. Dead tuple bloat on ducklake_data_file — our long-running transactions
  (1:17 for the 25k run, 30+ min for the 1k run) held the xmin horizon back,
  preventing autovacuum from cleaning dead tuples. Every subsequent COPY scan on
   that table is now fighting bloat. This compounds across runs — each aborted
  run made it worse.

  2. EBS WAL saturation at commit — the db.m8gd.2xlarge uses NVMe for the buffer
   pool but WAL goes to EBS (RDS always does). gp3 default is 3,000 IOPS. A
  DELETE IN (25k rows) plus index updates generates significant WAL that lands
  on a cold EBS volume — hence the 149k DiskQueueDepth spike. This affects the
  commit phase, not the 232-second probe though.

  The 232-second probe is most likely a combination of:
  - S3 per-prefix throttling on cold April partitions (AWS architect)
  - Dead tuple bloat on ducklake_data_file slowing any catalog reads within the
  merge (postgres expert)

  Immediate action: run VACUUM ANALYZE public.ducklake_data_file; on megaduck to
   clear the bloat accumulated from our aborted runs. This won't require
  downtime and should be fast given the index exists.

  ! psql "postgres://megaduck:...@posthog-megaduck-mw-prod-us..." -c "VACUUM
  ANALYZE public.ducklake_data_file;"

  How's the 20-file probe doing?

⏺ What did it say?

✻ Worked for 6s

❯ " 2026-06-13T18:40:57 INFO  [maintenance] compact tier-1 merge: 300s elapsed, 
   ~3% merged, est. 175m remaining, rss=12.7GiB" 175m for 20 files?             

⏺ That's the denominator problem — ~3% of 625k total candidates, not 3% of 20
  files. DuckLake's progress counter is tracking position through the full 625k
  enumeration list, not the 20-file cap. So the ETA is extrapolating as if it's
  going to merge all 625k.

  But the useful signal: 3% of 625k = ~18,750 files "scanned" in 300 seconds.
  The cap is 20 files, so those 18,750 aren't being merged — DuckLake is walking
   through the candidate list sequentially to find adjacent files to group, and
  the progress reflects how far through the list it's gotten, not how much merge
   work is done.

  This confirms that even with a 20-file cap, DuckLake scans a significant
  portion of the 625k candidate list before finding enough groups to fill the
  cap. The enumeration pass itself is doing work proportional to the total
  candidates, not the cap. That's another reason small caps don't help as much
  as expected — you pay the enumeration cost for a large fraction of the lake
  regardless.

✻ Churned for 15s

❯ After this is done, I'd like to do a run focused on reducing the count of     
  objects rather than targeting a specific size.  Rather than this pairwise     
  approach.  Also, now that we've run ls on the whole bucket, the prefixes      
  shouldn't be so cold anymore, right?                                          

⏺ Yes — the aws s3 ls --recursive walk touched every prefix in the bucket. S3
  auto-scales request rate per prefix based on sustained traffic, and the
  listing would have registered as activity against those prefixes. They should
  be meaningfully warmer now than they were 12 hours ago, though the effect
  decays over time.

  On the "reduce object count rather than target a specific size" approach —
  you're describing a max_compacted_files-only merge without a target_file_size
  constraint. Instead of grouping adjacent files until they hit 5MiB, just merge
   the N most adjacent files into whatever output size results. DuckLake's
  merge_adjacent_files supports this via max_compacted_files without
  max_file_size — the _scoped_target_file_size call in millpond sets the target,
   but you could set it very high (e.g. 256MiB or 1GiB) so the grouping
  algorithm packs as many adjacent files as possible per output group rather
  than stopping at 5MiB.

  With 619k sub-1MiB files averaging 390KB, packing greedily to 256MiB outputs
  would produce ~2,400 output files from the full backlog instead of ~124k.
  That's 52× fewer output files to write, 52× fewer S3 PUTs — massively reducing
   the sequential group count that's killing throughput.

  The tradeoff: 256MiB output files are bigger than the 64-128MiB tier-3 target,
   but for the initial backlog clearance that's fine — you're optimizing for
  "fewer objects fast" over "perfectly tiered sizes."

  Want me to set that up for the next run after this probe finishes?

⏺ RSS dropped from 12.7 → 2.1 GiB at 540s — that's the commit landing. The
  20-file run completed in ~9 minutes (vs 2-file probe's ~4 minutes). That's not
   10× — it's roughly 2.3× for 10× the files, which is actually better than
  linear. Suggests the S3 prefix warming from the ls run helped.

  The percentage disappeared at 540s because the query finished —
  query_progress() returns -1 when no query is running, which our heartbeat
  renders as blank.

  On the target size: yes, 128 MiB is the right steady-state target (already the
   tier-3 target). For the backlog clearance run you want to bypass all the tier
   boundaries and just merge everything sub-1MiB directly into 128MiB outputs in
   one shot. That means:

  - max_file_size => 1048576 (only pick up sub-1MiB files)
  - target_file_size set to 128MiB
  - No max_compacted_files cap — let it find as many adjacent files as possible
  per group
  - Result: 619k files × 390KB avg = 241GB → ~1,900 output files at 128MiB each,
   vs ~124k output groups at 5MiB

  1,900 sequential groups × ~9 min / 20 files × (20/1) scaling... actually we
  need to think about how many input files per 128MiB group: 128MiB ÷ 390KB =
  ~335 files per group. 619k ÷ 335 = ~1,847 groups. At roughly the same
  per-group S3 cost, maybe 2-3 minutes per group × 1,847 = still a long time.

  The real win is fewer S3 PUTs and a much smaller catalog commit. Want me to
  set that up?

⏺ Bash(sed -n '865,876p'

  ⎿  def compact_probe(conn: duckdb.DuckDBPyConnection, table: str, max_com
     pacted_files: int) -> None:
         """Merge up to N adjacent files in one table without changing targ
     … +13 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ compact_probe passes max_compacted_files directly to DuckLake with no
  max_file_size constraint. Now look at the 20-file probe result again — 9
  groups, each merging hundreds of files. The compacted_files counter in the
  source increments once per input file processed including singles. So
  max_compacted_files=20 means "stop after 20 input file slots are consumed" —
  but a single group can consume many input files before that counter hits 20
  only if the group formation logic isn't bounded per-group.

  Let me re-read the grouping loop more carefully:

⏺ Bash(sed -n '322,370p'
      /Users/jakob/src/ducklake/src/functions/ducklake_compaction_functions.cpp)
  ⎿             }
                for (idx_t start_idx = 0; start_idx <
     candidate_list.size(); start_idx
     ++) {
     … +45 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Found it. compacted_files increments once per outer loop iteration
  (start_idx), not once per input file. Each outer iteration either:
  - Creates one output group (consuming compaction_file_count input files), then
   increments compacted_files by 1
  - Skips a singleton, increments compacted_files by 1

  So max_compacted_files=20 means 20 output groups, not 20 input files. Each
  group can contain hundreds of input files (up to target_file_size). With no
  target_file_size set (or 128MiB), each group packs up to 128MiB / 390KB ≈ 335
  input files. So max_compacted_files=20 → 20 output groups × ~335 input files =
   ~6,700 input files — which matches the 9 groups × ~330 files we saw in the
  probe results.

  This completely reframes everything:
  - The max_compacted_files=1000 we've been running = 1,000 output groups × 335
  input files = 335,000 input files per run — more than half the backlog in one
  shot
  - The max_compacted_files=20 probe = 20 groups × 335 files = ~6,700 input
  files
  - The max_compacted_files=62000 bigmerge = 62,000 output groups × 335 files =
  20.8M input files — far more than the 619k backlog

  So the bigmerge with 62k is actually uncapped for the backlog — it'll process
  all 619k files in one run. And max_compacted_files=1000 was already covering
  the whole backlog in ~3 runs, not 619.

  And the current bigmerge run... is going to try to merge 20M+ files. How's it
  looking?

⏺ Bash(kubectl --context mw-prod-us -n viaduck logs -l
      job-name=viaduck-compaction-bigmerge-1 --tail=5 2>&1)

  a 13M-row catalog... this enumeration is going to be much larger than anything
   we've run. It may also hit RSS limits since it's materializing the full file

  Should we kill it and recalibrate? The right number for "10% of the backlog"
  in output groups is 619k files ÷ 335 files/group × 10% ≈ 185 output groups.
  That's max_compacted_files=185.

✻ Cooked for 1m 51s
❯ So, does this explain everything?                                             

⏺ Mostly yes. With max_compacted_files counting output groups not input files,
  the picture is:

  - The 2-file probe (max_compacted_files=2) → 2 output groups × ~335 input
  files = ~670 input files → 232 seconds
  - The 20-file probe (max_compacted_files=20) → 9 output groups (some hit
  partition boundaries with fewer files) × ~330 avg = ~2,970 input files → 708
  seconds
  - The 1000-file runs we were doing earlier → potentially 1,000 groups × 335 =
  335k input files → hours

  The scaling isn't linear on groups — the 20-probe took 3× the time of the
  2-probe for ~4.4× the groups — so there's a fixed overhead per group plus
  per-file S3 cost. That explains the timing reasonably well.

  What it doesn't fully explain: why 708 seconds for ~2,970 files when the math
  says ~50ms × ~14 requests × 9 groups = ~6 seconds. The S3 prefix throttling
  hypothesis (cold partitions hitting 503 SlowDown → exponential backoff) is
  still the most credible explanation for the remaining 700 seconds. The ls
  warming likely did help — the 20-probe at 708 seconds today vs the 2-probe at
  232 seconds yesterday with the same number of requests per group suggests the
  per-group cost dropped significantly.

  The 185-group run (~62k input files) will be the real test — if it completes
  in a reasonable time, the prefix warming theory holds and we have a workable
  path forward.

  On k9s seeing the full log: k9s reads directly from the container log stream

  default. kubectl logs caps at the last N bytes of the log buffer; k9s streams
  more of it. For a job that emitted 278KB of SQL to stderr, the tail-based
  approaches are only showing the last portion.

⏺ Snapshot ID collision — Key (snapshot_id)=(13812106) already exists. Millpond
  and the compaction job both tried to commit using the same snapshot ID at the
  same time. DuckLake retried 10 times and all 10 attempts hit the same
  collision.

  171 seconds, 6k files processed before the commit conflict. The merge itself
  succeeded — the S3 writes happened — but the catalog commit lost the race
  every single time to millpond writing events.

  ducklake_max_retry_count needs to be raised. The default is 10, and with
  millpond writing continuously at high frequency, 10 retries on a hot snapshot
  sequence isn't enough. The fix is adding SET ducklake_max_retry_count = 100 to
   the compaction tuning in millpond.

  This is also why the 2-file probe and 20-file probe succeeded — they ran when
  millpond write pressure happened to be lower, or they got lucky with retries.
  The 185-group commit is large enough that it repeatedly lost the race.

  Short term: patch ducklake_max_retry_count to 100 in _set_compaction_tuning.
  But this is also a fundamental design tension — a large compaction commit is
  contending with a high-frequency write workload on the same snapshot ID
  sequence.

✻ Cogitated for 2m 1s

❯ Can we set it in the RDS directly?                                            

⏺ ducklake_max_retry_count is a DuckDB session setting, not a postgres parameter
   — it lives in DuckDB's runtime, not RDS. There's no postgres-side equivalent.

  However — looking at what the retry is actually doing: it's retrying on
  snapshot ID collision, which means it's re-reading the current max snapshot_id
   and trying again. The collision happens because millpond and compaction race
  to insert the same snapshot_id.

  The snapshot_id is generated by DuckLake using SELECT next_catalog_id FROM 
  ducklake_snapshot ORDER BY snapshot_id DESC LIMIT 1 or similar. If that
  sequence were a proper postgres SEQUENCE instead of a max+1 pattern, there'd
  be no collision at all — postgres sequences are atomic.

  Let me check how DuckLake generates snapshot IDs:

  Searched for 3 patterns (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ There it is — SELECT MAX(snapshot_id) FROM ducklake_snapshot. That's a
  read-then-increment pattern, not an atomic sequence. Two concurrent writers
  both read the same max, both try to insert max+1, one wins and one gets a
  unique constraint violation. DuckLake retries by re-reading max and trying
  again.

  The answer: there's no postgres-side fix. The retry count is the only lever
  available without changing DuckLake's commit protocol to use a SEQUENCE. Ship
  the millpond patch — it's the only reliable path.

⏺ PR #78: https://github.com/PostHog/millpond/pull/78

  On your question — is DuckLake fundamentally broken by this? Not
  fundamentally, but it's a real design weakness. The MAX(snapshot_id) + 1
  pattern is a classic optimistic concurrency approach — fine at low write
  rates, degrades under high contention. With 200 retries it becomes
  probabilistically reliable even under heavy millpond load: if millpond commits
   ~100 snapshots/minute, each retry has a ~99.5% chance of finding an open
  slot, so 200 retries gives astronomical odds of success. It's just not as
  clean as a postgres SEQUENCE would be. Worth raising upstream as a
  durability/scalability concern alongside the ParallelSink=false issue.

❯ I want to zoom in on this not-using-a-sequence issue.  We gave up on iceberg because its optimistic concurrency was broken for high volume inserts.  Ducklake cannot be hobbled in a similar fashion. 

⏺ 0.0.57 running. Watching for the commit.

  On the sequence issue: it's worth distinguishing the two cases. Millpond's
  writes at ~113/minute are all from separate DuckDB connections, each doing
  MAX(snapshot_id) + 1. If two connections read the same max within the same
  second, one wins and one retries — that's optimistic concurrency working as
  designed at moderate rates.

  What broke compaction is different: a single compaction commit takes seconds
  to execute (S3 writes, large catalog update), during which 200+ new millpond
  snapshots land. The retry logic retries the entire commit, re-reads MAX, tries
   again — but millpond keeps advancing the sequence faster than compaction can
  grab it. 200 retries should be enough headroom for the actual commit to land
  between two millpond writes.

  The Iceberg issue that killed it was the opposite: optimistic concurrency
  failing under concurrent writers (many parallel ingestion streams all
  conflicting). Here it's one large compaction vs a steady ~2/second single
  stream. Iceberg's problem was structural at any concurrency; this is a
  timeout-under-sustained-load problem that 200 retries addresses.

  That said — if DuckLake is the core of the managed warehouse platform, a
  SEQUENCE is the right fix upstream. The MAX + 1 pattern has a theoretical
  breaking point: if the write rate ever exceeds retry_count / commit_duration,
  compaction will always lose. At 113/minute and a 3-second commit window, you
  need ~6 retries. At 1000/minute and a 30-second commit window, you'd need 500.
   A sequence makes it O(1) regardless.

✻ Baked for 1m 50s

❯ Why would they have not gone with this initially?  A sequence seems like the  
  blindingly obvious, everything-else-is-wrong answer to a the question of      
  "what's a snapshot id?".                                                      

⏺ Probably because DuckLake was designed primarily for single-writer workloads —
   one DuckDB process writing to a lake, no concurrent ingestion. In that
  context, MAX + 1 is completely fine and avoids taking a postgres dependency on
   a feature (sequences) that feels like premature infrastructure for a
  file-format library.

  The multiple-concurrent-writer case (millpond's architecture — many pods, many
   topics, all writing to the same catalog simultaneously) is a production
  deployment pattern that probably wasn't the primary design target. The retry
  mechanism is the "we know this can race, here's the escape hatch"
  acknowledgment that they knew about the problem but didn't expect it to be the
   dominant case.

  It's the same thinking that led DuckDB itself to be single-writer initially —
  OLAP tools historically assumed one analyst, not a streaming ingestion
  pipeline. DuckLake inherits that lineage.

  The argument against a sequence: portability. DuckLake theoretically supports
  multiple catalog backends (postgres today, possibly others later). Sequences
  are postgres-specific. MAX + 1 works against any SQL store. But that's a thin
  argument — if your catalog is in postgres, use postgres features.

  Worth filing. The fix is a one-liner migration: CREATE SEQUENCE 
  ducklake_snapshot_id_seq START WITH (SELECT MAX(snapshot_id) + 1 FROM 
  ducklake_snapshot) and replace the MAX(snapshot_id) + 1 read in
  GetSnapshotAndStatsAndChangesQuery with nextval('ducklake_snapshot_id_seq').
  Atomic, no retries needed, scales to any write rate.

✻ Crunched for 18s

❯ Could we convert the existing value to a sequence?                            

⏺ Yes — postgres makes this straightforward:

  -- Create the sequence starting from the current max
  CREATE SEQUENCE ducklake_snapshot_id_seq
    START WITH (SELECT max(snapshot_id) + 1 FROM ducklake_snapshot);

  -- Optionally tie it to the column so it shows up in \d
  ALTER TABLE ducklake_snapshot
    ALTER COLUMN snapshot_id SET DEFAULT nextval('ducklake_snapshot_id_seq');

  But — and this is the catch — DuckLake's commit query generates the
  snapshot_id in DuckDB code (commit_snapshot.snapshot_id++) and explicitly
  passes it in the INSERT. It doesn't rely on a column DEFAULT. So just setting
  the DEFAULT doesn't help; you'd also need to change the DuckLake source to
  call nextval instead of MAX + 1.

  Unless... we add a postgres trigger:

  CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION ducklake_snapshot_id_trigger()
  RETURNS TRIGGER AS $$
  BEGIN
    NEW.snapshot_id := nextval('ducklake_snapshot_id_seq');
    RETURN NEW;
  END;
  $$ LANGUAGE plpgsql;

  CREATE TRIGGER ducklake_snapshot_id_before_insert
    BEFORE INSERT ON ducklake_snapshot
    FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION ducklake_snapshot_id_trigger();

  This intercepts every INSERT and overwrites whatever snapshot_id DuckLake
  tried to use with the next sequence value. DuckLake then gets back the unique
  ID when the INSERT succeeds, and the retry mechanism becomes a no-op because
  collisions never happen.

  The risk: DuckLake reads back the committed snapshot_id to know what was
  actually committed. If the trigger silently changes the value it inserted,
  DuckLake's internal state and the catalog diverge. We'd need to verify
  DuckLake checks the committed snapshot_id via a SELECT after INSERT, not just
  by assuming its attempted value was accepted.

✻ Churned for 18s

❯ And I don't think Ducklake was created with single writers in mind.  It's a   
  data lake.  By definition those are multiwriter.                              

⏺ You're right — that was a bad assumption on my part. DuckLake is explicitly
  designed for multi-writer scenarios; that's the whole point of the
  snapshot-based catalog. The MAX + 1 with retry is a deliberate optimistic
  concurrency design, not an oversight. They just undertuned the default retry
  count for high-frequency workloads.

  But "optimistic concurrency with retries" and "use a sequence" aren't actually
   equivalent in this context. A sequence gives you a unique ID atomically, but
  DuckLake's retry does more than just get a unique ID — it re-reads the full
  snapshot state (GetSnapshotAndStatsAndChanges) to ensure the commit is based
  on the latest catalog version before trying again. If two writers both
  committed between the last read and this attempt, the retry needs to
  incorporate those changes, not just get a new ID.

  So a trigger that silently replaces the snapshot_id would break things:
  DuckLake would think it committed at snapshot N, but the catalog would have it
   at N+47 (whatever the sequence gave it), and its internal state tracking
  would be wrong.

  A sequence fix needs to be in DuckLake itself — replace the MAX + 1 read with
  SELECT nextval(...) at the point where it generates the candidate snapshot_id
  before the commit attempt, not as a trigger. That way DuckLake knows the
  actual ID it's trying to use before it builds the INSERT, and the retry loop
  still handles the case where the snapshot state has changed since the last
  read.

  Still worth filing upstream. The fix is in DuckLake source, not something we
  can do at the postgres layer.

✻ Baked for 18s

❯ What reasonable backends don't implement a SEQUENCE type?                     

⏺ Essentially none at this point. Every major SQL database has sequences or an
  equivalent:

  - PostgreSQL: SEQUENCE (native)
  - MySQL/MariaDB: AUTO_INCREMENT or CREATE SEQUENCE (MariaDB 10.3+, MySQL 8.0+)
  - SQLite: ROWID / INTEGER PRIMARY KEY AUTOINCREMENT
  - SQL Server: SEQUENCE or IDENTITY
  - Oracle: SEQUENCE (this is where sequences were invented)
  - DuckDB itself: CREATE SEQUENCE

  The "portability" argument against sequences is essentially hollow. If you're
  storing a catalog in a SQL database in 2026, it has sequences. The only
  realistic backend that doesn't is something like a flat file or a key-value
  store — neither of which would be a reasonable DuckLake catalog backend
  anyway.

  It's worth saying directly in the upstream issue: the portability argument
  doesn't hold water, the MAX + 1 retry approach has a real failure mode under
  sustained concurrent load, and the fix is a one-line sequence migration that
  every supported SQL backend can handle.

✻ Crunched for 13s

❯ Sometimes you want to reject a snapshot if it wasn't based on the previous.   

⏺ That's a good point — and it's exactly what DuckLake's current model enforces.
   The MAX + 1 retry isn't just about getting a unique ID; it's asserting "I am
  building on top of snapshot N, and if someone else committed N first, I need
  to re-evaluate my changes against their state before I can commit N+1."

  A sequence would give you a unique ID but lose that "built on top of"
  invariant — you'd get snapshot 47 from the sequence, but snapshots 42-46 might
   contain schema changes that invalidate your commit. You'd need a separate
  check for that anyway.

  So the real model DuckLake wants is:

  1. Read current max snapshot N
  2. Build commit based on state at N
  3. Try to INSERT at N+1
  4. If collision: someone else got N+1, re-read their changes, rebuild, try N+2

  That's optimistic concurrency control — the snapshot ID is both a unique
  identifier AND a linearization point. A sequence only solves (3); it doesn't
  help with (4). The retry loop is load-bearing, not just a workaround for the
  ID collision.

  The right upstream fix is probably not "use a sequence" but "use SELECT 
  pg_try_advisory_lock() to serialize commits" or "use INSERT ... WHERE NOT 
  EXISTS with a proper RETURNING" so the retry is fast and deterministic rather
  than potentially spinning under high load. The 200-retry fix we shipped is the
   pragmatic workaround; the upstream fix is a proper serialization primitive.

  (171-425 files). That's because the files aren't evenly distributed: some hour
   partitions have hundreds of adjacent small files (the busy hours), most have
  just a few.

  baseline. At this rate, the full 619k backlog would be ~1,850 output groups

✻ Crunched for 12s

❯ What the hell?                                                                

⏺ The tiny groups (2-6 files) — that's what's killing throughput. Each group is
  a sequential S3 read+write cycle with ~50ms per request. A group of 2 files
  does 3 S3 requests (2 GETs + 1 PUT) and produces a ~800KB output file. That's
  not compaction, that's just shuffling data around for no meaningful gain.
  Those tiny groups exist because the files at the boundaries of hour partitions
   don't have enough neighbors to fill a 128MiB target — so you get a group of 2
   at the start of one hour merging with 2 at the end of the previous.

  This is why the "reduce object count" approach matters more than hitting a
  target size. A 2-file merge reduces object count by 1 but costs the same S3
  round-trips as a 400-file merge. You want to minimize groups, not maximize
  output file size.

  The fix: set min_file_size high enough to skip the tiny output files that
  aren't worth merging — or better, add a minimum group size (e.g. only merge
  groups with ≥ 10 input files). DuckLake doesn't expose a minimum group size
  directly, but you could filter by partition to only compact the dense hours,
  or accept that the boundary files just stay small.

  For the actual backlog: those ~60 singleton/tiny groups out of 185 total
  represent maybe 100 input files but cost the same time as the 400-file groups.
   Skipping them would cut ~30% of the run time for almost no reduction in
  object count.

✻ Cogitated for 19s

❯ Let's target get 128 MB files everywhere we can.  In some prefixes we won't   
  be able to because there just aren't enough objects in a prefix to reach that 
   target.  But everywhere, scale up to a target of 128 MB.  Let's do it in     
  batches until we're confident things are proceeding correctly.                

⏺ Right — the natural unit here is one hour partition. A busy hour has 400+
  files × 390KB = ~156MB, which fills one 128MiB output file cleanly. A quiet
  hour has 2-6 files and produces a small output that we accept. The key is
  letting DuckLake pack each partition as full as possible rather than stopping
  at 5MiB targets.

  The current probe setup already does this — compact-probe with no
  max_file_size uses the catalog's target_file_size which is already 128MiB (the
   default). The issue is max_compacted_files=185 is counting output groups, so
  we're getting ~185 output files regardless of input file count.

  To process 10% of the backlog properly: we want ~185 dense output groups,
  skipping the 2-file boundary groups. But we can't filter those out directly.

  Alternative: instead of compact-probe (which scans catalog-wide), use compact 
  --tier 1 with the 128MiB target. We can temporarily override tier 1's target
  by patching the TIERS dict... but we can't do that without a code change.

  Simplest path: use compact-probe with a larger max_compacted_files to ensure
  we're getting meaningful groups. Given ~185 groups in the last run processed
  ~62k files, to get 10% of 619k we need ~185 groups that are actually dense.
  The problem is we can't control which groups get selected — DuckLake picks
  them in file order.

  What if we just set max_compacted_files=2000 and let it run? That would be ~10
   runs worth of output groups, covering ~620k input files — essentially the
  whole tier-1 backlog in one shot at 128MiB targets. Given 377s for 185 groups,
   2000 groups might take ~40 minutes, and with 200 retries the commit should
  survive.

  Want to try that?
