  ⎿  Running in the background (↓ to manage)

❯ Really what we want to is to compact all the leaf objects in the prefix, the colocated parquet objects.  The target shouldn't be size, but just to have one object per prefix, regardless of its size. 

⏺ That's a clean mental model — one file per hour partition, regardless of how
  big it ends up. Since the partitions are year=.../month=.../day=.../hour=.../,
   each hour prefix either has enough files to fill a 128MiB output or it
  doesn't, and the result is one file either way.

  DuckLake's grouping already does this naturally when you set the target high
  enough — it packs adjacent files until it hits the target, then starts a new
  group. If you set target_file_size to something huge (say 10GiB), each hour
  partition becomes a single group regardless of total size. The output files
  will vary wildly in size (2KB to 1GB+) but object count per prefix drops to 1.

  The constraint: DuckLake groups files that are "adjacent" — ordered by
  (begin_snapshot, row_id_start). If an hour has files from different snapshot
  ranges (which it will for a busy hour written over many millpond flushes), the
   grouping should pack them all together under a very large target.

  Set target_file_size to 10GiB and let it run. The current probe is already
  running — once it finishes, we can set a permanent large target in the
  catalog:

  CALL ducklake_set_option('lake', 'target_file_size', '10737418240');

  That persists across sessions. Then any subsequent compact-probe run will
  naturally produce one file per prefix.

✻ Sautéed for 1m 57s

❯ "If an hour has files from                                                    
    different snapshot ranges (which it will for a busy hour written over many  
  millpond flushes), the grouping should pack them all                          
    together under a very large target."  Huh.  You're saying there could be    
  rows in a parquet objects for different snapshots.                            

⏺ Yes — DuckLake's parquet files aren't snapshot-scoped. A single parquet file
  written by one millpond flush covers one batch of rows, but many flushes write
   many files into the same hour partition prefix, each with its own
  begin_snapshot. The compaction merge reads all of them and writes one output
  file containing rows from many different original snapshots. The output file
  gets a new begin_snapshot from the compaction commit itself.

  So "adjacent" in DuckLake means adjacent by (begin_snapshot, row_id_start)
  ordering within the catalog — not that the rows inside share a snapshot. The
  compaction is essentially: take N parquet files that happen to be in the same
  partition and whose catalog entries are sequential, concatenate their row
  data, write one output parquet, record the new file in the catalog.

  The snapshot metadata lives in ducklake_snapshot and ducklake_data_file, not
  inside the parquet file itself. The parquet is just row data.

✻ Churned for 13s

❯ But that's fine, one can read from different snapshots in a single query.     
  Queries aren't over contiguous snapshots...                                   

⏺ Right — queries are over a snapshot ID (point-in-time), and DuckLake resolves
  which files are live at that snapshot via ducklake_data_file.begin_snapshot
  and end_snapshot. A merged output file is just a file that happens to contain
  rows originally written across many snapshots — the catalog tracks it as a
  single live file starting from the compaction's snapshot. Queries don't care
  that the underlying rows came from different original flushes.

  The compaction is purely a physical optimization (fewer, larger S3 objects)
  with no semantic impact on query results. The snapshot history still exists in
   the catalog; the physical files just no longer correspond 1:1 to the original
   write batches.

  How's the 2000-group run doing?

✻ Worked for 12s

❯ That's basically what compaction is.                                          

⏺ Exactly. And it means the "one file per prefix" goal is completely sound — the
   output file contains all the rows for that hour partition, readable at any
  snapshot ≥ the compaction snapshot, with no semantic difference from querying
  the original N files. Clean.

  How's postgres looking on the running job?

⏺ It's in the commit phase — all 5 connections are actively trying to INSERT
  snapshot 13815767. The compaction (21802) and 4 millpond writers are all
  racing for the same snapshot ID right now. With 200 retries, the compaction
  will win eventually — millpond will get out of the way as each of those
  connections commits and moves on to 13815768, 13815769, etc.

  This is exactly the scenario the retry fix was designed for. Watch for it to
  clear.

✻ Crunched for 10s

❯ We need a better solution for this.  If it's not sequences, what is it?       

⏺ The cleanest solution within DuckLake's existing model is a postgres advisory 
  lock on the commit path:

  SELECT pg_try_advisory_xact_lock(hashtext('ducklake_snapshot_commit'))

  Held for the duration of the INSERT transaction. This serializes all
  committers through a single queue — compaction, millpond pods, everyone. The
  lock is session-scoped (or transaction-scoped with xact_lock), automatically
  released on commit or rollback, and costs nothing when uncontested.

  The key difference from the current model: instead of "try, fail, re-read,
  retry" in a loop, it's "wait for the lock, commit once, done." Zero collision
  rate, no retry needed, no wasted S3 work from failed commits.

  The tradeoff: serializes all writers. At 113 commits/minute that's ~530ms
  average wait per commit — acceptable. At 10k commits/minute it becomes a
  bottleneck, but that's not the current scale.

  The reason they probably didn't do this: advisory locks are postgres-specific,
   and they change the concurrency model from "optimistic, parallel, retry on
  conflict" to "pessimistic, serial, wait." For a read-heavy lake with
  occasional writes, optimistic is better. For a high-frequency write workload
  like this one, pessimistic wins.

  Worth filing upstream alongside the sequence suggestion — give them both
  options. The advisory lock is a 2-line change in
  GetSnapshotAndStatsAndChangesQuery.

⏺ Bash(psql "postgres://megaduck:$(kubectl --context mw-prod-us -n viaduck get

❯ Just the SQL, I'll run it.                                                    

⏺ SELECT
    t.table_name,
    CASE
      WHEN df.file_size_bytes < 1024*1024 THEN '< 1 MiB'
      WHEN df.file_size_bytes < 10*1024*1024 THEN '1-10 MiB'
      WHEN df.file_size_bytes < 64*1024*1024 THEN '10-64 MiB'
      WHEN df.file_size_bytes < 128*1024*1024 THEN '64-128 MiB'
      ELSE '>= 128 MiB'
    END AS size_bucket,
    count(*) AS files,
    pg_size_pretty(sum(df.file_size_bytes)) AS total_size
  FROM public.ducklake_data_file df
  JOIN public.ducklake_table t USING (table_id)
  WHERE df.end_snapshot IS NULL
  GROUP BY t.table_name, 2
  ORDER BY t.table_name, min(df.file_size_bytes);

❯ Why do we still have events tables running around with sizes less than 64 MB? 

⏺ The 267 files in 1-10MiB and 3,654 in 10-64MiB are the output files from the 
  compaction runs we just did — the compact-probe runs merged the 619k sub-1MiB
  events files into groups, producing intermediate-sized outputs (1-10MiB for
  the tiny 2-3 file boundary groups, 10-64MiB for the larger groups that didn't
  quite fill a 128MiB target). They're the result of the 5MiB and
  variable-target merges from earlier experiments.

  They'll need tier-2 and tier-3 passes to roll up to 128MiB. But since events
  is a read-heavy table (it's the main query target) and there are only ~4k
  files in those intermediate tiers, it's not urgent — query performance on 4k
  files vs 1 file per prefix is negligible compared to the 607k
  person_distinct_id mess.

     … +3 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

❯ This pair-wise compaction strategy that Ducklake recommends seems odd. 

⏺ Running. While it goes — agreed, it's odd. The "adjacent files" model makes
  sense for append-only writes where files land in sequential order and you want
   to merge neighbors into one larger file without reordering. It's borrowed
  from LSM-tree compaction (LevelDB, Cassandra, RocksDB) where you compact
  adjacent SSTable runs to reduce read amplification.

  But for a data lake where files are partitioned by time (year/month/day/hour),
   "adjacent in catalog order" and "in the same partition" are mostly the same
  thing — files written to the same hour land close together in begin_snapshot
  order. So the adjacency model works well for the normal case.

  Where it breaks down is exactly what we're seeing: when files from multiple
  compaction passes are interleaved with large existing files in the catalog
  ordering, they're no longer adjacent to each other. The model assumes one
  writer writing sequentially — not iterative compaction passes that produce
  intermediate-sized files that then need another pass.

  The cleaner model would be partition-aware compaction: "merge all files in
  this hour prefix into one, regardless of catalog adjacency." That's what you'd
   get with REWRITE_DELETES or an explicit table scan scoped by partition. Worth
   filing alongside the snapshot ID issue.

✻ Worked for 1m 40s

❯ Yeah.  Also, could we sneakily implement with the existing APIs?              

⏺ Yes — via --table scoped to a single partition. If DuckLake supports filtering
   by partition value in merge_adjacent_files, we could call it once per hour
  prefix rather than catalog-wide. Let me check:

  Searched for 2 patterns (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ No partition_filter named parameter in the API. The function accepts
  max_compacted_files, min_file_size, max_file_size, delete_threshold — but no
  way to scope by partition value.

  However — we can sneak it in via the scan itself. Looking at
  GetFilesForCompaction, it queries ducklake_data_file for candidates. If we
  could add a WHERE clause on path LIKE 
  'main/person_distinct_id/year=2026/month=4/day=27/hour=23/%'... but that's
  inside DuckLake internals.

  The sneaky approach with existing APIs: call
  ducklake_merge_adjacent_files('lake', 'person_distinct_id', 
  max_compacted_files => 1) in a loop from Python — one call per partition. With
   max_compacted_files=1 it merges exactly one output group per call, and since
  it picks candidates in catalog order (which follows partition order), repeated
   calls would walk through partitions sequentially. Slow but effective.

  Or — does DuckLake support CALL ducklake_merge_adjacent_files('lake', 
  'schema.table') with a WHERE clause somehow via the scan pushdown? Let me
  check if there's a filter parameter we missed:
  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ No partition filter parameter. The sneakiest existing-API approach is actually
   in the catalog itself — temporarily set end_snapshot on the large files in a
  partition to make them invisible to the compaction candidate query, run
  compaction, then restore. But that's dangerously close to catalog surgery.

  The cleaner sneaky approach: use min_file_size and max_file_size bounds to
  select only the intermediate-sized files (the 1-64MiB range) that need rolling
   up, excluding the already-large files. Since the adjacency constraint groups
  files in catalog order, two intermediate files that are adjacent (written in
  the same hour but separated by a large file in between) still won't group —
  but intermediate files that ARE adjacent (multiple compaction outputs landing
  in the same hour) will.

  Honestly the real answer is a millpond-side tool that calls
  merge_adjacent_files once per partition in a loop — we know the partition
  scheme (year/month/day/hour), we can enumerate them from the catalog and call
  one merge per prefix. That's the partition-aware compaction the API doesn't
  expose but Python can implement on top of it.

✻ Sautéed for 53s

❯ These files are being partitioned by ingestion time (because event time would 
   explode the number of objects).  So, almost always, once an hour passes, no  
  more files should be added to that prefix.                                    

⏺ Right — so each closed hour prefix is a finite, stable set of files. The only
  open prefix is the current hour. That makes partition-aware compaction trivial
   to implement: enumerate all closed hour prefixes from the catalog, call
  merge_adjacent_files scoped to each one, done.

  We can do it by querying ducklake_file_partition_value to get the distinct
  partition combinations, filtering for closed hours (hour < current hour), and
  calling the merge per partition. Except... merge_adjacent_files doesn't accept
   a partition filter.

  But we have another lever: the catalog's path column in ducklake_data_file
  contains the full relative path including the partition prefix. If we
  temporarily mark files outside the target partition as "too large" by setting
  end_snapshot... no, that's catalog surgery.

  The practical implementation: a Python loop in millpond that:
  1. Queries distinct closed hour partitions with >1 small file from
  ducklake_data_file
  2. For each partition, calls merge_adjacent_files with max_compacted_files=1
  (one output group — the whole partition merges into one file)
  3. Moves to the next partition

  With ParallelSink=false this is equivalent to what DuckLake does internally,
  but explicitly partition-scoped. Each call is one catalog transaction, one S3
  read + write per partition, clean commit. Want me to add a compact-partition
  command to millpond?

✻ Brewed for 16s

⏺ All green. On your question about prefix warming vs understanding the
  mechanics — honestly both, but the mechanics matter more. The 185-group run at
   377s today vs the 2-file probe at 232s yesterday — if prefix warming were
  dominant we'd expect the per-group cost to drop massively, but 377/185 =
  2s/group vs 232/2 = 116s/group. That's a 58× improvement, which is way too
  large to be explained by S3 warming alone (which gives maybe 2-5× improvement
  on cold prefixes).

  The real gains were: fixing the missing index (4+ hours → 1s enumeration),
  understanding max_compacted_files counts output groups not input files (so our
   "small" runs were actually processing far more than we thought), and the
  retry count fix. The prefix warming is probably a single-digit percentage of
  the improvement.

  Ready to commit/push/PR?

✻ Sautéed for 13s

❯ I want to develop a metric for how fragmented a table is based on its         
  potential partition-aware file distribution.  We can query the RDS to get the 
   information.  A partition with 100 128MB files is very fragmented.  That     
  same partition with 1 128 MB file isn't.  But a partition with only enough    
  data to have 1 12 MB object is also not fragmented.  The target value should  
  be fragmented.  We should be able to check per parition, as well as an        
  average or median value applied to the table as a whole.                      
⏺ The metric is: fragmentation_ratio = actual_files / ideal_files where
  ideal_files = GREATEST(1, CEIL(total_bytes / 128MiB)).

  - 100 × 128MiB files in a partition → ideal=100, ratio=1.0 ✓ (correctly sized,
   just many of them — actually not fragmented by this measure)

  Wait — rethinking. 100 × 128MiB files IS fragmented (100 files when
  theoretically compressible to 1 if they were all the same data... but they're
  not the same data). The fragmentation isn't about file count vs total size,
  it's about file count vs the minimum number of files needed to hold that data 
  at the target size.

  So ideal_files = GREATEST(1, CEIL(total_bytes / 128MiB)) is correct:
  - 100 × 128MiB = 12.8GiB → ideal = 100 → ratio = 1.0 — not fragmented
  (correctly: 100 files is right for 12.8GiB at 128MiB target)
  - 100 × 1MiB = 100MiB → ideal = 1 → ratio = 100 — very fragmented
  - 1 × 12MiB = 12MiB → ideal = 1 → ratio = 1.0 — not fragmented

  That's the right semantics. Here's the SQL:

  WITH partition_stats AS (
      SELECT
          t.table_name,
          regexp_replace(df.path, '/[^/]+\.parquet$', '') AS partition_prefix,
          count(*)                  AS file_count,
          sum(df.file_size_bytes)   AS total_bytes
      FROM public.ducklake_data_file df
      JOIN public.ducklake_table t USING (table_id)
      WHERE df.end_snapshot IS NULL
      GROUP BY t.table_name, partition_prefix
  ),
  scored AS (
      SELECT
          table_name,
          partition_prefix,
          file_count,
          pg_size_pretty(total_bytes)
     AS total_size,
          GREATEST(1, CEIL(total_bytes::numeric / (128 * 1024 * 1024)))::int
     AS ideal_files,
          ROUND(
              file_count::numeric /
              GREATEST(1, CEIL(total_bytes::numeric / (128 * 1024 * 1024))),
          2)
      AS fragmentation_ratio
      FROM partition_stats
  )
  -- Per-partition view (most fragmented first):
  SELECT * FROM scored
  ORDER BY fragmentation_ratio DESC, file_count DESC
  LIMIT 50;

  And the table-level rollup:

  -- Table summary:
  SELECT
      table_name,
      count(*)                                                                AS
   partition_count,
      ROUND(AVG(fragmentation_ratio), 2)                                     AS
  avg_fragmentation,
      ROUND(PERCENTILE_CONT(0.5) WITHIN GROUP (ORDER BY fragmentation_ratio), 2)
   AS median_fragmentation,
      MAX(fragmentation_ratio)                                                AS
   max_fragmentation,
      SUM(file_count)                                                         AS
   total_files,
      SUM(ideal_files)                                                        AS
   total_ideal_files
  FROM scored
  GROUP BY table_name
  ORDER BY avg_fragmentation DESC;

  A ratio of 1.0 is optimal. The interesting band is 2–10 (mildly fragmented,
  worth compacting eventually) vs 10+ (actively hurting scan performance).

⏺ Single pasteable query:

  WITH partition_stats AS (
      SELECT t.table_name,
             regexp_replace(df.path, '/[^/]+\.parquet$', '') AS
  partition_prefix,
             count(*)               AS file_count,
             sum(df.file_size_bytes) AS total_bytes
      FROM public.ducklake_data_file df
      JOIN public.ducklake_table t USING (table_id)
      WHERE df.end_snapshot IS NULL
      GROUP BY t.table_name, partition_prefix
  ),
  scored AS (
      SELECT table_name, partition_prefix, file_count,
             pg_size_pretty(total_bytes) AS total_size,
             GREATEST(1, CEIL(total_bytes::numeric / (128*1024*1024)))::int AS
  ideal_files,
             ROUND(file_count::numeric / GREATEST(1, CEIL(total_bytes::numeric /
   (128*1024*1024))), 2) AS fragmentation_ratio
      FROM partition_stats
  )
  SELECT table_name,
         count(*)
       AS partition_count,
         ROUND(AVG(fragmentation_ratio), 2)
      AS avg_fragmentation,
         ROUND(PERCENTILE_CONT(0.5) WITHIN GROUP (ORDER BY fragmentation_ratio),
   2) AS median_fragmentation,
         MAX(fragmentation_ratio)
       AS max_fragmentation,
         SUM(file_count)
       AS total_files,
         SUM(ideal_files)
       AS total_ideal_files
  FROM scored
  GROUP BY table_name
  ORDER BY avg_fragmentation DESC;

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ WITH partition_stats AS (
      SELECT t.table_name,
             regexp_replace(df.path, '/[^/]+\.parquet$', '') AS
  partition_prefix,
             count(*)                AS file_count,
             sum(df.file_size_bytes) AS total_bytes
      FROM public.ducklake_data_file df
      JOIN public.ducklake_table t USING (table_id)
      WHERE df.end_snapshot IS NULL
      GROUP BY t.table_name, partition_prefix
  ),
  scored AS (
      SELECT table_name, partition_prefix, file_count,
             pg_size_pretty(total_bytes) AS total_size,
             GREATEST(1, CEIL(total_bytes::numeric / (128*1024*1024)))::int AS
  ideal_files,
             ROUND(file_count::numeric / GREATEST(1, CEIL(total_bytes::numeric /
   (128*1024*1024))), 2) AS fragmentation_ratio
      FROM partition_stats
  )
  SELECT table_name,
         count(*)
                  AS partition_count,
         ROUND(AVG(fragmentation_ratio), 2)
                 AS avg_fragmentation,
         ROUND(PERCENTILE_CONT(0.5) WITHIN GROUP (ORDER BY
  fragmentation_ratio)::numeric, 2)  AS median_fragmentation,
         MAX(fragmentation_ratio)
                  AS max_fragmentation,
         SUM(file_count)
                  AS total_files,
         SUM(ideal_files)
                  AS total_ideal_files
  FROM scored
  GROUP BY table_name
  ORDER BY avg_fragmentation DESC;

✻ Cogitated for 11s

❯ That enumeration seems wasteful, or cache-able.                               

⏺ Agreed — every batch re-scans the full person_distinct_id candidate set from
  postgres to find the next 2000 groups, even though the candidates don't change
   between batches (millpond isn't adding sub-1MiB files during compaction).
  With 607k candidates that's a full COPY of the filtered ducklake_data_file
  rows on every loop iteration.

  The natural fix is for DuckLake to retain the candidate list in memory between
   batches and resume from where the last batch ended, rather than re-querying
  from scratch. It already has the begin_snapshot/row_id_start ordering — it
  could checkpoint its position in the candidate list and restart the inner loop
   from there instead of re-running GetFilesForCompaction.

  Alternatively, max_compacted_files controlling the outer loop rather than the
  inner loop: process all candidates in one GetFilesForCompaction call, then
  execute them in batches of N with separate commits. The candidate list is
  built once, batched commits keep transactions small.

  Both require DuckLake source changes. For now, the pragmatic mitigation is:
  run fewer, larger batches so the enumeration overhead is amortized. With 2000
  groups we're paying ~5 minutes of enumeration per batch. At 10000 groups we'd
  pay the same 5 minutes but process 5× more work — better ratio. The tradeoff
  is a larger catalog commit per batch and longer open transactions.

  Worth adding to the upstream issues list alongside ParallelSink=false and the
  sequence problem.

✻ Churned for 21s

❯ Please do.  Any indices to improve this?                                      

⏺ The enumeration query (from the source we read earlier) does:

  SELECT ... FROM ducklake_data_file
  WHERE table_id = ? AND end_snapshot IS NULL AND file_size_bytes < ?
  ORDER BY begin_snapshot, row_id_start, data_file_id

  The existing index covers (table_id, end_snapshot, file_size_bytes) WHERE 
  end_snapshot IS NULL — that handles the filter. But the ORDER BY 
  begin_snapshot, row_id_start, data_file_id still requires a sort of the result
   set since those columns aren't in the index. Adding them to the index makes
  it a covering index-scan with pre-sorted output:

  CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY ducklake_data_file_compaction_order_idx
    ON public.ducklake_data_file (table_id, end_snapshot, file_size_bytes,
  begin_snapshot, row_id_start, data_file_id)
    WHERE end_snapshot IS NULL;

  This also eliminates the COPY cost of pulling all candidate rows into DuckDB
  for sorting — postgres can stream them already sorted. At 607k rows that's the
   difference between a sort-and-stream and a pure sequential scan with
  pre-ordered output.

  Also worth checking whether ducklake_file_column_stats and
  ducklake_file_partition_value have indexes on data_file_id — those are JOINed
  in the enumeration query and we only added an index on
  ducklake_file_partition_value(data_file_id) earlier. Check:

  ! psql "..." -c "\d public.ducklake_file_column_stats"

⏺ Update(~/src/runbooks/docs/maintenance/ducklake-compaction.md)
  ⎿  Added 15 lines
      259  ## Upstream Feature Requests
      260  
      261  - **`ParallelSink=true` for compaction**: `DuckLakeCompaction:
           :ParallelSink()` currently returns `false`, serializing all co
           mpaction group pipelines regardless of thread count. This is t
           he primary performance limitation — fixing it would allow the 
           UNION ALL plan to execute groups concurrently and would likely
            reduce per-run time by 10–50×.
      262 +- **Partition-aware compaction API**: `merge_adjacent_files` h
          +as no partition filter parameter. Files from prior compaction 
          +passes interleaved with large files in catalog order can't be 
          +re-grouped. A `partition_filter` parameter (or a separate `com
          +pact_partition` function) would allow targeting a specific hou
          +r prefix directly, producing one file per closed partition reg
          +ardless of catalog ordering. Workaround: call `merge_adjacent_
          +files` in a Python loop, one call per closed-hour partition en
          +umerated from `ducklake_data_file`.                           
      263 +- **Candidate list caching between batches**: each `max_compac
          +ted_files` batch re-runs `GetFilesForCompaction` from scratch 
          +(full COPY of filtered `ducklake_data_file`), even though cand
          +idates don't change between batches. Retaining the sorted cand
          +idate list in memory and resuming from a checkpointed position
          + would eliminate the repeated enumeration cost (~5 min/batch a
          +t 607k candidates).                                           
      264 +- **Catalog snapshot ID allocation via SEQUENCE**: `MAX(snapsh
          +ot_id) + 1` with retry-on-collision fails under sustained conc
          +urrent write load (observed: 10 retries all collided at ~113 s
          +napshots/minute). A postgres `SEQUENCE` would make allocation 
          +atomic and eliminate retries entirely. The retry-based model i
          +s load-bearing for the "built on top of snapshot N" invariant,
          + but a `SELECT pg_try_advisory_xact_lock(...)` approach would 
          +also work — serialize committers through a lock rather than sp
          +inning on collisions.                                         
      265  - **`ducklake_compaction_progress` table**: per-file INSERT/UP
           DATE within the merge transaction, readable via a separate REA
           D COMMITTED connection. Would give live files-merged counter w
           ithout waiting for commit.
      266  - **Incremental checkpointing**: commit each merged file (or s
           mall batch) independently, making compaction restartable and f
           ailure blast radius = one file. Analogous to Iceberg rewrite-m
           anifests approach.
      267  - **Network I/O in heartbeat**: expose bytes-read and bytes-wr
           itten counters alongside RSS to distinguish read/sort/write ph
           ases without manual pod exec sampling.
      268  - **S3 write verification before catalog commit**: DuckLake's 
           catalog commit is atomic but S3 PUTs preceding it are not. A f
           ailed S3 PUT that goes undetected before the postgres commit w
           ould produce a catalog entry pointing to a nonexistent object.
            Verified write (HEAD request or ETag check) before committing
            would close this gap.
      269 +

          +data_file_id` requires an in-memory sort of the filtered candi
          +date set. A covering index with the sort columns eliminates th
          +is:

✻ Brewed for 27s

❯ Is ducklake_file_column_stats defined efficiently?                            

⏺ Let me look at the schema:

⏺ Bash(psql "postgres://megaduck:$(kubectl --context mw-prod-us -n viaduck get
      secret viaduck-postgres-uris -o jsonpath='{.data.source_password}'
      2>/dev/null | base64…)
  ⎿                 Table "public.ducklake_file_column_stats"
           Column       |       Type        | Collation | Nullable | Defaul
     t
     … +12 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Index is INVALID — still building. On the schema itself: the design is
  reasonable for what it does, but min_value and max_value as character varying
  means every value (including numerics, timestamps, UUIDs) is stored as text.
  That's flexible but wastes space compared to typed storage — a timestamp as
  varchar is ~25 bytes vs 8 bytes native. With 4B rows that adds up. Not much
  DuckLake can do without type-per-column storage, though.

  The bigger efficiency question is whether ducklake_file_column_stats needs to
  exist at all for compaction. The compaction query JOINs it to get per-file
  stats — but it's only needed for the REWRITE_DELETES path (which needs to
  check if deletes can be skipped). For MERGE_ADJACENT_TABLES (our path), it may
   just be noise. Worth checking whether the JOIN is always performed or
  conditional on compaction type.

❯ Rough ballpark estimate for how long is remaining?                            

⏺ At 2,300 files merged in ~38 minutes, that's ~60 files/minute. 607k remaining
  ÷ 60 = ~10,000 minutes = ~7 days at current throughput.

  That's clearly not the steady-state rate — we were still building indexes
  during this run and the enumeration was slow. But even with the new indexes
  cutting enumeration from 5+ minutes to hopefully <1 minute per batch, the
  fundamental bottleneck is the sequential S3 execution (ParallelSink=false).

  A more optimistic estimate assuming indexes halve the overhead: 2,300 files
  per ~20 min = 115 files/min → 607k ÷ 115 = ~5,200 minutes = ~3.5 days at
  15-minute cron cadence.

  The honest answer is we need to see the next batch complete with the new
  indexes before we can give a reliable number. If the enumeration drops from 8
  minutes to 1 minute and the merge itself stays at ~20 minutes per batch, and
  each batch processes significantly more than 2,300 files, the estimate changes
   dramatically.

  Kill this run, let the indexes finish building, then do a timed test run to
  get a real per-batch number?

✻ Sautéed for 7s

❯ How do we get reasonably short jobs (less than one hour) and good progress?   

⏺ The key insight is max_compacted_files counts output groups, not input files.
  With person_distinct_id averaging ~227 files per group (from the fragmentation
   query), we need to pick a cap that gives us ~1 hour of work.

  From the events data: 185 groups × ~377 seconds = 2s/group. At that rate:
  - 1 hour = 3600s ÷ 2s/group = 1,800 groups per run
  - 1,800 groups × 227 files/group = ~408k files per run
  - 607k remaining ÷ 408k = ~2 runs to clear the backlog

  But we don't know if person_distinct_id groups take the same 2s as events
  groups — the files are smaller (person_distinct_id avg ~390KB too, so
  similar). Let's assume similar.

  So: set max_compacted_files=1800, run once, time it. If it takes ~1 hour and
  clears ~400k files, one more run finishes the job. If it takes longer, adjust
  down.

  Also — once the indexes are built, enumeration should be faster, which means
  more of that hour goes to actual merging rather than catalog scans.

  Kill the current job, wait for indexes to finish, then launch with
  max_compacted_files=1800?

✻ Worked for 9s

❯ We still need a better solution for the concurrent snapshot insertion.  The   
  whole point of using postgres is to avoid things like that.  Postgres is the  
  undisputed king of OLTP; our piddly insert rate shouldn't be causing          
  problems.                                                                     

⏺ Agreed completely. The irony is DuckLake chose postgres specifically for its
  ACID guarantees, then reimplemented optimistic concurrency on top of it
  instead of using the primitive postgres already has for exactly this problem.

  The right fix upstream is a single line:

  -- At the start of the commit transaction:
  SELECT pg_advisory_xact_lock(hashtext('ducklake_commit'));

  Transaction-scoped advisory lock — automatically released on commit or
  rollback, no cleanup needed, zero overhead when uncontested, and serializes
  all committers through postgres's own lock manager. No retries, no collisions,
   no ducklake_max_retry_count tuning. At 113 commits/minute the average wait is
   ~530ms, which is negligible for a data lake write path.

  The alternative — a SEQUENCE — would give unique IDs but doesn't solve the "I
  need to build on top of snapshot N" linearization requirement. The advisory
  lock solves both: it ensures each committer reads the true current max
  snapshot before inserting, so the "built on top of" invariant is maintained
  without any retry logic.

  Worth filing as a concrete proposal with the code location
  (GetSnapshotAndStatsAndChangesQuery in ducklake_metadata_manager.cpp) and the
  single-line fix. You have the relationship with the DuckLake team — this plus
  the ParallelSink=false issue are the two things that would make DuckLake
  production-viable at PostHog's write scale.

✻ Worked for 15s
❯ We have a very bad relationship with the Ducklake team.  It's why we tried to 
   go to iceberg.  They're asses.                                               

⏺ Noted. Then the fix needs to happen on our side without upstream cooperation.

  The cleanest path: add the advisory lock in millpond's connect(), before the
  DuckLake ATTACH. When DuckLake goes to commit, the lock is already held by the
   connection, so the postgres-level transaction that DuckLake uses for the
  snapshot INSERT will serialize naturally without DuckLake knowing anything
  about it.

  Wait — that won't work. The advisory lock needs to be held for the duration of
   DuckLake's internal commit transaction, but DuckLake manages that transaction
   internally. Millpond's connection is a DuckDB connection; DuckLake's postgres
   transactions are separate connections managed by the postgres extension.

  The only lever we have at the postgres layer without touching DuckLake source:
   a BEFORE INSERT trigger on ducklake_snapshot that acquires the advisory lock:

  CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION ducklake_snapshot_serialize()
  RETURNS TRIGGER AS $$
  BEGIN
    PERFORM pg_advisory_xact_lock(hashtext('ducklake_snapshot_commit'));
    RETURN NEW;
  END;
  $$ LANGUAGE plpgsql;

  CREATE TRIGGER ducklake_snapshot_before_insert
    BEFORE INSERT ON public.ducklake_snapshot
    FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION ducklake_snapshot_serialize();

  The trigger fires inside DuckLake's commit transaction, acquires the lock, and
   holds it until the transaction commits or rolls back. DuckLake doesn't need
  to know it exists. Zero code changes on our side beyond this DDL.

  The risk: if DuckLake inspects ducklake_snapshot in ways that assume the
  inserted snapshot_id is what it tried to insert (vs what a trigger might
  change), that could break things. But the trigger isn't changing the value —
  just serializing the insert. DuckLake would still insert the snapshot_id it
  chose; it just waits its turn. The retry logic becomes a no-op since
  collisions can't happen with serialized inserts.

  Want to test this?

✻ Sautéed for 18s

❯ No, we can push it through if we need to.  What factor of an improvement      
  would this likely provide and what would be the pushback?                     

⏺ Improvement factor: for the compaction case specifically, the 185-group run
  took 377 seconds of which maybe 60-90 seconds were retry spin (10 retries ×
  ~6-9 seconds each waiting for the snapshot sequence to advance). With the
  trigger, the commit becomes one clean lock-acquire + insert. Call it 10-15%
  improvement on commit time — not dramatic because the commit was already small
   relative to the S3 work.

  The bigger win is reliability: no more Exceeded maximum retry count failures
  on large batches. The 185-group run failed once before we bumped the retry
  count to 200. At higher concurrency or higher millpond write rates, failures
  would recur. The trigger makes it impossible to fail this way regardless of
  write rate.

  For normal millpond writes: at 113 commits/minute, each commit waits ~530ms
  average for the lock. That adds ~8% latency to each write but zero to
  throughput (they're sequential anyway — millpond pods don't batch-commit in
  parallel, each flush is independent). Negligible.

  Pushback arguments:
  1. Triggers are magic — they make postgres behavior invisible to DuckLake,
  which could confuse future debugging. Fair, but documented.
  2. Advisory locks serialize all writers globally — yes, that's the point. At
  113/min it's fine; at 10k/min it becomes a bottleneck. Not our problem today.
  3. DuckLake might add their own fix later that conflicts — if they ever add
  pg_advisory_xact_lock upstream, our trigger becomes redundant but harmless
  (lock is idempotent within a transaction).
  4. Triggers run in the committer's transaction — if the trigger raises an
  exception, DuckLake's commit rolls back. The function is a PERFORM with no
  failure path, so this is theoretical.

  Net: low risk, high reliability gain, worth doing.

✻ Cogitated for 19s

❯ "Advisory locks serialize all writers globally — yes, that's the point. At    
  113/min it's fine; at 10k/min it becomes a                                    
    bottleneck. Not our problem today."  How does postgres deal with 1-10/k min 
   inserts?                                                                     

⏺ A postgres INSERT on a table with a sequence is atomic and takes microseconds
  — postgres's lock manager serializes concurrent inserts at the row level, not
  the table level, so 10k/min (167/s) is trivial. The sequence nextval is a
  single atomic operation with no contention.

  Which circles back to: the sequence is the right answer even without upstream
  cooperation. We can add it at the postgres layer:

  CREATE SEQUENCE ducklake_snapshot_id_seq
    START WITH (SELECT max(snapshot_id) + 1 FROM ducklake_snapshot);

  CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION ducklake_snapshot_assign_id()
  RETURNS TRIGGER AS $$
  BEGIN
    NEW.snapshot_id := nextval('ducklake_snapshot_id_seq');
    RETURN NEW;
  END;
  $$ LANGUAGE plpgsql;

  CREATE TRIGGER ducklake_snapshot_before_insert
    BEFORE INSERT ON public.ducklake_snapshot
    FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION ducklake_snapshot_assign_id();

  The trigger silently replaces whatever snapshot_id DuckLake tried to insert
  with the sequence value. No collisions, no retries, scales to 10k+/min without
   contention.

  The one genuine risk we raised earlier: DuckLake reads back the committed
  snapshot_id to know what actually landed. If it assumes committed_id == 
  attempted_id without re-reading, its internal state diverges from the catalog.
   Let me check that assumption in the source:

  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Bash(sed -n '1530,1550p'
      /Users/jakob/src/ducklake/src/storage/ducklake_transaction_state.cpp)
  ⎿                     double random_multiplier = (random.NextRandom() +
     1.0) / 2.0;
                        uint64_t sleep_amount =
     (uint64_t)((double)retry_config.retry_wait_ms
     * random_multiplier *
     … +16 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Bash(sed -n '1460,1485p'
      /Users/jakob/src/ducklake/src/storage/ducklake_transaction_state.cpp)
  ⎿                                        const TransactionChangeInformati
     on &transaction_changes,
                                           const DuckLakeRetryConfig &retry
     … +23 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ It uses commit_snapshot.snapshot_id — the value it incremented locally — and
  sets that as the committed ID without reading back from postgres. The trigger 
  approach would silently replace the ID, making DuckLake's internal state 
  wrong. It would think it committed at snapshot N but postgres actually stored
  it at sequence value M. Subsequent reads using snapshot N would fail or return
   wrong results.

  So the sequence trigger is off the table without source changes. The advisory
  lock trigger is safe because it doesn't change the value — it just serializes
  the insert so no two writers try the same ID. DuckLake's assumed snapshot_id
  still matches what lands in postgres.

  The advisory lock trigger is the right call. Sequence requires upstream.

✻ Cooked for 53s

❯ But in general, not Ducklake specific, who do applications implement large    
  scale number of insertions that require this type of coordination?            

⏺ They use sequences — that's the canonical answer. Every high-throughput insert
   system that needs unique monotonic IDs uses a database sequence or
  equivalent:

  - Postgres: SERIAL/BIGSERIAL/IDENTITY columns, or explicit CREATE SEQUENCE +
  nextval()
  - MySQL: AUTO_INCREMENT
  - Kafka: partition offsets are sequence numbers assigned by the broker
  - Snowflake: AUTOINCREMENT / sequence objects
  - Cassandra: UUIDs (avoiding the coordination problem entirely by accepting
  non-monotonic IDs)
  - Twitter Snowflake / UUID v7: distributed sequence generation via timestamp +
   machine ID + counter — still a sequence, just decentralized

  The common thread: the coordination is pushed into the database or a dedicated
   service that handles it atomically. Application code never does SELECT 
  MAX(id) + 1 — that's a known anti-pattern precisely because it breaks under
  concurrency.

  DuckLake's MAX + 1 with retry is essentially reinventing a poorly-performing
  sequence in application code. The fact that it works at all is because the
  retry loop approximates serialization — badly. The advisory lock trigger makes
   it work correctly by adding the serialization postgres would have given you
  with a sequence anyway.

  The deeper question is why DuckLake didn't use a sequence from the start. My
  earlier "portability" theory was weak. The more likely reason: the snapshot_id
   also encodes semantic meaning (it's the linearization point for the "built on
   top of" invariant), and the designers may have wanted explicit control over
  the assignment to ensure the retry loop could re-read current state before
  trying again. A sequence gives you a number but not the current catalog state
  — you still need to re-read anyway. So the retry loop is genuinely necessary
  regardless of whether you use a sequence or MAX+1; the sequence just
  eliminates the collision on the INSERT itself.

  Which means the full fix is: sequence for unique ID generation + advisory lock
   (or SERIALIZABLE isolation) for the "read current state before commit"
  invariant. Two separate problems that DuckLake conflated into one retry loop.

✻ Baked for 21s

❯ Dive into that proposal with a principal architect.  Seems obvious, so I      
  wonder why they didn't do it.                                                 

⏺ Agent(Principal architect review of DuckLake snapshot ID design)
  ⎿  Done (29 tool uses · 54.9k tokens · 2m 3s)
  (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The architect's verdict is that the current DuckLake design is actually
  correct, and our fix was right all along. Key insights:

  1. The PK constraint IS the uniqueness enforcement — not MAX + 1. The
  violation is the signal that someone else committed first; a sequence would
  need a different signal to trigger the conflict-check retry, making things
  more complex not less.
  2. The two concerns are already separated in the code — snapshot_id++ for the
  ID, CheckForConflicts for the linearization check. There's nothing to
  untangle.
  3. Advisory lock is actually worse for normal multi-writer workloads — it
  eliminates all write concurrency, including non-conflicting transactions on
  different tables.
  4. The real fix was what we already did: raise ducklake_max_retry_count
  to 200. The pathological case is compaction (large commit, many concurrent
  millpond writes) — the right targeted fix is higher retries for compaction,
  not changing the protocol globally.

  So the DuckLake team got this right. The problem wasn't a design flaw — it was
   an under-tuned default retry count for high-concurrency production workloads.
   We already fixed it.

✻ Baked for 41s

❯ What kind of index can we do to actually improve this?                        

⏺ The fundamental problem is that DuckLake's postgres extension pulls the entire
   candidate set over the wire into DuckDB memory before doing anything with it.
   The indexes help postgres find and sort the rows, but don't reduce the volume
   transferred.

  The only index that would actually reduce the wire transfer is a partial index
   that physically limits the rows returned — which we already have (WHERE 
  end_snapshot IS NULL). That cuts it from 13M rows to 607k, which is already
  the minimum for person_distinct_id.

  The real lever isn't indexing — it's reducing the number of columns in the
  COPY. Looking at GetFilesForCompaction, it selects a wide row: path,
  file_size_bytes, begin_snapshot, end_snapshot, row_id_start, partition info,
  delete file info, column stats. That's potentially 20+ columns × 607k rows =
  significant wire volume even with fast indexes.

  A covering index that includes all the selected columns would let postgres
  serve the query entirely from the index (index-only scan), avoiding heap
  fetches and potentially streaming faster. But the path column (a long varchar)
   alone makes that impractical — it can't be included in a btree index
  efficiently.

  The honest answer: for person_distinct_id with 607k small files, the
  enumeration is going to take several minutes regardless of indexes because
  607k rows of wide data have to cross the postgres wire into DuckDB. The only
  structural fix is reducing the number of candidate files — i.e., get the
  compaction done so next time there are only hundreds of files, not hundreds of
   thousands.

  After this run clears the backlog, steady-state enumeration will be fast
  because the candidate set will be tiny.

✻ Cogitated for 19s

❯ How likely is it to finish?                                                   

⏺ The first 60 seconds showed 65 MB/s network (active S3 reads), then it dropped
   to near-zero for 25+ minutes. That means it did one fast batch then got stuck
   in a long enumeration. With 1800 groups requested and the candidate set
  re-enumerated from scratch on each internal loop iteration, and each
  enumeration taking ~25 minutes, the math is grim:

  1800 groups × 25 min enumeration overhead each = 750 hours if the pattern
  holds.

  It will eventually finish but not in any reasonable timeframe. The internal
  looping behavior (re-enumerating all 607k candidates for every batch) is the
  killer. Kill it.

  The working approach: instead of one compact-probe job that loops internally,
  run many short jobs from the cron — each job enumerates once, merges one
  batch, commits, exits. The cron fires the next one. No re-enumeration within a
   single job.

  Switch back to compact --tier 1 (not compact-probe), set COMPACTION_MAX_FILES
  to something that completes in 5-10 minutes per run, and let the 15-minute
  cron grind through the backlog. The key insight from the source: compact calls
   ducklake_merge_adjacent_files once and exits — no internal loop.

❯ The ultimate goal is to delete all but the most recent snapshots so viaduck isn't trying to replay 600k of them and to get rid of our "small files problem."  Both in aim of getting viaduck running. 

⏺ 49 seconds for enumeration — much better than before. The heartbeat will tell
  us the merge rate once it fires.

  On the ultimate goal: the two objectives are linked:
  - Compact first — merge the 607k small files down to ~2,852 (the
  total_ideal_files from the fragmentation query). This reduces the number of
  data files viaduck has to scan during CDC reads.
  - Then expire snapshots — once compaction commits, the pre-merge files get
  scheduled for deletion. Expiring old snapshots lets cleanup actually delete
  the S3 objects and prune the 13.7M-snapshot catalog down to a manageable
  window.

  But viaduck's CDC performance isn't actually gated on snapshot count — it
  reads from ducklake_data_file filtered by snapshot range, which the new
  indexes now serve efficiently. The 600k "small files" viaduck is replaying are
   the data files per CDC batch, not snapshots. Compaction directly reduces
  that.

  So the priority order is right: finish compaction, then expire. Let's watch
  this run and see if 150 groups × ~5MiB targets completes in under 10 minutes.

✻ Brewed for 2m 50s

❯ How much of the logic that depends on that five table join could be shifted   
  to postgres?                                                                  

⏺ Most of it. The enumeration query pulls five tables into DuckDB over the wire
  and does the join there — but postgres is perfectly capable of doing this join
   itself and streaming only the result rows. The issue is DuckLake uses COPY 
  ... TO STDOUT for every table separately, not a single joined query.

  If DuckLake issued one COPY of the joined result instead of five separate
  COPYs:

  COPY (
    SELECT data.data_file_id, data.path, data.file_size_bytes,
           data.begin_snapshot, data.row_id_start, data.partition_id,
           del.delete_file_id,
           pv.partition_value
    FROM ducklake_data_file data
    LEFT JOIN ducklake_delete_file del ON del.data_file_id = data.data_file_id
      AND del.end_snapshot IS NULL
    LEFT JOIN ducklake_file_partition_value pv ON pv.data_file_id =
  data.data_file_id
    WHERE data.table_id = ? AND data.end_snapshot IS NULL
      AND data.file_size_bytes < ?
    ORDER BY data.begin_snapshot, data.row_id_start, data.data_file_id
  ) TO STDOUT

  Postgres executes the join with its indexes, streams only the matching joined
  rows. Instead of COPYing 607k rows from ducklake_data_file + all the join
  partners separately, you get 607k result rows in one pass. Postgres's query
  planner with the new indexes would handle this efficiently — nested loop join
  on the data_file_id indexes for the left joins, index scan on the filter+sort
  index for the outer table.

  The estimated reduction: instead of transferring data_file_size × N_tables
  worth of rows, you transfer result_rows × result_width. For sparse joins (most
   files have no delete files, many have no partition values), the joined result
   is smaller than the sum of the parts.

  This is the right upstream fix to file — DuckLake's postgres extension should
  emit joined queries rather than separate per-table COPYs. It's a significant
  implementation change but the correctness model doesn't change at all.

  Worth adding to the upstream issues list, and it's a much more impactful fix
  than any index we can add on our side.

✻ Crunched for 18s

❯ Yeah.  What is the expected reduction in transfer volume under different      
  situations?                                                                   

⏺ Let me characterize the five tables and their join cardinalities:

  Tables joined in GetFilesForCompaction:

  ┌───────────────────────────┬─────────────────┬──────────────┬────────────┐
  │           Table           │ Rows for person │  Join type   │  Typical   │
  │                           │  _distinct_id   │              │  hit rate  │
  ├───────────────────────────┼─────────────────┼──────────────┼────────────┤
  │ ducklake_data_file        │ 607k            │ base         │ 100%       │
  ├───────────────────────────┼─────────────────┼──────────────┼────────────┤
  │                           │ ~0              │ LEFT JOIN on │ ~0% for ap │
  │ ducklake_delete_file      │ (append-only)   │              │ pend-only  │
  │                           │                 │ data_file_id │            │
  ├───────────────────────────┼─────────────────┼──────────────┼────────────┤
  │ ducklake_file_partition_v │                 │ LEFT JOIN on │ ~100% (par │
  │ alue                      │ varies          │              │ titioned   │
  │                           │                 │ data_file_id │ table)     │
  ├───────────────────────────┼─────────────────┼──────────────┼────────────┤
  │ ducklake_schema_versions  │ small           │ JOIN on begi │ ~100% but  │
  │                           │                 │ n_snapshot   │ tiny table │
  ├───────────────────────────┼─────────────────┼──────────────┼────────────┤
  │ ducklake_file_column_stat │ ~607k × 6 cols  │ LEFT JOIN on │            │
  │ s                         │ = 3.6M          │              │ 100%       │
  │                           │                 │ data_file_id │            │
  └───────────────────────────┴─────────────────┴──────────────┴────────────┘

  Current transfer (separate COPYs):
  - ducklake_data_file: 607k rows × ~200 bytes/row = ~120MB
  - ducklake_file_column_stats: 3.6M rows × ~150 bytes/row = ~540MB ← dominant
  - ducklake_file_partition_value: 607k rows × ~50 bytes = ~30MB
  - ducklake_delete_file: ~0 (append-only)
  - ducklake_schema_versions: tiny

  Total: ~700MB transferred

  Joined result (single COPY):
  - 607k result rows × ~300 bytes (base columns + 6 col stats min/max inlined) =
   ~180MB

  Reduction: ~75% less wire transfer for person_distinct_id's append-only,
  6-column, fully-partitioned case.

  The savings vary by situation:

  ┌───────────────────────────┬───────────┬─────────────────────────────────┐
  │         Scenario          │ Reduction │               Why               │
  ├───────────────────────────┼───────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │                           │           │ No delete files, 6 cols; column │
  │ Append-only, few columns  │ ~75%      │  stats dominate; join           │
  │ (person_distinct_id)      │           │ eliminates repeated             │
  │                           │           │ data_file_id overhead           │
  ├───────────────────────────┼───────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Append-only, wide schema  │           │ Column stats balloon to 52×     │
  │ (events, 52 cols)         │ ~85%      │ rows; join collapses them into  │
  │                           │           │ one result row per file         │
  ├───────────────────────────┼───────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │                           │           │ Delete file join adds rows but  │
  │ Table with deletes (CDC   │ ~60%      │ postgres filters them more      │
  │ mode)                     │           │ efficiently than DuckDB         │
  │                           │           │ scanning the full table         │
  ├───────────────────────────┼───────────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │                           │           │ No partition value rows;        │
  │ Unpartitioned table       │ ~70%      │ savings come entirely from      │
  │                           │           │ column stats collapse           │
  └───────────────────────────┴───────────┴─────────────────────────────────┘

  The events table (52 columns × 13M files = 676M rows in
  ducklake_file_column_stats) is the extreme case — that's why the 83GB table
  exists and why our index build took so long. A joined COPY would reduce events
   enumeration from transferring 676M + 13M + ... rows to ~13M result rows.
  That's the order-of-magnitude improvement that would make compaction fast at
  scale.

⏺ 225 seconds (~3.75 minutes), success. And the groups are tiny — 16-17 input
  files each. That's only ~2,400 input files total (150 groups × 16 files).

  On the join question: a materialized view won't help DuckLake directly since
  it issues its own COPYs and can't be redirected to query a view instead. But
  at the postgres layer, if we could intercept the separate COPYs and replace
  them with a single joined query — that's what a rewrite rule or view might
  enable, but postgres doesn't support rewriting COPY statements.

  The practical option we control: a denormalized materialized view that we
  refresh periodically, which DuckLake could query if the extension were
  modified to use it. But that requires source changes.

  What we CAN do right now without source changes: add a composite index on 
  ducklake_file_column_stats(data_file_id, table_id, column_id, min_value, 
  max_value) — a covering index that lets postgres serve the column stats join
  entirely from the index without hitting the 83GB heap. At the cost of index
  size, it would dramatically reduce the I/O for the column stats COPY.

  How many files were merged in the 225s run? Let me check the remaining count:

  before hitting the 5MiB target. With a 128MiB target each group would pack

✻ Crunched for 52s

❯ Right, I'm saying if we changed ducklake, it would make sense to just make    
  that five-way join a materialized view.                                       

⏺ Yes — and it's the right call architecturally. The materialized view would be
  refreshed on each commit (DuckLake already touches these tables
  transactionally, so a REFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW CONCURRENTLY post-commit is
  natural). The COPY then becomes:

  COPY (SELECT * FROM ducklake_compaction_candidates WHERE table_id = ? AND
  file_size_bytes < ?) TO STDOUT

  One table, pre-joined, pre-filtered by live files only, pre-sorted. Transfer
  volume drops 75-85%. The view definition is the same 5-way join we analyzed —
  postgres maintains it, DuckLake queries it.

  The change in DuckLake source is minimal: replace GetFilesForCompaction's
  multi-table query with a single view query, add CREATE MATERIALIZED VIEW to
  the catalog initialization SQL, add REFRESH to the commit path.

  The concurrent refresh is important — you don't want REFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW
   blocking reads during a busy commit. With CONCURRENTLY postgres maintains two
   versions and swaps atomically. The tradeoff is slightly stale data during the
   refresh window, but since compaction reads candidates once per call and
  commits immediately after, a few-millisecond staleness window is harmless.

  This is a clean, well-understood postgres pattern — it's how production OLTP
  systems handle expensive repeated aggregations all the time. File it as a
  concrete proposal with the view DDL written out.

  Now — shall we fix the 5MiB target before launching the next run?

❯ Should we just fix millpond's compactor to be useful now that we know what we're doing? 

⏺ Yes. The tier system is fundamentally mismatched with what we need — fixed
  size buckets (5MiB/32MiB/128MiB) when the real goal is "one file per partition
   regardless of size." And the separate enumeration per tier means paying the
  catalog scan cost three times per cron run.

  What we actually want:

  1. compact-partition — one call per closed hour prefix, merges everything in
  that partition to one file. No size target, just "pack it all." Catalog-aware
  loop in Python.
  2. Single compact-all command — replaces compact-to-tier-1/2/3 sequential
  chain with one call that picks the right target per file based on current size
   and merges up to 128MiB targets. One enumeration, one transaction.
  3. Smarter max_compacted_files default — based on estimated run time rather
  than an arbitrary file count.

  This is a meaningful refactor but not a huge one — the core
  ducklake_merge_adjacent_files call stays, we're just changing the Python
  orchestration around it. Want to do it on a branch now?

❯ It really seems like almost all of the coordination work for compaction should be moved to RDS, not duckdb. 

⏺ 0.0.59 running, 128MiB targets confirmed in the log. Watching for the first
  heartbeat.

  On moving coordination to RDS — agreed completely. Right now DuckDB is:
  1. Pulling tables over the wire into memory to do the candidate JOIN
  2. Building the merge plan in DuckDB's optimizer
  3. Executing S3 reads/writes
  4. Writing results back to postgres

  Steps 1 and 4 should never have left postgres. The candidate enumeration is a
  pure SQL query — postgres can join 5 tables and return a result set far more
  efficiently than DuckDB can COPY each table and join them in memory. The
  catalog writes (snapshot INSERT, data file DELETE/INSERT) are already single
  SQL statements that run in postgres anyway.

  What belongs in postgres:
  - Candidate enumeration (the materialized view / joined COPY we discussed)
  - Snapshot ID allocation (sequence or advisory lock)
  - Metadata commit (already there)

  What belongs in DuckDB:
  - Actual parquet file reads from S3
  - Merge/sort of row data
  - Writing output parquet files to S3

  The current design has DuckDB doing the coordination work it's bad at (catalog
   joins, snapshot allocation) and postgres doing what it's good at (durability,
   ACID commits) — but only at the very end. The coordination should never leave
   postgres in the first place.

⏺ Excellent. 50 seconds for enumeration, ~1% merged at 60s, 66 MB/s reads, RSS
  only 1.1 GiB. The 128MiB target is packing ~335 files per group so ~1% of 150
  groups = ~1.5 groups done in 60 seconds — that's ~2 groups/minute. 150 groups
  total = ~75 minutes per run.

  ETA of 80 minutes is consistent. At 150 groups × ~335 files = ~50k files per
  run, clearing 605k remaining takes ~12 runs. With a 15-minute cron cadence and
   ~75 min runs that's serial — but we can run manually back-to-back. Or bump
  the cron to every 2 minutes since concurrencyPolicy: Forbid handles overlap.

  The key number to watch: how many files the result rows show when it commits.
  If each group packs 300+ files we're in good shape.

⏺ ~1% for 7 minutes, ETA climbing instead of falling, RSS growing at ~0.5
  GiB/min. This is the old pattern — DuckDB is buffering all the merged data in
  memory before writing. The 128MiB target means each group reads ~335 × 390KB =
   ~130MB of S3 data before writing the output. With 150 groups all executing
  sequentially, the RSS accumulation is the merged output buffers for completed
  groups not yet flushed.

  The net at 10-11 MB/s symmetric means it's in the read+write phase (both up
  and down active), but slower than the first minute's 66 MB/s read burst. That
  66 MB/s burst was probably the first group being read; subsequent groups are
  slower because the merged data is being held in DuckDB's buffer while it
  processes the next group.

  The ETA growing (80m → 148m → 222m → 296m...) is because ~1% in DuckDB's
  denominator is the full 605k candidate count, and it's not advancing —
  consistent with a single first group still being processed or the % counter
  not updating until a checkpoint.

  It's making progress (RSS growing = data being merged), just slowly. At ~0.5
  GiB/min RSS growth and a 1.1 GiB/group expected working set, we're seeing
  roughly one group every 2 minutes — matching the ~75 minute estimate for 150
  groups.

  Let it run. The result rows when it commits will tell us exactly how many
  files each group packed.

      13 +                                                               
      14 +The current DuckLake compaction architecture has the wrong divi
         +sion of labor:                                                 
      15 +                                                               
      16 +**Currently in DuckDB (should be in postgres):**               
      17 +- Candidate enumeration: COPYs 5 tables separately over the wir
         +e, joins them in DuckDB memory (~700MB transfer for person_dist
         +inct_id)                                                       
      18 +- Snapshot ID allocation: MAX+1 with retry-on-collision instead
         + of a postgres SEQUENCE or advisory lock                       
      19 +- Group planning: builds UNION ALL of 150 merge plans in DuckDB
         +'s optimizer                                                   
      20 +                                                               
      21 +**Currently in postgres (correct):**                           
      22 +- Metadata commit: snapshot INSERT, data file DELETE/INSERT — a
         +lready atomic SQL                                              
      23 +

      25 +- Parquet file reads from S3

      27 +- Writing output parquet files to S3                           
      28 +                                                               
      29 +**Proposed architecture:**                                     
      30 +1. Postgres function/procedure enumerates candidates, groups th
         +em by partition, returns groups as a result set (replaces `GetF
         +ilesForCompaction` multi-COPY)                                 
      31 +2. Python/DuckDB iterates the result set, executes one S3 merge
         + per group

         +le metadata swap) — already fast

⏺ 21 minutes in, still ~1%, ETA climbing to 26 hours, RSS at 12 GiB. The same
  unbounded accumulation pattern — DuckDB is buffering all 150 groups worth of
  merged data in memory before committing, not flushing between groups. At ~0.5
  GiB/min we'll hit the pod memory limit (100 GiB) in ~3 more hours and get OOM
  killed.

  The max_compacted_files=150 at 128MiB target means 150 × 128MB = ~19 GB of
  output data all held in RAM before the single catalog commit. That's the
  fundamental problem — compact executes all 150 groups as one UNION ALL plan
  and accumulates everything.

  The only way to bound memory with the current DuckLake design is fewer groups
  per run. At 10 GiB RSS with 150 groups running 21 minutes, we need to cut to
  maybe 20 groups to keep under 2 GiB and finish in a few minutes. Kill it and
  drop to 20?

❯ What happens if we remove the table filter (e.g. run on all tables)? 

⏺ Running. 47s enumeration again, consistent.

  On removing the table filter: compact --tier 1 without --table runs
  catalog-wide, hitting all tables. With the current candidate counts:

  - person_distinct_id: 591k files
  - groups: 6,331 files
  - heatmap_events: 4 files
  - person: 6 files
  - events: 1 file

  The enumeration would scan all 598k candidates across all tables in one pass,
  which is essentially the same cost as today. The merge groups would include
  groups table files interleaved with person_distinct_id groups in catalog order
   — they'd all be processed in the same UNION ALL plan.

  The risk: groups has 6k sub-1MiB files which would add ~20-30 groups to each
  run, slowing it down slightly but also making progress on that table
  simultaneously. The benefit is one cron job handles everything rather than
  needing to switch --table arguments between tables.

  For now person_distinct_id is so dominant (591k vs 6k) that catalog-wide and
  table-specific are nearly identical. Once person_distinct_id is cleared,
  switching to catalog-wide for steady-state makes sense.

  Let the 80-group run finish first — it'll tell us whether the linear scaling
  holds or we start hitting memory pressure.

✻ Worked for 1m 52s

❯ What happens if we expire snapshots now?                                      

⏺ Two separate effects:

  For storage: expiring snapshots marks pre-compaction files for deletion —
  ducklake_files_scheduled_for_deletion gets populated, then
  ducklake_cleanup_old_files actually removes the S3 objects. This would free up
   the ~227GB of original small files we've already merged. But the
  pre-compaction files are only eligible for deletion once no live snapshot
  references them. We've been running compaction for ~2 hours, so files merged
  in the first runs (from ~2 hours ago) can be expired if we set the horizon to
  "snapshots older than 2 hours." Files merged in recent runs need to wait
  longer.

  For viaduck: this is the critical question. Viaduck's last_snapshot_id for
  team-2 is currently tracking near the live snapshot head (13.7M+ range).
  Expiring anything below that cursor is safe. But if we expire snapshots
  aggressively and viaduck is ever down for longer than the retention window, it
   would need to re-seed.

  The real risk right now: the compaction itself is creating new snapshots
  rapidly (~1 per run × many runs). If we expire "older than N hours" during
  active compaction, we could accidentally expire snapshots that are referenced
  by currently-running compaction transactions — that would corrupt the catalog.

  Recommendation: finish the compaction backlog first, then expire. Once
  person_distinct_id is clean, set a retention window of e.g. 7 days and expire
  everything older. That's conservative enough to be safe and would still
  recover the ~227GB immediately.

  How's the 80-group run doing?

  Perfectly linear — throughput doesn't improve with more groups, duration and
  memory both scale 1:1. The bottleneck is purely sequential S3 execution
  (ParallelSink=false), not memory or enumeration.

  This means the optimal group count is whatever keeps runs short enough to
  commit reliably and stay within memory. 40 groups at ~8 minutes and ~4 GiB
  seems like the sweet spot — two runs per 15-minute cron tick if we drop the
  cron to every 8 minutes.

✻ Cooked for 15s

❯ That's a general cron.                                                        

⏺ Right — the cron fires the job, the job runs for ~8 minutes,
  concurrencyPolicy: Forbid blocks the next tick if the job is still running. So
   at */8 * * * * you'd get at most one running job at a time, effectively
  back-to-back runs with a small gap. That's the maximum throughput from the
  cron.

  At 40 groups / ~960 files per minute: 591k remaining ÷ 960 = ~616 minutes ÷ 8
  min/run = ~77 runs to clear the backlog. At continuous back-to-back runs
  that's ~10 hours.

  Let this 80-group run finish to confirm the linear scaling holds, then set
  back to 40 groups and */8 * * * *.

✻ Sautéed for 12s

❯ Can we have parallel per-table compactions?                                   

⏺ Yes — the concurrencyPolicy: Forbid only blocks a second instance of the same
  CronJob. Nothing stops us from running separate Jobs for each table
  simultaneously.

  The constraint is the shared megaduck RDS — concurrent compaction runs on
  different tables will compete for the same postgres connections and the
  snapshot ID sequence. With our ducklake_max_retry_count=200 that should be
  fine for the snapshot contention. The bigger question is whether concurrent
  enumeration queries on ducklake_data_file (one per table) saturate RDS I/O.

  The practical approach: keep the existing CronJob for person_distinct_id, and
  create a second one for groups (6,331 files — clears in one or two runs).
  They'd run simultaneously, both hitting the same RDS. At the current
  enumeration rate (~48s per table) and ~10 MB/s network per run, two concurrent
   runs would be ~20 MB/s — well within RDS capacity.

  For steady-state, catalog-wide compaction (no --table filter) handles
  everything in one job with no coordination needed. The parallel approach only
  makes sense during backlog clearance when one table dominates.

  Want a second CronJob for groups while person_distinct_id grinds?

❯ How is the millpond person_distinct_id configured? It's apparently generating 
   too many small files.                                                        

⏺ Bash(grep -rn "person_distinct_id\|person-distinct"
      /Users/jakob/src/millpond/millpond/ --include="*.py" --include="*.yaml"

  ⎿  (No output)

  Searched for 5 patterns, listed 1 directory (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Configured the same as events: 256MB flush size, 1 minute interval. The
  problem isn't the flush config — it's that person_distinct_id has low volume.
  Events generates enough data to hit the 256MB size trigger frequently,
  producing large files. person_distinct_id is smaller volume so it almost
  always hits the 1-minute interval trigger before accumulating 256MB, producing
   sub-1MB files every minute.

  You'd need either a longer flush interval (e.g. 30 minutes) or a lower size
  threshold calibrated to the actual person_distinct_id write rate. What's the
  typical throughput on that topic — do we know events/second for
  person_distinct_id?

✻ Brewed for 1m 53s

❯ How many millpond workers are assigned to it?                                 

  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ 8 workers × 1-minute flush interval = 8 files per minute into
  person_distinct_id at steady state (one file per worker per flush). At 390KB
  average per file that's only 3MB/min of actual data — a very low-volume table
  spread across 8 workers.

  The fix is straightforward: either reduce worker count or increase the flush
  interval. 8 workers flushing every minute when the data rate doesn't justify
  it is the root cause of the 600k small files. A 30-minute flush interval would
   reduce file generation by 30×, producing ~16 files/minute × 30min = ~480
  files/hour instead of 8 × 60 = 480 files/hour... same count actually, just
  larger files.

  The real fix is fewer workers. person_distinct_id at 390KB/worker/min doesn't
  need 8 workers — that's a very low throughput topic. 2 workers with a longer
  flush interval would produce 2 files/min at larger size. Or 1 worker with
  256MB flush would produce files only when it accumulates enough data, which at
   low volume means hourly rather than per-minute.

  What's the Kafka partition count for clickhouse_person_distinct_id? Worker
  count should match partition count.

✻ Cooked for 25s

❯ Worker count shouldn't match the partition count.  That's the whole point.    
  Worker count should be a function of data production rate to match the target 
   file size.  (Which is a fancy way of saying we have too many partitions for  
  our particular use case on that topic, but there may be another job somewhere 
   else that needs the relatively slow production rate to keep up).             

⏺ Right — the consumer group can have more consumers than produces meaningful
  parallelism for the file write side. The workers are consuming fast enough to
  keep up with Kafka, but each worker independently flushes on the 1-minute
  interval regardless of how little data it's accumulated.

  The fix is in millpond's flush logic rather than worker count: size-based
  flushing should dominate, not time-based. At 390KB per worker per minute,
  you'd need to wait ~660 minutes for one worker to accumulate 256MB. The
  time-based flush of 1 minute is always winning.

  Options:
  1. Increase flush_intervalMs for person_distinct_id to something like 15-30
  minutes — files would still be sub-256MB but much larger (5-10MB instead of
  390KB), reducing fragmentation by 15-30×.
  2. Add a minimum file size threshold — don't flush on the time interval unless
   accumulated data exceeds some minimum (e.g. 10MB). This is the "smart flush"
  approach that avoids tiny files regardless of worker count.

  Option 2 is the right architectural answer and would benefit all low-volume
  tables universally. Option 1 is a config-level fix we can ship today.

  Want me to bump person_distinct_id flush interval in the charts values to 30
  minutes as an immediate fix?

✻ Cogitated for 16s

❯ Let's just go down to one worker.                                             

⏺ Makes sense — one worker still keeps up with the topic (it's low volume), and
  one file per minute × one worker is half the problem. Combined with a longer
  flush interval it would be better, but one worker is the minimal change.

✻ Cogitated for 1m 1s

❯ We're targeting 128MB, right?                                                 

⏺ Yes — tier 1 is now targeting 128MiB. But the fragmentation metric's
  denominator is also 128MiB, so the total_ideal_files of 2,854 reflects what a
  fully compacted lake would look like. We're at 546k actual vs 2,854 ideal —
  still ~191× fragmented on average.

  The ideal file count isn't changing much (2,852 → 2,854) even as files
  decrease, because the new compaction runs are producing 128MiB outputs that
  count as "ideal" — so total_ideal_files stays roughly stable while total_files
   drops. The fragmentation ratio will improve linearly as we reduce file count.

✻ Cogitated for 10s

❯ It's unlikely hourly partitions for this data can reach 128MB.                

⏺ Right — person_distinct_id at 390KB/worker × 8 workers × 60 minutes/hour =
  ~187MB/hour at peak, but that's the old config. With 1 worker at 390KB/min ×
  60 = ~23MB/hour. Many hours will have far less depending on actual traffic.

  So the ideal_files = GREATEST(1, CEIL(total_bytes / 128MiB)) is returning 1
  for most partitions regardless — a 23MB partition has an ideal of 1 file. The
  fragmentation ratio of 226 means those partitions currently have ~226 files
  averaging ~100KB each, and the target is 1 file of ~23MB. The metric is
  correct; the target is achievable.

  The target file size of 128MiB in the tier definition controls what DuckLake
  aims for when packing groups — it'll pack as many adjacent files as fit within
   128MiB. For a partition with only 23MB total, the whole partition merges into
   one ~23MB output file. That's fine and is exactly what we want. The output
  won't be 128MiB, but the fragmentation ratio goes from 226 → 1.

  So the 128MiB target is correct for person_distinct_id — it just means output
  files will usually be smaller than 128MiB, which is expected and acceptable
  for low-volume partitions.

❯ Does Ducklake let you delete specific snapshots?                              

⏺ Yes — ducklake_expire_snapshots accepts either older_than (timestamp) or
  versions (explicit list of snapshot IDs). So you can target specific
  snapshots:

  CALL ducklake_expire_snapshots('lake', versions => [13000000, 13000001,
  13000002]);

  Or expire everything before a specific snapshot:

  CALL ducklake_expire_snapshots('lake', older_than => '2026-06-01'::TIMESTAMP);

  What are you thinking — selectively expire some of the oldest snapshots to
  start recovering storage while compaction is still running?
