❯ What api does ducklake give for enumerating the snapshots?                    

  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Two paths:

  DuckDB SQL (fastest for bulk queries):
  SELECT * FROM lake.snapshots() ORDER BY snapshot_id;
  -- or scoped to a table:
  SELECT * FROM lake.snapshots('events') ORDER BY snapshot_id;

  pyducklake Python API:
  catalog.inspect().snapshots()   # returns pa.Table of all snapshots
  table.snapshots()               # list[Snapshot] for one table
  table.current_snapshot()        # just the latest

  For 13.85M snapshots, the SQL path via postgres directly is by far the fastest
   — SELECT count(*), min(snapshot_id), max(snapshot_id) FROM ducklake_snapshot
  as we've been doing. The snapshots() table function goes through DuckDB's
  catalog layer which adds overhead.

  What are you thinking about querying them for — finding a safe expiry horizon,
   or something else?

✻ Churned for 20s

❯ 188k/day is a lot.  May be useful to have an ability to prune down to one per 
   hour.                                                                        

⏺ Exactly the right shape — keeping one snapshot per hour gives you hourly
  time-travel granularity while reducing 13.85M snapshots to ~1,752 (73 days ×
  24 hours). That's a 99.99% reduction.

  The tricky part: ducklake_expire_snapshots deletes snapshots but needs at
  least one snapshot to remain per "epoch" so that file references don't break.
  You'd want to keep the last snapshot of each hour and expire everything else
  in that hour.

  The SQL to identify what to expire:

  -- Keep the max snapshot_id per hour, expire all others
  SELECT snapshot_id
  FROM ducklake_snapshot
  WHERE snapshot_id NOT IN (
      SELECT max(snapshot_id)
      FROM ducklake_snapshot
      GROUP BY date_trunc('hour', snapshot_time)
  )
  AND snapshot_time < now() - INTERVAL '1 hour'  -- leave current hour alone
  ORDER BY snapshot_id;

  Then pass the result as the versions list to ducklake_expire_snapshots. At
  13.85M snapshots that's a lot of IDs to pass in one call — you'd want to batch
   them.

  Worth adding a prune-snapshots command to millpond's maintenance tool. But
  before building it: the viaduck cursor safety check still applies — need to
  verify the viaduck cursor is above the highest snapshot being expired in each
  batch.

  Want to add this to NOTES and build it later, or tackle it now?

✻ Churned for 17s

       90 +megaduck has 13.85M snapshots (~188k/day from millpond + compa
          +ction). Pruning to one                                        
       91 +snapshot per hour would reduce to ~1,752 (73 days × 24h) — 99.
          +99% reduction — while                                         
       92 +preserving hourly time-travel granularity.                    
       93 +                                                              
       94 +Strategy: keep MAX(snapshot_id) per hour, expire everything el
          +se older than 1 hour.                                         
       95 +                                                              
       96 +```sql                                                        
       97 +SELECT snapshot_id FROM ducklake_snapshot                     
       98 +WHERE snapshot_id NOT IN (                                    
       99 +    SELECT max(snapshot_id)                                   
      100 +    FROM ducklake_snapshot                                    
      101 +    GROUP BY date_trunc('hour', snapshot_time)                
      102 +)                                                             
      103 +AND snapshot_time < now() - INTERVAL '1 hour'                 
      104 +ORDER BY snapshot_id;                                         
      105 +```                                                           
      106 +                                                              
      107 +Pass result to `ducklake_expire_snapshots('lake', versions => 
          +[...])` in batches.                                           
      108 +                                                              
      109 +**Safety check before each batch**: every viaduck cursor (`las
          +t_snapshot_id` in                                             
      110 +`viaduck.viaduck_state`) must be ≥ the highest snapshot_id bei
          +ng expired. With viaduck                                      
      111 +healthy and tracking near the live head, this passes trivially
          + for any batch of old                                         
      112 +snapshots.                                                    
      113 +                                                              
      114 +Candidate millpond command: `prune-snapshots [--dry-run] [--ke
          +ep-hours N]` (default N=1).                                   
      115 +Also worth adding a `--keep-days N` mode that keeps one per da
          +y for older history.                                          
      116 +                                                              
      117 +After pruning: run `ducklake_cleanup_old_files` to actually de
          +lete the now-unreferenced                                     
      118 +S3 files and reclaim storage.                                 
      119 +                                                              
      120  ## Follow-up: reactive per-table compaction job
      121  Rather than a single catalog-wide cron, a per-table reactive j
           ob that:
      122  1. Queries the fragmentation metric to find partitions above a
            threshold (e.g. ratio > 2)

⏺ Added.

✻ Churned for 23s

❯ New viaduck destinations just start from whatever the oldest snapshot is.     

⏺ That's the key constraint. If we expire snapshots below the oldest snapshot
  that any destination might need to seed from, a new destination addition would
   fail to find its starting point.

  But viaduck's seed is a full scan at current_snapshot_id — it doesn't replay
  from snapshot 0, it scans the live data at the current head snapshot and then
  starts CDC from there. So the seed itself doesn't depend on old snapshots at
  all.

  The CDC replay after seed starts from the seed snapshot (current head at seed
  time) and goes forward — never backward. So old snapshot expiry has no impact
  on new destination onboarding.

  The only scenario where old snapshots matter for new destinations is if you
  want to onboard a destination at a historical point-in-time rather than
  current state. That's not what viaduck does today.

  So the prune-snapshots safety check is simpler than I said: just verify
  viaduck cursors are above the expiry horizon, which they always will be for
  any expiry of "older than a few hours." No special handling needed for new
  destinations.

⏺ Zero — ducklake_files_scheduled_for_deletion is empty. That's because
  compaction's commit does two things: marks old files as end_snapshot IS NOT 
  NULL (dead in the catalog) and schedules them for deletion. But the scheduling
   only happens after ducklake_expire_snapshots runs — compaction itself just
  marks the snapshot range, the files only move to the deletion queue once no
  live snapshot references them anymore.

  Since we've never run ducklake_expire_snapshots, all the pre-compaction files
  are dead in the catalog (end_snapshot set) but still referenced by historical
  snapshots, so they can't be queued for deletion yet. The S3 objects are still
  there.

  Once we run snapshot expiry, the old compaction source files would immediately
   flood into ducklake_files_scheduled_for_deletion.

⏺ CleanupFiles() is only called on failed commits — it removes the newly written
   S3 output files when the transaction aborts (rollback cleanup). That's the
  opposite of what I thought. Now let me find where the source files get removed
   on successful compaction commit:

⏺ Bash(sed -n '4703,4800p'
      /Users/jakob/src/ducklake/src/storage/ducklake_metadata_manager.cpp)
  ⎿  string DuckLakeMetadataManager::WriteMergeAdjacent(const vector<DuckLa
     keCompactedFileInfo> &compactions,
                                                        const vector<DuckLa
     … +95 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Verified in source. The two paths are confirmed:

  MERGE_ADJACENT_TABLES (our compaction) — WriteMergeAdjacent:
  1. DELETE FROM ducklake_data_file WHERE data_file_id IN (...) — source catalog
   rows gone immediately
  2. INSERT INTO ducklake_files_scheduled_for_deletion — S3 paths queued for
  cleanup
  No end_snapshot involved. Source files deletable immediately after compaction
  commits.

  REWRITE_DELETES — WriteDeleteRewrites:
  1. UPDATE ducklake_data_file SET end_snapshot = N — marks source file as dead
  but keeps the row
  These require snapshot expiry before cleanup can happen.

  The discrepancy is real and by design — compaction doesn't need snapshot-based
   read consistency for the source files (they've been physically merged into a
  new file), so DuckLake just deletes them and queues for S3 cleanup
  immediately. Regular write supersession keeps the old row for time-travel
  reads until the snapshot is expired.

  Now the NOTES entry is verified by source code. The lifecycle discrepancy is
  undocumented upstream.

⏺ Bash(sed -n '130,165p'

  ⎿  void DuckLakeCleanupExecute(ClientContext &context, TableFunctionInput
      &data_p, DataChunk &output) {
        auto &data = data_p.bind_data->Cast<CleanupBindData>();
     … +32 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Confirmed. fs.RemoveFiles(paths) is the physical S3 delete — called via
  DuckDB's FileSystem abstraction which routes to the S3 client for S3 paths. It
   bulk-deletes all queued paths in one call, then
  RemoveFilesScheduledForCleanup removes them from
  ducklake_files_scheduled_for_deletion in the catalog.

  So the full lifecycle is:
  1. Compaction commit → catalog DELETE + INSERT into deletion queue (catalog
  only, no S3 yet)
  2. ducklake_cleanup_old_files → fs.RemoveFiles(paths) (actual S3 delete) +
  removes from queue

  The S3 delete only happens when you explicitly call cleanup. We haven't called
   it yet, so all 198k queued files are still physically on S3 waiting.

⏺ There are only two places that physically delete S3 files:

✻ Brewed for 48s

❯ That table is named wrong.  They're eligible for deletion, nothing is         
  scheduled.  If no ducklake extension ever calls cleanup, they'll never be     
  deleted.                                                                      

⏺ Exactly right. "Scheduled" implies something will automatically happen — but
  nothing does. It's really ducklake_files_eligible_for_deletion. The cleanup
  only happens when you explicitly call ducklake_cleanup_old_files. If you never
   call it, those 208k files sit on S3 forever, taking up space, silently
  accumulating.

  It's another item for the upstream issue list — the name is misleading and the
   lack of automatic cleanup means storage reclamation requires operator
  intervention with no obvious signal that it's needed. A reasonable design
  would have the cleanup run automatically after compaction, or at least emit a
  metric for "eligible files awaiting cleanup."

  Want to add it to NOTES?

✻ Cogitated for 12s

❯ Nah.  But let's go ahead and call cleanup.  Can it be batched?                

  Searched for 2 patterns (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ No batching parameter — it reads all eligible files and deletes them in one
  fs.RemoveFiles() call. The only parameters are older_than, cleanup_all, and
  dry_run.

  S3's DeleteObjects API handles up to 1,000 objects per request, and DuckDB's
  RemoveFiles almost certainly batches internally. So 208k files = ~208 S3
  DeleteObjects API calls — fast, maybe a few seconds.

  Let's add it to the millpond CronJob as a step after compaction. Or run it
  manually right now via a one-off job. What do you prefer?

✻ Cogitated for 22s

❯ I was hoping for this to run concurrently with the cron job...                

⏺ The concurrencyPolicy: Forbid on the CronJob blocks any new job from the same
  CronJob while one is running — but viaduck-cleanup-1 was created from the
  CronJob template, so the controller sees it as an active job and blocks the
  next compaction tick.

  Two options going forward:
  1. Separate CronJob for cleanup — different resource, no concurrency conflict
  with compaction.
  2. Run cleanup as a step inside the compaction job — add it to the just recipe
   so each compaction run does cleanup after merging.

  Option 2 is cleaner long-term. The justfile already has compact-all-tiers — we
   could add a compact-and-clean recipe that runs compact-to-tier-1 then
  cleanup-all. No second CronJob needed.

  For now let cleanup finish, then restore the compaction args and the cron will
   resume. Want to build the combined recipe for next time?

❯ Why not just add the cleanup-all call to the arguments in the cron?           

⏺ Much simpler. Just patch the CronJob args directly:

  compact-to-tier-1 person_distinct_id && cleanup-all

  But just doesn't support && between recipes in args — it takes recipe names as
   separate positional args and runs them sequentially. So:

  ["compact-to-tier-1", "person_distinct_id", "cleanup-all"]

  Wait — just compact-to-tier-1 person_distinct_id passes person_distinct_id as
  the table arg to the recipe. Adding cleanup-all as a third arg would be
  interpreted as another arg to compact-to-tier-1, not a separate recipe.

  So the justfile recipe is still the right approach — or we change the command
  to python and chain two python calls. The justfile recipe is cleaner. Let me
  commit it:

       "compact-to-tier-1-and-clean")

✻ Cooked for 43s

❯ That's a good thing for the console we'll write: a way to view snapshots and  
  their ages.  Millpond definitely shouldn't do that.                           

⏺ Agreed — snapshot lifecycle management (view, expire, cleanup) is exactly the
  kind of operational visibility that belongs in a dedicated console, not tucked
   inside a maintenance script. The console would show:

  - Snapshot count and age distribution (hourly/daily buckets)
  - Which snapshots are referenced by live files vs orphaned
  - The viaduck cursor positions overlaid on the snapshot timeline (so you can
  see the safe expiry horizon at a glance)
  - Files eligible for cleanup vs still referenced

  Millpond's maintenance tool is the right execution vehicle (the expire and
  cleanup-all commands are already there), but the decision of when and how much
   to expire should be driven by visibility in the console, not a config flag in
   a CronJob.

  Shall I add that to NOTES and then go ahead with the expiry?

✻ Cooked for 13s

✻ Crunched for 1m 54s
❯ When does ducklake_file_column_stats get pruned?                              

⏺ It follows the same lifecycle as ducklake_data_file — when
  ducklake_expire_snapshots marks snapshots as expired, the associated file
  column stats for dead files (those with end_snapshot set to an expired
  snapshot) become eligible for cleanup. Then ducklake_cleanup_old_files removes
   them from the table alongside the file entries.

  But — ducklake_file_column_stats rows are linked to data_file_id, not directly
   to snapshots. Looking at the WriteMergeAdjacent code we read earlier,
  compaction explicitly does:

  DELETE FROM ducklake_file_column_stats WHERE data_file_id IN (...)

  So compaction already prunes column stats immediately when it deletes the
  source file rows. The 83GB table should be shrinking with every compaction
  run. The 9.99M dead events files in ducklake_data_file (the end_snapshot IS 
  NOT NULL ones from millpond writes) still have their column stats rows — those
   get pruned when snapshot expiry + cleanup runs, which is what we're doing
  right now.

  So: compaction → immediate column stats prune; millpond write supersession →
  waits for expire + cleanup.

✻ Cogitated for 17s

❯ dbeaver doesn't seem to update its row counts, even on refresh...             

⏺ That's a DBeaver caching issue — it caches table statistics from
  pg_stat_user_tables.n_live_tup which postgres only updates via
  autovacuum/analyze, not on every query. After large deletes (like what expire
  is doing right now to 12.5M snapshot rows), the stats can be stale for minutes
   to hours.

  To get accurate counts in DBeaver: right-click the table → "Refresh" won't
  help. You need to either:

  1. Run SELECT count(*) FROM table_name; directly — always accurate
  2. Run ANALYZE table_name; first to update stats, then DBeaver's count will
  reflect it

  The ANALYZE on ducklake_snapshot after the expire finishes would be useful
  anyway — the planner will need fresh stats after deleting 12.5M rows to keep
  query plans optimal.

  How's the expire job doing?

✻ Worked for 12s

❯ Seems to be fine.  Not very chatty.                                           

⏺ The expire command in millpond is just:

  result = conn.execute(
      f"CALL ducklake_expire_snapshots('{ATTACH_NAME}', "
      f"older_than => now() - INTERVAL '{days} days', "
      f"dry_run => {str(dry_run).lower()})"
  ).fetchall()
  for row in result:
      log.info("expire: %s", row)

  One blocking call, logs the result rows when done. No heartbeat, no progress —
   just silence until it finishes. Given it's expiring 12.58M snapshots from a
  13.85M-row table, it's going to take a while. Postgres is doing the work
  internally.

  Worth adding a heartbeat to expire too at some point. For now, watch
  pg_stat_activity to see it working.

✻ Crunched for 15s

❯ This DB is gonna need a good vacuum.                                          

⏺ Absolutely. 12.5M snapshot rows expired + 9.99M data file rows + 520M column
  stats rows deleted = enormous dead tuple accumulation. Autovacuum will
  eventually get there but on a busy RDS instance with continuous millpond
  writes it'll be fighting for resources.

  After the expire job finishes, run:

  VACUUM ANALYZE public.ducklake_snapshot;
  VACUUM ANALYZE public.ducklake_data_file;
  VACUUM ANALYZE public.ducklake_file_column_stats;
  VACUUM ANALYZE public.ducklake_files_scheduled_for_deletion;

  The column stats one will take the longest given its size. VACUUM on RDS
  doesn't block reads or writes (it's non-exclusive), but it will consume I/O.
  Probably worth doing when traffic is lighter — or accepting that it'll take a
  while and run it anyway.

  Also worth bumping RDS's autovacuum_vacuum_cost_delay temporarily to 0 to let
  autovacuum run faster after the expire, then restore it. But that's a
  parameter group change.

✻ Sautéed for 15s

❯ Right now viaduck is OOMing on the events table; is this work likely to help  
  that?                                                                         

⏺ Yes, directly. Viaduck's seed scan OOM was caused by the
  arrow_large_buffer_size issue we fixed (PR #26) — the 2GiB string offset
  limit. That's already fixed in the image.

  But the seed scan also has to read all the events data files to reconstruct
  current state for team-2. Right now the events table has 12.66M data files
  (mostly 64-128MiB, already well-compacted). The expire + cleanup we're running
   now doesn't reduce that count — those are live files.

  What would help the seed scan:
  - Snapshot expiry (in progress) — reduces the catalog metadata volume DuckDB
  has to process during the enumeration phase, not the actual file reads
  - The arrow_large_buffer_size fix — already shipped, this was the actual OOM
  cause
  - The seed scan progress + ETA — already shipped so at least we can see it's
  not stuck

  The seed scan timing is mostly a function of S3 data volume for team-2, which
  is ~1.1 PiB. The catalog work (snapshot enumeration etc.) is secondary. With
  the fix already in place, the next seed attempt should get past the 2GiB limit
   that was killing it.

  Should we bring viaduck back up after the expire finishes and see how the seed
   goes?

⏺ cdc_replay skips seeding entirely — the destination starts empty and viaduck
  begins delivering CDC from the current snapshot forward. For append-only
  events that's perfect: no historical backfill, just start from now.

  The config change is one line in the viaduck chart values:

  routing:
    seedMode: cdc_replay

  This is exactly "start from latest" in Kafka terms. The destination table
  starts empty and fills forward from the current snapshot. For events where
  historical data doesn't matter, this is the right call. Want me to update the
  chart values?

✻ Brewed for 30s

❯ No, not partitioned.  Sorted.  So the parquet file gets sorted correctly.     
  Partitioning is too heavy weight; we have 10k team_id possible values.        

⏺ Right — sorting by team_id within each parquet file lets DuckLake use column
  statistics (min/max per row group) to skip entire row groups that don't
  contain team_id=2. No extra files needed, just better locality within each
  file. At the row group level (~100k rows each), DuckLake's zone map pruning
  can skip most of the data with a single metadata read.

  millpond has the sortBy config option for this. Is it currently configured at
  all for events, and is team_id just missing from the sort key?

⏺ Already at 64 (the live patch to 128 didn't take earlier — confirmed now).
  Good.

  On batching expiry: ducklake_expire_snapshots accepts an explicit versions
  list, so yes — we can batch by snapshot ID range:

  -- Expire in batches of 500k snapshots
  CALL ducklake_expire_snapshots('lake', versions => (
      SELECT array_agg(snapshot_id)
      FROM ducklake_snapshot
      WHERE snapshot_time < now() - INTERVAL '7 days'
      ORDER BY snapshot_id
      LIMIT 500000
  ));

  The problem is the OOM isn't in the snapshot enumeration — it's in the
  downstream cascade: expiring snapshots triggers deletion of the referenced
  ducklake_data_file rows which cascades into ducklake_file_column_stats (520M
  rows). Even a small batch of snapshots could trigger a large column stats
  delete if those snapshots reference many files.

  The real fix is batching by file count affected, not snapshot count. Query
  which snapshots have no more than N dead files, expire those. But
  ducklake_expire_snapshots doesn't expose that level of control.

  Worth adding a expire-batched command to millpond that does the
  batch-by-snapshot-range approach and runs multiple small transactions. Add to
  NOTES?

✻ Worked for 39s

❯ meh... Lots of small batches should be reasonable.  Any way to patch in a new 
   cron job to run independently of the compaction job that just expires the    
  oldest 100 snapshots at a time?                                               

⏺ Yes — create a separate CronJob with its own nodepool tolerance, tiny memory
  footprint, running every few minutes. 100 snapshots at a time means tiny
  transactions, minimal cascade deletes, no OOM risk.

  The challenge: millpond's expire command takes --days N, not a snapshot count
  limit. We'd need to call ducklake_expire_snapshots directly with a versions
  list of 100 IDs. That requires either a new millpond command or overriding the
   command with a python one-liner.

  Python one-liner approach via the command override:

  import sys; sys.path.insert(0,'/app/tools')
  import ducklake_maintenance as dm
  conn = dm.connect()
  ids = [r[0] for r in conn.execute("SELECT snapshot_id FROM 
  __ducklake_metadata_lake.ducklake_snapshot WHERE snapshot_time < now() - 
  INTERVAL '7 days' ORDER BY snapshot_id LIMIT 100").fetchall()]
  if ids: conn.execute(f"CALL ducklake_expire_snapshots('lake', versions => 
  {ids})")
  print(f"expired {len(ids)} snapshots")

  Or — cleaner — add expire-batched --days 7 --batch-size 100 to millpond.
  That's a small PR.

  Which do you prefer — quick one-liner CronJob now, or a proper millpond
  command first?

  Let me take stock of what's happening:

  1. Compaction OOMing at 8Gi — COMPACTION_MEMORY_LIMIT=4GB + untracked c8
  overshoot exceeds 8Gi on a freshly provisioned node. The node the expire job
  was on may have had fragmented memory.

  into DuckDB memory.
  3. Root cause: everything runs on the compaction nodepool (4xlarge = 26Gi
  allocatable), and DuckDB's memory usage is poorly bounded for catalog
  operations.

⏺ That's DuckDB pulling in the catalog — the expire operation reads all the file
   metadata for the expired snapshots to figure out what to schedule for
  deletion. With 12.5M snapshots and their associated file references, that's a
  lot of CPU on the catalog read phase.

  The 100-snapshot batching should help once it gets past the initial catalog
  load — each subsequent batch should be lighter since the connection is already
   warm. But the first batch is always expensive because DuckDB has to load the
  full catalog state.

  Is it currently still running or did it OOM again?

✻ Baked for 14s

❯ Still running.  More work that can be moved directly into the postgres.       

⏺ Exactly. The expire operation is:

  1. DuckDB reads the full snapshot catalog into memory (expensive CPU)
  2. DuckDB identifies which files are no longer referenced
  3. DuckDB generates DELETE statements for those files
  4. Those DELETEs execute in postgres

  Steps 1-3 are pure catalog metadata work that postgres could do directly:

  -- Find and delete dead data files in one postgres transaction
  DELETE FROM ducklake_data_file
  WHERE end_snapshot IN (
      SELECT snapshot_id FROM ducklake_snapshot
      WHERE snapshot_time < now() - INTERVAL '7 days'
  )
  AND end_snapshot NOT IN (
      SELECT snapshot_id FROM ducklake_snapshot
      WHERE snapshot_time >= now() - INTERVAL '7 days'
  );

  Postgres already has all the information — it doesn't need DuckDB to mediate.
  The file column stats, scheduled deletions, and snapshot table updates are all
   foreign-key-style cascades that postgres could handle with CTEs or triggers.

  DuckDB's role here is purely as a query layer that re-reads data postgres
  already has. The same pattern as compaction — postgres is the right place for
  this coordination, DuckDB should just do the S3 work.

  Add to the "move coordination into RDS" NOTES entry?

⏺ 90 minutes on a 32Gi r-family node — the catalog load for 12.5M snapshots is
  genuinely that expensive. DuckDB is pulling the full snapshot and file
  metadata into memory before it can identify what to expire.

  The 100-snapshot batch limit doesn't help because ducklake_expire_snapshots
  loads the full catalog state regardless of how many snapshots you're expiring
  — the batch size only affects the DELETE, not the initial read.

  This is fundamentally broken for our scale. The postgres-native approach in
  the NOTES is the only thing that will actually work. ducklake_expire_snapshots
   was designed for small lakes.

✻ Baked for 1m 0s

❯ No, we need to be able to expire them now.                                    

⏺ The postgres-native approach then. Let me write it directly — no DuckDB, no
  millpond, just SQL executed against megaduck. The steps are:

  1. Mark files scheduled for deletion (files whose end_snapshot references
  only-expired snapshots)
  2. Delete the dead file catalog rows (data_file, column_stats,
  partition_value, delete_file)
  3. Delete the expired snapshot rows

  The tricky part is step 1 — we need to only touch files whose end_snapshot
  will have no remaining live references after expiry. Let me write a safe
  batched version:

  ! psql "postgres://megaduck:...@posthog-megaduck-..." -c "
  WITH expired AS (
      SELECT snapshot_id FROM ducklake_snapshot
      WHERE snapshot_time < now() - INTERVAL '7 days'
      ORDER BY snapshot_id
      LIMIT 10000
  ),
  dead_files AS (
      SELECT df.data_file_id, df.path, df.path_is_relative
      FROM ducklake_data_file df
      WHERE df.end_snapshot IN (SELECT snapshot_id FROM expired)
      AND NOT EXISTS (
          SELECT 1 FROM ducklake_snapshot s
          WHERE s.snapshot_id = df.end_snapshot
          AND s.snapshot_time >= now() - INTERVAL '7 days'
      )
  )
  INSERT INTO ducklake_files_scheduled_for_deletion (data_file_id, path,
  path_is_relative, schedule_start)
  SELECT data_file_id, path, path_is_relative, NOW() FROM dead_files
  ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING;
  "

  But before we write this — are we confident about the schema? We've verified
  ducklake_data_file has end_snapshot. What does DuckLake use to determine a
  file is safe to delete, beyond end_snapshot? I want to make sure we don't
  accidentally schedule live files for deletion.

⏺ Now the picture is clear. GetAllSnapshots loads all 12.5M matching snapshot

  The postgres-native solution bypasses this entirely. Here's what
  DeleteSnapshots is actually doing in SQL — we can replicate it directly:

  1. DELETE FROM ducklake_snapshot WHERE snapshot_id IN (...)
  2. DELETE FROM ducklake_snapshot_changes WHERE snapshot_id IN (...)
  3. Find files with end_snapshot IS NOT NULL where no remaining snapshot
  references them
  4. Schedule those files for deletion
  5. Delete dead file catalog rows

  We can write a postgres function or just run the SQL directly in batches by
  timestamp. Shall I write the direct SQL approach?

✻ Churned for 1m 13s

❯ So, there's no need for DuckDB/the ducklake extension to do anything at all.  

⏺ Exactly. Every step is a pure catalog operation:

  1. Delete ducklake_snapshot rows older than N days
  2. Delete corresponding ducklake_snapshot_changes rows
  3. Find data files whose end_snapshot is now gone (no longer referenced by any
   remaining snapshot)
  4. Insert those into ducklake_files_scheduled_for_deletion
  5. Delete those dead rows from ducklake_data_file, ducklake_file_column_stats,
   ducklake_file_partition_value, ducklake_delete_file

  All foreign-key-style cascades that postgres can do in a single transaction.
  No S3 involved, no DuckDB needed. The only thing DuckDB adds is overhead.

  The S3 cleanup (ducklake_cleanup_old_files) is the one place DuckDB is
  genuinely needed — it calls fs.RemoveFiles() on the S3 paths. But that's
  already separated into its own call, and we have millpond's cleanup-all for
  that.

  So the plan is: write a postgres function expire_old_snapshots(days int, 
  batch_size int) that does steps 1-5 directly, run it in batches small enough
  not to OOM postgres itself, then run millpond's cleanup-all to do the S3
  deletes. No DuckDB, no OOM, no 90-minute catalog reads.

  Want me to write that function now?

  range.

✻ Baked for 1m 7s

❯ Ducklake doesn't have a function to flush inlined data to disk?               

⏺ Let me check:

  Searched for 2 patterns (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ ducklake_flush_inlined_data exists. It flushes inlined postgres data to actual
   S3 parquet files. If we run this before expiry, it converts the inlined
  tables to regular data files, removing the ducklake_inlined_data_* tables from
   the picture entirely. Then the postgres-native expiry has no special cases to
   handle.

  CALL ducklake_flush_inlined_data('lake', 'events');
  CALL ducklake_flush_inlined_data('lake', 'person_distinct_id');

  This writes the inlined data to S3, creates proper ducklake_data_file entries,
   and marks the inlined tables for deletion. After that, the catalog is in a
  clean state where expiry is purely about snapshot rows and data file rows — no
   special inlined table handling needed.

  Worth running this before the postgres-native expiry. It's also genuinely
  useful for query performance — inlined data requires a separate read path vs
  the normal S3 parquet path.

      180 +WITH expired AS (

      182 +    WHERE snapshot_time < now() - INTERVAL '7 days'           
      183 +    ORDER BY snapshot_id LIMIT 10000  -- tune batch size

      187 +    FROM ducklake_data_file df                                
      188 +    WHERE df.end_snapshot IN (SELECT snapshot_id FROM expired)
      189 +    AND NOT EXISTS (                                          
      190 +        SELECT 1 FROM ducklake_snapshot s                     
      191 +        WHERE s.snapshot_id >= df.begin_snapshot              
      192 +        AND (df.end_snapshot IS NULL OR s.snapshot_id < df.end
          +_snapshot)

          +d, path, path_is_relative, schedule_start)

          +ata_files ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING;

          +ata_file_id FROM dead_data_files);

          +LECT snapshot_id FROM expired);

          +pshot_id FROM expired);

✻ Churned for 15s

❯ Can all of the snapshot expiration be done (per batch) in a transaction?      

⏺ Yes — and it should be. Each batch (steps 1-5) in a single postgres
  transaction gives you atomicity: either all the deletes happen (snapshots
  gone, files scheduled, catalog cleaned) or none do. No partial state.

  The only constraint is transaction size — 10k snapshots worth of cascade
  deletes (especially into ducklake_file_column_stats with its 520M rows) could
  be a large transaction. But smaller batches (1k-5k snapshots) keep each
  transaction tight and won't stress RDS.

  The millpond prune-snapshots command would look like:

  while True:
      with conn.transaction():  # or conn.execute("BEGIN") ... COMMIT
          ids = fetch_next_batch(conn, days=7, limit=1000)
          if not ids:
              break
          delete_file_column_stats(conn, ids)
          delete_data_files(conn, ids)
          schedule_for_deletion(conn, ids)
          delete_snapshots(conn, ids)
      print(f"expired batch of {len(ids)}")

  Each iteration is one atomic transaction, fully committed before the next
  batch starts. Failure mid-batch rolls back cleanly. No DuckDB, no OOM, no
  90-minute catalog reads.

✻ Baked for 17s

❯ We can't build one giant sql statement?                                       

⏺ We can — postgres CTEs with data-modifying statements handle this in one shot:

  WITH expired AS (
      DELETE FROM ducklake_snapshot
      WHERE snapshot_id IN (
          SELECT snapshot_id FROM ducklake_snapshot
          WHERE snapshot_time < now() - INTERVAL '7 days'
          ORDER BY snapshot_id
          LIMIT 1000
      )
      RETURNING snapshot_id
  ),
  dead_files AS (
      SELECT df.data_file_id, df.path, df.path_is_relative
      FROM ducklake_data_file df
      WHERE df.end_snapshot IN (SELECT snapshot_id FROM expired)
      AND NOT EXISTS (
          SELECT 1 FROM ducklake_snapshot s
          WHERE s.snapshot_id >= df.begin_snapshot
          AND (df.end_snapshot IS NULL OR s.snapshot_id < df.end_snapshot)
      )
  ),
  schedule AS (
      INSERT INTO ducklake_files_scheduled_for_deletion (data_file_id, path,
  path_is_relative, schedule_start)
      SELECT data_file_id, path, path_is_relative, NOW() FROM dead_files
      ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING
      RETURNING data_file_id
  ),
  del_col_stats AS (
      DELETE FROM ducklake_file_column_stats WHERE data_file_id IN (SELECT
  data_file_id FROM dead_files)
  ),
  del_partition AS (
      DELETE FROM ducklake_file_partition_value WHERE data_file_id IN (SELECT
  data_file_id FROM dead_files)
  ),
  del_variant AS (
      DELETE FROM ducklake_file_variant_stats WHERE data_file_id IN (SELECT
  data_file_id FROM dead_files)
  ),
  del_data_files AS (
      DELETE FROM ducklake_data_file WHERE data_file_id IN (SELECT data_file_id
  FROM dead_files)
  )
  DELETE FROM ducklake_snapshot_changes WHERE snapshot_id IN (SELECT snapshot_id
   FROM expired);

  One statement, one transaction, one round-trip. Run it in a loop until it
  returns 0 rows deleted. Each iteration expires 1000 snapshots and cascades all
   the catalog cleanup atomically.

✻ Brewed for 14s

❯ Let's get a lead QE and SQL architect to review.                              

⏺ Agent(QE and SQL architect review of postgres-native snapshot expiry SQL)
  ⎿  Done (0 tool uses · 12.6k tokens · 21s)
  (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Two blocking issues:

  HIGH #1 — CTE snapshot visibility bug: dead_files sees the pre-delete state of
   ducklake_snapshot (all CTEs in a statement share the same command snapshot),
  so the NOT EXISTS check still finds the expired snapshots and incorrectly
  marks files as still live — nothing gets deleted. Fix: exclude expired IDs
  explicitly:

  AND NOT EXISTS (
      SELECT 1 FROM ducklake_snapshot s
      WHERE s.snapshot_id NOT IN (SELECT snapshot_id FROM expired)
      AND s.snapshot_id >= df.begin_snapshot
      AND (df.end_snapshot IS NULL OR s.snapshot_id < df.end_snapshot)
  )

  HIGH #2 — Missing ducklake_delete_file: deleting from ducklake_data_file while
   leaving ducklake_delete_file rows pointing at those data_file_id values risks
   either a constraint violation or permanent orphans.

  Also: loop termination should check the expired CTE rowcount, not
  ducklake_snapshot_changes. And the ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING needs an explicit
  conflict target.

  Fix these before running. Want me to write the corrected version?

✻ Sautéed for 2m 5s

❯ That makes me nervous to have missed anything so obvious.                     

⏺ Fair — and it's a good sign that the review caught it cleanly. The CTE
  snapshot visibility behavior is one of those postgres subtleties that's easy
  to miss even for experienced SQL writers. The intuition that "later CTEs see
  earlier CTEs' effects" is wrong in postgres; they all see the same
  pre-statement snapshot. It's well-documented but non-obvious.

  The ducklake_delete_file miss is more embarrassing — we looked at the
  DeleteSnapshots source and it explicitly handles delete files, but didn't
  carry that through to the SQL. That's a documentation-to-implementation gap.

  Both would have been caught immediately on a dry run (nothing would get
  deleted in case 1; case 2 would either error or silently accumulate orphans).
  But "would have been caught in testing" is cold comfort for production catalog
   surgery on 13.85M rows.

  The review process worked as intended. Let me write the corrected version when
   you're ready, and we can get another pass on it before running anything.

❯ OK                                                                            

⏺ Bash(psql "postgres://megaduck:$(kubectl --context mw-prod-us -n viaduck get
      secret viaduck-postgres-uris -o jsonpath='{.data.source_password}'
      2>/dev/null | base64…)
  ⎿               Table "public.ducklake_files_scheduled_for_deletion"
           Column      |           Type           | Collation | Nullable |
     Default
     … +6 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ No constraints, no indexes at all. The ON CONFLICT (data_file_id) DO NOTHING
  will fail. The fix is simple — drop both ON CONFLICT clauses and instead guard
   with a WHERE NOT EXISTS:

  -- Step 4: schedule dead data files for physical deletion
  schedule_data_files AS (
      INSERT INTO ducklake_files_scheduled_for_deletion
                  (data_file_id, path, path_is_relative, schedule_start)
      SELECT data_file_id, path, path_is_relative, now()
      FROM   dead_data_files
      WHERE  NOT EXISTS (
          SELECT 1 FROM ducklake_files_scheduled_for_deletion
          WHERE data_file_id = dead_data_files.data_file_id
      )
  ),

  -- Step 5: schedule dead delete files for physical deletion
  schedule_delete_files AS (
      INSERT INTO ducklake_files_scheduled_for_deletion
                  (data_file_id, path, path_is_relative, schedule_start)
      SELECT delete_file_id, path, path_is_relative, now()
      FROM   dead_delete_files
      WHERE  NOT EXISTS (
          SELECT 1 FROM ducklake_files_scheduled_for_deletion
          WHERE data_file_id = dead_delete_files.delete_file_id
      )
  ),

  Also — FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED on ducklake_snapshot in the expired CTE won't
  work because it's a subquery, not a direct table reference. Need to
  restructure that or just omit it (concurrent inserts from millpond are fine at
   READ COMMITTED — we just retry on serialization errors).

  Also also - the FOR UPDATE in a CTE SELECT that's then used by a DELETE isn't
  valid postgres syntax anyway. Drop it.

  This is getting to the point where we should get another QE pass on the
  corrected version before running it. Agree?

  ('main', 'person_distinct_id', 343, 1) means 343 input files were merged into
  1 output file in that group.

  ~8 minutes. The node has 24Gi and we've seen RSS peak around 4-6 GiB at 40
  groups — plenty of headroom. We could safely go to 80 groups (~16 minutes per
  run) and still be well within memory.

  The risk at 80 groups is the commit — a larger batch means a bigger postgres
  transaction, more WAL, and slightly higher contention with millpond. But we've
   proven 80 groups works (the earlier test ran fine, just had high RSS because
  we had the wrong memory limit at the time; with the current config it should

✻ Churned for 40s

❯ Just checking again, because I'm tired - even expiring a single specified     
  snapshot requires Ducklake to pull all the information down?                  

⏺ Yes — that's exactly what we verified in the source.
  DuckLakeExpireSnapshotsBind calls metadata_manager.GetAllSnapshots(filter)
  which runs:

  SELECT snapshot_id, snapshot_time, schema_version, changes_made, author,
  commit_message, commit_extra_info
  FROM ducklake_snapshot
  LEFT JOIN ducklake_snapshot_changes USING (snapshot_id)
  WHERE <filter>
  ORDER BY snapshot_id

  And loads every matching row into a vector<DuckLakeSnapshotInfo> in memory —
  all at bind time, before any deletes happen. Even if you pass versions => [42]
   (one specific snapshot), it still runs this query against the full catalog to
   find that one row and load it into memory.

  The memory usage isn't from loading the target snapshots — it's from the
  cascade: finding all dead files referenced by those snapshots requires
  scanning ducklake_data_file (13M rows) to find files whose end_snapshot
  matches. That full scan is what blows up on our catalog.

  So yes — even expiring one snapshot triggers the full catalog load. It's not
  batch-size-sensitive at all.

✻ Brewed for 16s

❯ That's amazing.                                                               

⏺ It really is. The batch size parameter we added (versions => [42]) controls
  how many snapshots get deleted, but the catalog scan that determines what's
  dead happens unconditionally for the entire table. You pay the full 13M-row
  scan cost whether you're expiring 1 snapshot or 12 million.

  It's as if DELETE FROM table WHERE id = 42 first loaded every row into
  application memory to find which one to delete.

  Good night — compaction will grind overnight. Check the fragmentation metric
  in the morning.
